The results are most directly applicable to games with 5-player groups, 1000-player populations, 600 rounds (generations), no chat, continuous contributions, and known MPCR and punishment cost parameters as specified. The study does not vary most game design dimensions, so its predictive value is strongest for the specific parameter regime tested.

- source: Hintze, A., Staudacher, J., Gelhar, K., Pothmann, A., Rasch, J., & Wildegger, D. (2020). Inclusive groups can avoid the tragedy of the commons. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that costly punishment in public goods games lowers the critical synergy threshold required for cooperation to emerge, thereby increasing the likelihood of cooperation and improving group-level payoff (efficiency) compared to the no-punishment baseline. However, the cost of punishment reduces the total group payoff, so the efficiency gain from increased cooperation is partially offset by punishment costs. The effect is stronger when punishment is more efficient (higher fine per unit cost). The paper also shows that inclusiveness (redistribution of payoffs) is an even more effective way to promote cooperation and efficiency, often making punishment unnecessary if inclusiveness is high enough.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and computational evidence that enabling punishment in a standard public goods game increases group efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline, especially when punishment is efficient (high fine, low cost). The effect is moderated by the costliness of punishment: higher punishment costs reduce the efficiency benefit. The findings also highlight that the structure of payoff redistribution (inclusiveness) can be a more powerful moderator than punishment. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that, all else equal, adding punishment will increase efficiency, but the magnitude depends on punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and the degree of payoff redistribution.

- source: Wang, C. Q., Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2024). Evolutionary dynamics of any multiplayer game on regular graphs. *Nature Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper analytically demonstrates that in structured populations (regular graphs), the introduction of punishment mechanisms (peer or pool) in public goods games can lead to the extinction of defection or to cyclic dominance among strategies, depending on the punishment type and strength. For peer punishment, if the punishment fine exceeds a critical threshold, defection is eliminated and only cooperators and punishers coexist, maximizing group efficiency. For pool punishment, strong punishment leads to cyclic dominance, preventing fixation of defection and maintaining higher group welfare. The paper provides explicit formulas for the critical punishment thresholds and shows that these effects are unique to structured populations and do not occur in well-mixed populations, where defection always retains a basin of attraction. The results are presented in terms of group payoff, mean payoff, and phase diagrams, all directly related to efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for predicting that, in public goods games on regular graphs, enabling punishment (peer or pool) will increase group efficiency and welfare, provided the punishment strength exceeds a critical threshold. The analytical results specify how the effect depends on the number of neighbors (player_count), the punishment cost and effectiveness (punishment_cost, punishment_tech), and the structure of the game (e.g., mpcr, payoff function). The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency, especially in settings with structured populations and multi-strategy dynamics. The explicit threshold formulas for punishment strength can be used to estimate when punishment will be effective in increasing efficiency.

- source: Eldakar, O. T., & Wilson, D. S. (2008). Selfishness as second-order altruism. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game, punishment (even when carried out by selfish individuals) can stabilize cooperation and maintain higher group efficiency. Altruistic punishers are vulnerable to being outcompeted by altruistic nonpunishers (second-order free riders), but selfish punishers—who do not contribute but do punish non-contributors—can coexist with altruistic nonpunishers in a stable equilibrium. This equilibrium maintains a higher level of cooperation and group payoff than would be possible without punishment. The stability and efficiency of this equilibrium depend on parameters such as group size, punishment cost, and the marginal per-capita return. As punishment cost increases or group size increases, the frequency of punishers decreases, but the system can still maintain cooperation if selfish nonpunishers are rare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment in a public goods game increases group efficiency compared to the no-punishment baseline, especially when punishment is not prohibitively costly and group size is not too large. The model is directly relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes in games with punishment enabled, as it analytically derives the conditions under which punishment stabilizes cooperation and increases group payoff. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by punishment cost, group size, and the marginal per-capita return. The model also highlights the importance of considering the types of punishers present (altruistic vs. selfish) and the potential for second-order free riding. For prediction, this paper supports a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency, with the caveat that high punishment costs or large group sizes can reduce this benefit.

- source: Botta, R., Blanco, G., & Schaerer, C. E. (2024). Discipline and punishment in panoptical public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a compulsory, fully monitored PGG, it is possible to achieve full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) by punishing only a fraction of defectors, provided the punishment is sufficiently severe. The model formalizes the trade-off between the fraction of defectors punished (scope) and the severity of punishment (magnitude), showing that these can be tuned to reach stable full cooperation. The results indicate that the threat of punishment, even if not universally applied, can discipline the population and sustain cooperation, thus maximizing group payoff. The findings are entirely theoretical, based on replicator dynamics and equilibrium analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that, in a compulsory and fully observable PGG, enabling punishment (even if not universal) can substantially increase efficiency compared to the no-punishment baseline. The model gives explicit conditions (in terms of player count, MPCR, punishment severity, and punishment scope) under which full cooperation is stable. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that introducing a sufficiently strong and well-targeted punishment mechanism will likely move efficiency close to 1, even if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low. However, the model assumes perfect monitoring, compulsory participation, and centralized punishment, so predictions should be adjusted if these assumptions do not hold in the target environment.

- source: Hauert, C., Traulsen, A., Brandt, H., Nowak, M. A., & Sigmund, K. (2007). Via freedom to coercion: The emergence of costly punishment. *Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can choose whether to participate in the public goods game, costly punishment can emerge and become the dominant strategy, leading to high group efficiency and payoffs. In compulsory participation settings, defectors dominate and group efficiency is low, even if punishment is possible. The model predicts that voluntary participation is essential for the evolutionary stability and emergence of punishment as a mechanism for cooperation. The results are robust across a range of parameter values for group size, multiplication factor, and baseline income.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling voluntary participation in public goods games with punishment will result in higher group efficiency compared to compulsory participation. The presence of costly punishment alone is not sufficient to ensure high efficiency; the institutional context (voluntary vs. compulsory participation) is a critical moderator. For prediction tasks, the model suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on whether players can abstain from participation. Other design dimensions such as punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and MPCR are discussed as parameters, but the main qualitative result is robust to their variation.

- source: Brandt, H., Hauert, C., & Sigmund, K. (2006). Punishing and abstaining for public goods. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, in a public goods game with the option to abstain (be a loner) and with costly punishment, evolutionary dynamics can lead to either a high-efficiency equilibrium (with cooperators and punishers) or a low-efficiency oscillatory state (with only cooperators, defectors, and loners). The high-efficiency outcome is only reached if punishers are present in sufficient numbers initially; otherwise, the system can become trapped in the low-efficiency state. The model contrasts with previous work that predicted inevitable dominance of punishers. The key finding is that punishment can sustain high efficiency, but its success is contingent and not automatic.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games with abstention (loner option) is context-dependent and can be bistable. If the initial conditions or parameters favor the emergence of punishers, efficiency can be high; otherwise, the system may remain at low efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee increased efficiency unless the game design and initial state support the stability of punishers. The model also clarifies the importance of group size, punishment cost, and the structure of participation (abstention option) in moderating the effect of punishment.

- source: Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2017). Competitions between prosocial exclusions and punishments in finite populations. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that, in finite-population public goods games, exclusion strategies (both peer and pool) consistently outperform punishment strategies (peer and pool) in terms of evolutionary advantage, which is measured by stationary frequencies and average payoffs. Peer exclusion dominates in the absence of second-order sanctioning, while pool exclusion dominates when second-order sanctioning is present. The results hold for both optional and compulsory PGGs and are robust to variations in group size and punishment fine (unless the fine is extremely high). The findings imply that, for a given control efficiency, enabling exclusion (especially peer exclusion) will yield higher group efficiency than enabling punishment, and that the presence of second-order sanctioning shifts the advantage to pool exclusion.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in standard PGGs, enabling exclusion (especially peer exclusion) will increase group efficiency more than enabling punishment, across a wide range of parameter settings. The effect is robust to group size, number of rounds (since the model is evolutionary and not round-based), and the relative costs of punishment and exclusion (as long as they are not extremely imbalanced). The presence of second-order sanctioning is a key moderator: without it, peer exclusion is optimal; with it, pool exclusion is optimal. The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment (vs. exclusion), one should expect a positive but smaller efficiency gain than with exclusion, and that the details of sanctioning (peer vs pool, second-order) are critical.

- source: Fowler, J. H. (2005). Altruistic punishment and the origin of cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper presents a formal evolutionary model of a voluntary public goods game with contributors, defectors, nonparticipants, and altruistic punishers. Without punishment, the population cycles between cooperation, defection, and nonparticipation, and cooperation is not stable. When altruistic punishment is introduced, punishers can invade and eventually dominate the population, leading to a stable equilibrium with high cooperation and higher group payoffs. The model shows that punishment can only enforce strategies that are payoff-improving relative to nonparticipation, and that the presence of punishment eliminates the previously observed cycles and leads to persistent cooperation and higher efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in voluntary public goods games, enabling punishment leads to higher group efficiency and welfare, provided that the punishment is sufficiently strong and not excessively costly. The model predicts that punishment will eliminate cycles of cooperation and defection, stabilize cooperation, and increase average payoffs relative to the baseline without punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment in a voluntary PGG-like environment should increase efficiency, but only if the parameters (punishment impact, cost, and net benefit of cooperation) allow for a payoff advantage over nonparticipation. The model also highlights that punishment cannot enforce inefficient or welfare-reducing strategies.

- source: Dong, Y. L., Zhang, B. Y., & Tao, Y. (2016). The dynamics of human behavior in the public goods game with institutional incentives. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In repeated four-player PGGs, institutional punishment and combined reward/punishment significantly increase average contributions and group payoffs compared to control and reward-only. The effect is not due to direct behavioral response to being punished or rewarded, but to a change in self-regarding preferences (b1). The group average payoff is strongly positively correlated with b1 (self-regarding preference) in punishment and combined conditions. The effect of varying the intensity of incentives (Up, Const, Down) is minimal. The presence of institutional punishment is the key driver of higher cooperation and payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a standard repeated PGG with four players, 50 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, and known MPCR, adding institutional punishment (with moderate cost and effectiveness) increases group payoffs and average contributions compared to control. The effect is robust to variations in punishment intensity. Reward alone does not significantly increase payoffs. For prediction, the presence of institutional punishment should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, especially when combined with reward. The effect is mediated by changes in self-regarding preferences, not by direct response to being punished.

- source: Fehr, E., & Gächter, S. (2002). Altruistic punishment in humans. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated public goods game with random group assignment and no reputation or repeated interaction, the opportunity for costly punishment (1 MU cost to punisher, 3 MU cost to target per point) leads to a large and sustained increase in average contributions compared to a no-punishment baseline. Most punishment is directed at below-average contributors by above-average contributors. The threat and execution of punishment both increase cooperation. Individual income is maximized when contributions are close to the group average, and both positive and negative deviations reduce income. The emotional mechanism underlying punishment is negative affect toward free riders. The paper does not report group efficiency as a percentage of the social optimum, but the data and payoff structure imply that efficiency is much higher with punishment enabled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in a standard linear public goods game with four players, six rounds, no communication, continuous contribution choice, and no reputation or repeated interaction. The control (no-punishment) condition shows declining contributions and low efficiency, while the punishment condition shows increasing contributions and much higher efficiency. The punishment mechanism is costly (1:3 cost:impact), and punishment is anonymous. The findings support the prediction that, in similar designs, enabling punishment will substantially increase group efficiency relative to the control. The paper does not manipulate other design dimensions, so its predictive value is strongest for games matching its structure.

- source: Fehl, K., Sommerfeld, R. D., Semmann, D., Krambeck, H. J., & Milinski, M. (2012). I Dare You to Punish Me-Vendettas in Games of Cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In public goods games with multiple rounds of punishment, participants often retaliate and escalate into vendettas, incurring high costs. Although cooperation increases, the costs of punishment and counter-punishment reduce overall earnings, especially for those involved in vendettas. The negative impact on payoffs is clear: participants involved in vendettas or retaliation earn significantly less than those who abstain. In contrast, in dyadic games, punishment and vendettas are rare, and most participants act cooperatively, so efficiency losses from punishment are minimal. The study highlights that while punishment can increase cooperation, it can also lead to costly vendettas that undermine group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling repeated, peer-based punishment in public goods games can increase cooperation but at the cost of reduced efficiency due to costly vendettas. The negative effect on payoffs is especially pronounced for those who engage in cycles of retaliation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, even if control efficiency is moderate, enabling punishment may not increase efficiency and can even reduce it if vendettas are likely. The design dimensions most relevant here are the number of punishment rounds, the ability to identify punishers, and the group vs. dyad structure. The findings are most directly applicable to settings with repeated, open punishment opportunities and identifiable punishers.

- source: Herrmann, B., Thöni, C., & Gächter, S. (2008). Antisocial punishment across societies. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a punishment option in a repeated public goods game increases cooperation and group earnings in some societies, but not in others. In societies with high levels of antisocial punishment, the positive effect of punishment on cooperation and group earnings is diminished or absent. The variation in outcomes is strongly associated with societal-level factors such as norms of civic cooperation and the strength of the rule of law. The study demonstrates that punishment is not universally beneficial for efficiency or cooperation; its effect depends on the prevalence of antisocial punishment, which varies widely across societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is highly context-dependent. When predicting the efficiency of a game with punishment enabled, it is crucial to consider not only the game design dimensions but also the likelihood of antisocial punishment, which may be influenced by societal or group-level norms. In environments similar to those with low antisocial punishment (e.g., Western Europe, US), punishment is likely to increase efficiency. In environments with high antisocial punishment, punishment may have little or even negative effect on efficiency. The game design dimensions in this study (e.g., player count, rounds, punishment cost/tech, anonymity) are held constant, so the main moderator is the social context, not the game mechanics. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not a reliable predictor of treatment efficiency (with punishment) unless antisocial punishment is low.

- source: Muthukrishna, M., Francois, P., Pourahmadi, S., & Henrich, J. (2017). Corrupting cooperation and how anti-corruption strategies may backfire. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This paper experimentally demonstrates that allowing bribery in an institutional punishment public goods game (IPGG) substantially reduces public good provisioning (by about 25%). In the standard IPGG (control), stronger leaders (higher punishment multiplier) increase contributions and public good provisioning. However, when bribery is possible (bribery game, BG), stronger leaders actually decrease public good provisioning, as they use their power to extract bribes rather than enforce cooperation. Anti-corruption strategies (partial/full transparency, leader investment) can increase contributions in some contexts (strong leaders or high economic potential), but may have no effect or even reduce contributions further in contexts with weak leaders and poor economic potential. The main outcome is mean contribution to the public good, which is a close proxy for efficiency. The results show that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly context-dependent and can be reversed or undermined by corruption opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that the presence of corruption opportunities (e.g., bribery) can reverse the usual positive effect of institutional punishment on efficiency. The effect of punishment depends critically on whether leaders can be corrupted, the strength of the leader's punitive power, and the economic potential (mpcr). In standard IPGGs, stronger punishment increases efficiency, but in bribery-enabled games, stronger punishment can decrease efficiency. Anti-corruption interventions (transparency, leader investment) can help, but only under certain structural conditions. When using this paper for prediction, it is important to consider whether the game design allows for corruption/bribery, the strength of punishment, and the economic context. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not reported directly, but the relative changes are clear: punishment increases efficiency in standard IPGG, but decreases it when bribery is possible.

- source: Hetzer, M., & Sornette, D. (2013). The Co-Evolution of Fairness Preferences and Costly Punishment. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The simulation model, based on the structure of standard public goods games with punishment (n=4, mpcr=0.4, punishment cost: 1 MU spent removes 3 MUs from target), shows that when agents evolve under disadvantageous inequity aversion, they develop a stable, empirically-matched propensity to punish defectors (k≈0.25). This level of punishment is sufficient to sustain high cooperation and maximize group fitness/wealth. The model finds that disadvantageous inequity aversion is evolutionarily stable and dominates other preference types, leading to higher average wealth, lower volatility, and longer agent lifetimes. While the model does not report efficiency in the strict sense (group payoff as a fraction of maximum possible), it provides strong evidence that punishment under disadvantageous inequity aversion increases group welfare and evolutionary success compared to self-regarding or non-punishing populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in a standard public goods game with punishment (n=4, mpcr=0.4, punishment cost 1:3), enabling punishment leads to higher group welfare and evolutionary success, provided agents have disadvantageous inequity aversion. The model is calibrated to match empirical punishment levels and shows that these levels are sufficient to sustain high cooperation and maximize group fitness. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar game designs, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency (group payoff/welfare) relative to the control. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative efficiency ratios, so the evidence is most useful for qualitative or directional prediction, especially in settings matching the model's design parameters.

- source: Huang, F., Chen, X. J., & Wang, L. (2018). Conditional punishment is a double-edged sword in promoting cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing conditional punishment into a public goods game can have both positive and negative effects on cooperation, depending on how easy it is for conditional punishers to join in (the threshold H). When H is low, conditional punishment increases the basin of attraction for cooperative outcomes and raises the average frequency of cooperation and punishment, compared to the case without conditional punishment. When H is high, conditional punishment reduces cooperation and makes defection more likely. These effects are observed in both well-mixed and structured populations, and are robust to changes in punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and observation cost. The model tracks average payoffs for each strategy, but the main reported outcomes are behavioral (strategy frequencies, basins of attraction).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency (or related payoff outcomes) in public goods games depends critically on the design of the punishment mechanism, specifically whether punishment is conditional and how easy it is to coordinate punishment. If conditional punishment is easy to trigger (low threshold), it can increase cooperation and, by implication, group payoff/efficiency. If it is hard to trigger (high threshold), it can reduce cooperation and efficiency. The model also shows that the effect is robust to changes in punishment cost, effectiveness, and observation cost. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence and design of conditional punishment (threshold H) are key moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency, and that simply enabling punishment is not always sufficient to increase efficiency.

- source: Abdallah, S., Sayed, R., Rahwan, I., LeVeck, B. L., Cebrian, M., Rutherford, A., & Fowler, J. H. (2014). Corruption drives the emergence of civil society. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with both peer and centralized punishment, the presence of corruption undermines the effectiveness of strong centralized punishment. When centralized punishment is severe and corruption is possible, corruptors exploit the system, leading to lower cooperation and more defectors. Weaker centralized punishment allows peer-punishers to maintain cooperation even in the presence of corruption. Introducing a hybrid strategy (paying both the pool and peer-punishing) can maintain high cooperation across all levels of centralized punishment. The model suggests that a mix of centralized and decentralized enforcement is necessary for robust cooperation, especially in the face of corruption.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency (or cooperation) in public goods games depends critically on the structure of punishment (peer, pool, hybrid), the possibility of corruption, and the severity of centralized punishment. In environments where only centralized punishment is available and corruption is possible, increasing punishment severity can reduce efficiency by enabling corruptors to exploit the system. Allowing peer or hybrid punishment can mitigate this effect and restore cooperation. For prediction, the presence of corruption, the type of punishment available, and the severity of punishment are key moderators. The findings suggest that simply enabling (strong) punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency; the institutional context and the possibility of corruption must be considered.

- source: Nakagawa, M., Lefebvre, M., & Stenger, A. (2022). Long-lasting effects of incentives and social preference: A public goods experiment. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that all three incentive mechanisms (monetary punishment, non-monetary reward, recommendation) significantly increase contributions to the public good during the policy phase. Monetary punishment is most effective in raising contributions, but this effect is not persistent: contributions drop sharply after the punishment phase ends. Non-monetary reward and recommendation also increase contributions, with some evidence of longer-lasting effects, especially for recommendation among certain types (e.g., free-riders). However, the main outcome is contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The study also finds that the effect of incentives varies by social preference type: monetary punishment increases contributions across all types, while non-monetary reward and recommendation mainly affect strong conditional cooperators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling monetary punishment in a repeated linear PGG increases contribution rates during the intervention, but the effect is not persistent after removal. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency outcomes is not possible. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are well specified and match standard PGG lab experiments. The findings support the expectation that punishment increases cooperation (and likely efficiency) during the intervention, but the lack of payoff/efficiency data means the magnitude of efficiency gains cannot be directly inferred. The results are most useful for predicting contribution rate changes, not efficiency per se.

- source: Lefebvre, M., & Stenger, A. (2020). Short- & long-term effects of monetary and non-monetary incentives to cooperate in public good games: An experiment. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Monetary and non-monetary punishments and rewards all significantly increase contributions to the public good compared to baseline. Monetary punishment and non-monetary reward are the most effective. After incentives are removed, contributions remain higher than baseline for several rounds, indicating some long-lasting effect, but eventually decline. The effect of punishment and reward on later contributions depends on whether individuals were high or low contributors and how many points they received. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the findings are limited to contribution behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (especially monetary punishment) increases contribution rates in a standard linear public goods game with four players and 30 rounds. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are highly informative for predicting contribution behavior, but less so for efficiency unless one assumes a direct mapping from contribution to efficiency (which is not always valid, especially when punishment is costly). The design dimensions are well specified, so the paper is useful for understanding the behavioral response to punishment and reward mechanisms in similar lab PGGs.

- source: Chapkovski, P. (2021). Strike one hundred to educate one: Measuring the efficacy of collective sanctions experimentally. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Collective sanctions are not effective at increasing cooperation or contributions in a public good game, and may even reduce them compared to individual sanctions, especially when peer punishment is available. The presence of peer punishment increases contributions overall, but under collective sanctions, contributions are significantly lower than under individual sanctions with peer punishment. Average earnings are slightly lower under collective sanctions, but this difference is not statistically significant. There are notable gender differences: men contribute more under collective sanctions, women less. Perceptions of fairness mediate these effects, with collective sanctions seen as less fair, especially by women.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a standard PGG with 3 players, 15 rounds, continuous contributions, and a fixed threshold for external punishment, enabling collective sanctions (punishing the whole group for one member's under-contribution) does not improve efficiency or group payoff compared to individual sanctions, and may even reduce cooperation and contributions, especially when peer punishment is also available. The presence of peer punishment increases contributions, but collective sanctions reduce this effect. The paper does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, but average earnings are slightly lower under collective sanctions. For prediction tasks, this suggests that switching from individual to collective sanctions is unlikely to increase efficiency, and may decrease it, particularly in small groups with peer punishment. Gender composition may moderate effects.

- source: Clark, J., & Dickinson, D. L. (2020). The effect of sleep on public good contributions and punishment: Experimental evidence. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that, in a standard VCM with 3-person groups and 10 rounds per treatment, the introduction of peer punishment increases contributions for sleep-restricted subjects but not for well-rested subjects. Without punishment, contributions do not differ by sleep condition. There is no significant difference in punishment behavior between sleep-restricted and well-rested subjects. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as a function of punishment, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are robust to various compliance and attrition corrections, but some results are sensitive to the sleep deprivation measure used.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on the effect of punishment on contribution behavior in a standard VCM, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For the downstream prediction task, the paper confirms that, in a 3-player, 10-round VCM with standard punishment (linear cost, known identities), the introduction of punishment increases contributions, especially for sleep-restricted subjects. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not analyzed, the paper cannot directly inform predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment. The design dimensions are well specified, and the behavioral results are likely to correlate with efficiency, but the lack of direct payoff analysis is a limitation.

- source: Paál, T., & Bereczkei, T. (2015). Punishment as a Means of Competition: Implications for Strong Reciprocity Theory. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a 10-round, 4-player PGG with a competitive, rank-based payoff and costly punishment enabled from round 6 onward, average contributions declined steadily and did not increase after punishment was introduced. Punishment was not targeted at low contributors but at those with higher accumulated profits, indicating strategic rather than norm-enforcing use. There was no association between contributions and punishment received or imposed. The results suggest that in competitive environments, punishment can become a tool for rivalry rather than for sustaining cooperation, and does not reverse the decline in cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in a PGG with a competitive, tournament-style payoff structure and full information about others' profits, enabling punishment does not increase efficiency or group payoff. Instead, punishment is used for strategic competition, and cooperation continues to decline. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly context-dependent: in competitive, rank-based settings, punishment may not improve and may even undermine group efficiency. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so this inference is based on contribution behavior and the described use of punishment.

- source: Otten, K., Buskens, V., Przepiorka, W., & Ellemers, N. (2020). Heterogeneous groups cooperate in public good problems despite normative disagreements about individual contribution levels. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that, in a linear public goods game with peer punishment and heterogeneous returns, experimentally induced normative disagreement within groups does not reduce cooperation (measured by average contributions) or increase punishment. Instead, the average normative view within a group predicts the group's contribution level. The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but notes that joint payoffs are maximized at full contribution, and that observed contribution levels are lower than this maximum, consistent with underprovision. The presence of punishment maintains cooperation at moderate levels, but does not eliminate the effect of group-mean norms. The study does not include a no-punishment control, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on the effect of normative disagreement in a linear PGG with peer punishment, but does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, nor does it include a no-punishment control. For prediction tasks, it shows that, in a 3-player, 10-round, continuous-contribution PGG with punishment (cost 1, impact 3), with full information about others' contributions and payoffs but anonymous punishment, group-mean normative views are a strong predictor of contribution levels. The presence of punishment maintains moderate cooperation, but the paper does not allow direct estimation of the efficiency gain from enabling punishment versus disabling it. The results suggest that, under these parameters, punishment supports cooperation even in the presence of normative disagreement, but the efficiency effect relative to no-punishment is not measured.

- source: Bond, R. M. (2019). Low-cost, high-impact altruistic punishment promotes cooperation cascades in human social networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in public goods games, the presence and structure of punishment (especially low-cost, high-impact punishment) increases individual contributions and the persistence and spread of cooperative behavior through the network. The effect of one player's contribution on another's is stronger and endures longer in the low-cost, high-impact punishment condition. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral measures (contribution and its network effects).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that low-cost, high-impact punishment increases individual contributions and the spread of cooperation in public goods games, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results suggest that punishment (especially when low-cost and high-impact) is likely to increase efficiency if higher contributions translate to higher group payoffs, but this is an inference rather than a direct finding. The paper is most useful for understanding how punishment design affects contribution behavior and its propagation, rather than direct efficiency outcomes.

- source: Molleman, L., Kölle, F., Starmer, C., & Gächter, S. (2019). People prefer coordinated punishment in cooperative interactions. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that in a one-shot, three-player binary public goods game with a costly punishment stage, a substantial fraction of participants prefer to punish free riders only if another group member also punishes (coordinated punishment). This preference is especially prevalent among those who do not punish unconditionally. The presence of coordinated punishment is robust across two large samples and is not correlated with conditional cooperation preferences. The study provides detailed behavioral evidence on how punishment decisions are conditioned on others' actions, but does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative about the behavioral mechanisms of punishment in public goods games, especially the prevalence and motivations for coordinated punishment. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff, nor does it compare efficiency between punishment-enabled and punishment-disabled conditions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper suggests that the structure of punishment (e.g., whether it is coordinated) may matter, but does not provide quantitative or comparative efficiency data. The game design dimensions are well specified, which may help in mapping to other studies that do report efficiency outcomes.

- source: Ozono, H., & Nakama, D. (2022). Effects of experimental situation on group cooperation and individual performance: Comparing laboratory and online experiments. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds no significant difference in PGG contribution or punishment behavior between laboratory and online settings among Japanese university students. The introduction of punishment does not significantly increase average contributions or maintain higher cooperation levels, though it may slow the decay of cooperation over rounds. In the online-worker sample, cooperation is lower and the effect of punishment is even weaker. The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution, punishment), not efficiency or group payoff. The paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is likely weak or negligible in these settings, especially with a punishment efficiency of 2 (punishment impact per unit cost).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a standard repeated PGG with a punishment efficiency of 2, enabling punishment does not meaningfully increase average group cooperation or, by implication, efficiency, in either laboratory or online settings among Japanese university students. In a Japanese online-worker sample, punishment may even be associated with lower cooperation. The absence of direct efficiency or group payoff data limits quantitative prediction, but the behavioral results suggest that, under these design parameters, the efficiency gain from enabling punishment is likely minimal or absent. The study also highlights the importance of sample heterogeneity and the specific punishment parameters (cost and effectiveness) in moderating punishment effects.

- source: Kubena, A. A., Houdek, P., Lindová, J., Príplatová, L., & Flegr, J. (2014). Justine Effect: Punishment of the Unduly Self-Sacrificing Cooperative Individuals. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a 12-player PGG, the introduction of punishment leads to a U-shaped relationship between contribution and punishment received: both the lowest and highest contributors are punished, with the most severe punishment falling on the highest contributors (the 'Justine Effect'). Antisocial punishment is primarily carried out by free riders and low contributors. While the paper reports that average contributions nearly double when punishment is available, it does not analyze or report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, nor does it compare these outcomes between the punishment and no-punishment conditions. The focus is on the behavioral dynamics of punishment, not on payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the behavioral dynamics of punishment in large-group PGGs, especially regarding antisocial punishment and its targets. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, which are central to the downstream prediction task. The finding that punishment increases average contributions suggests a possible positive effect on efficiency, but the presence of antisocial punishment (which reduces the payoffs of high contributors) could offset these gains. Without explicit efficiency or payoff data, the paper's value for predicting treatment efficiency is limited to indirect inference based on contribution patterns and punishment behavior.

- source: Dickson, E. S., Gordon, S. C., & Huber, G. A. (2022). Identifying legitimacy: Experimental evidence on compliance with authority. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study demonstrates that a central authority's costly action to improve the fairness of punishment (procedural fairness) increases citizens' willingness to contribute to a public good, even when material incentives are held constant. The effect is robust and persists over time. The mechanism is shown to be intrinsic (normative legitimation) rather than reciprocity or conditional cooperation. However, the main outcome is contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The paper provides strong evidence that legitimacy-enhancing actions by authorities can increase cooperation, but does not directly report on efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence that features of the punishment regime that enhance perceived legitimacy (such as procedural fairness) can increase contribution rates in public goods games with centralized punishment. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that, in similar game designs, legitimacy-enhancing punishment regimes may increase efficiency indirectly via increased contributions, but the magnitude of the effect on efficiency is not quantified here. The paper is most useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators of cooperation under punishment, rather than for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Baldassarri, D., & Grossman, G. (2011). Centralized sanctioning and legitimate authority promote cooperation in humans. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Centralized sanctioning, where a single monitor can punish, increases contributions to the public good compared to a baseline with no punishment. When the monitor is elected (legitimate authority), contributions increase even more than when the monitor is randomly assigned. The effect is attributed to the legitimacy of the authority, not to differences in the monitors' intrinsic characteristics or sanctioning behavior. The study is conducted in the field with Ugandan farmer cooperatives, using a repeated PGG with 8-12 players per group. The main outcome is contribution, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that centralized punishment (by a single monitor) increases cooperation (contributions) in public goods games, and that legitimacy of the authority (election vs. random assignment) further enhances this effect. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for predicting the effect of centralized punishment and authority legitimacy on contribution rates, and suggest that similar effects may be expected for efficiency if contributions translate directly to group payoff in the game design. The study is highly relevant for understanding the impact of centralized punishment and authority legitimacy, but less so for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Fowler, J. H., & Christakis, N. A. (2010). Cooperative behavior cascades in human social networks. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in both standard and punishment-enabled public goods games, individuals' contributions are causally influenced by the contributions of others in their group, and this influence spreads to others in subsequent rounds and groups, up to three degrees of separation. The presence of punishment increases contributions (as established in the original Fehr & Gachter 2002 study), but in this analysis, punishment does not fundamentally change the pattern or extent of behavioral cascades. Punishment can trigger cooperative cascades, but punishment behavior itself does not spread. The main outcome is the spread and persistence of cooperative behavior, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the spread of cooperative behavior in public goods games with and without punishment, but it does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Therefore, it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. However, it does confirm that punishment increases contributions (as in the original Fehr & Gachter 2002), and that the presence of punishment does not fundamentally alter the network dynamics of cooperation spread. For efficiency prediction, this paper is only indirectly informative, as it does not quantify payoff or efficiency effects.

- source: Wu, J. J., Li, C., Zhang, B. Y., Cressman, R., & Tao, Y. (2014). The role of institutional incentives and the exemplar in promoting cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Institutional punishment increases average group contributions above control and reward-only treatments, and the combination of reward and punishment (IRP) is most effective at promoting cooperation. The effect is robust across three incentive schemes (fixed, increasing, decreasing). The study finds that individuals react more strongly to punishment than to reward, especially among high contributors. The presence of 'cooperating-individuals' (exemplars) in a group is associated with higher average contributions, as conformists tend to follow their lead. The main outcome is contribution, not efficiency or group payoff, but the design and behavioral findings are highly relevant for understanding how institutional punishment affects cooperation in repeated PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutional punishment (and especially combined reward and punishment) increases average group contributions in repeated PGGs with four players and 50 rounds. While efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, the contribution results suggest that punishment is likely to improve efficiency relative to control, especially when combined with reward. The experimental design and manipulation of incentive schemes are informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in similar lab PGG settings, but direct quantitative prediction of efficiency is not possible from this paper alone due to the lack of payoff/efficiency reporting.

- source: Weber, T. O., Weisel, O., & Gächter, S. (2018). Dispositional free riders do not free ride on punishment. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that the availability of punishment leads dispositional free riders (DFR) to increase their contributions to the public good to levels similar to dispositional conditional cooperators (DCC). Both DFR and DCC are equally likely to punish non-cooperators, and their punishment expenditures and motives are similar, regardless of whether punishment reduces payoff inequality (3:1 ratio) or not (1:1 ratio). The presence of punishment thus increases cooperation among DFR, but the paper does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, focusing instead on behavioral outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a one-shot public goods game increases contributions among individuals who would otherwise free ride, and that both free riders and conditional cooperators are willing to punish non-cooperators. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does, however, support the expectation that punishment increases cooperation even among those predisposed to free ride, which is likely to translate into higher efficiency in similar game designs. The lack of direct efficiency data is a limitation for quantitative prediction.

- source: Alexander, M., & Christia, F. (2011). Context Modularity of Human Altruism. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that costly punishment (sanctions) increases contributions to the public good, but only in institutional contexts characterized by integration (i.e., integrated schools). In segregated institutional contexts, the availability of punishment does not increase contributions. Group diversity (ethnic heterogeneity) reduces contributions in segregated contexts but not in integrated ones. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the results imply that the effect of punishment on efficiency would be positive in integrated contexts and negligible in segregated contexts, assuming higher contributions translate to higher group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency (proxied by contributions) is highly context-dependent: punishment increases contributions only when institutional integration is present. The game design dimensions most directly supported are player count (4), number of rounds (20), continuous contribution, no chat, and the presence/absence of punishment. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so predictions must infer from contribution rates. The findings suggest that, in similar field settings, enabling punishment will only improve efficiency if the institutional context supports integration among participants.

- source: Fang, Y. H., Benko, T. P., Perc, M., Xu, H. Y., & Tan, Q. M. (2019). Synergistic third-party rewarding and punishment in the public goods game. *Proceedings of the Royal Society A-mathematical Physical and Engineering Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment and reward, when applied together, strongly promote cooperation in a spatial public goods game. The synergy between punishment and reward is more effective than either alone, especially at lower cost to the third party. Lower thresholds for intervention (greater tolerance for defection) and higher intensity of intervention both increase cooperation. More frequent intervention (shorter time intervals) also promotes cooperation. However, the main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the combination of punishment and reward can robustly increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, and that the effect is sensitive to the threshold, intensity, and frequency of intervention. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Its findings are most useful for understanding how design dimensions like intervention intensity, threshold, and frequency affect cooperation rates, which may correlate with efficiency in some settings, but this is not demonstrated here.

- source: Posten, A. C., Ugurlar, P., Kube, S., & Lammers, J. (2025). Maintaining cooperation through vertical communication of trust when removing sanctions. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that when punishment institutions are removed from a public goods game, contributions (i.e., cooperation) typically drop sharply. However, if the authority communicates trust in participants' willingness to cooperate, this drop is substantially reduced, and contribution levels remain similar to those observed when punishment is still in place. This effect is observed for centralized, third-party, and peer punishment mechanisms. The effect of vertical trust communication persists beyond a single round and is mediated by increased trust in coplayers. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be determined, but the findings strongly suggest that vertical trust communication can substitute for punishment in maintaining cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the removal of punishment institutions in public goods games leads to a sharp decline in cooperation (contributions), but that this decline can be prevented if the authority communicates trust in participants' willingness to cooperate. While the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, the close link between contribution rates and efficiency in standard PGGs suggests that vertical trust communication could maintain efficiency at levels similar to those with punishment, at least in the short term. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the presence or absence of vertical trust communication is a critical moderator when punishment is removed, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, etc.) are well specified, but the main outcome is contribution, not efficiency.

- source: Rockenbach, B., & Milinski, M. (2011). To qualify as a social partner, humans hide severe punishment, although their observed cooperativeness is decisive. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that in a public goods game with punishment, players strategically hide low contributions and severe punishment from an observer who may select future partners. Observers focus on observed contributions when making partner choices, largely ignoring punishment behavior. Punishment is used mainly to discipline low contributors, and its presence increases average contributions compared to the no-punishment game. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff as a main outcome, nor does it analyze the effect of punishment on efficiency directly. The main findings are about behavioral strategies for hiding and observing, and the determinants of partner selection.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punishment increases average contributions in a public goods game, but does not directly report on efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks, it supports the idea that punishment can raise cooperation, but the effect on efficiency must be inferred indirectly. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are reported and can be mapped, but the main outcomes are behavioral rather than payoff-based. The presence of strategic hiding and observation may moderate the effect of punishment, but the paper does not quantify this in terms of efficiency. Thus, the paper is informative for understanding behavioral mechanisms, but less directly useful for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Dewey, G., Ando, H., Ikesu, R., Brewer, T. F., Goto, R., & Nishi, A. (2024). Punishment is slower than cooperation or defection in online network games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Punishment decisions are consistently slower than cooperation or defection in repeated network public goods games. The frequency of punishment is low (~6-7%) and is not significantly affected by the imposition of time pressure. Time pressure reduces decision times but does not shift the frequency of punishment, cooperation, or defection. The study classifies punishment decisions by hypothesized mechanism (retaliation, negative reinforcement, inequality aversion, unclassified) and finds no effect of time pressure on the frequency of any mechanism. The paper does not analyze the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff, and payoff outcomes are only reported as descriptive means.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on the behavioral frequency and timing of punishment in repeated network PGGs, but does not report or analyze efficiency or group payoff as a function of punishment. Therefore, it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. It does, however, confirm that punishment is rare and not easily suppressed by time pressure in this design, which may be indirectly relevant for predicting the magnitude of any efficiency effect (i.e., if punishment is rare, its effect on efficiency may be limited).

- source: Rodrigues, J., Leipold, N., Hewig, J., & Hein, G. (2024). The relationship of the source of punishment and personality traits with investment and punishment in a public goods game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that allowing punishment from the common pool (i.e., at no personal cost) or giving participants a choice between punishment sources increases investment in the public good compared to a no-punishment baseline. When punishment must be paid from one's own money, investment does not increase relative to the no-punishment condition. Punishment is more frequent and higher in magnitude when it can be taken from the pool. Personality traits (anger, altruism, empathy) modulate investment and punishment behaviors, but these effects are secondary to the main manipulation. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the design of the punishment mechanism (who pays the cost) affects contribution rates in public goods games. Specifically, punishment that is free to the punisher (taken from the pool) increases contributions, while punishment that is costly to the punisher does not. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on whether punishment is personally costly or not, and that punishment from the common pool may increase contributions but could also reduce total group resources due to the cost being taken from the pool. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful for understanding how punishment design affects contribution behavior, but less so for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Makovi, K., Bonnefon, J. F., Oudah, M., Sargsyan, A., & Rahwan, T. (2025). Rewards and punishments help humans overcome biases against cooperation partners assumed to be machines. *Iscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that both peer rewards and punishments independently increase contributions in a repeated public goods game, regardless of whether partners are assumed to be humans or machines. However, the gap in contributions ('machine penalty') between human- and machine-assumed partners persists unless both rewards and punishments are available together, in which case the gap is eliminated. The combination of rewards and punishments is uniquely effective for machine-assumed partners but inefficient for human-assumed partners. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency is not established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a repeated public goods game with four players and 20 rounds, enabling peer punishment (and/or reward) increases contribution rates, but only the combination of both eliminates the machine penalty in contribution. However, because the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in contribution rates under different sanctioning regimes, especially in human-machine interaction contexts, but less so for direct efficiency prediction unless contribution is used as a proxy.

- source: Krasnow, M. M., Delton, A. W., Cosmides, L., & Tooby, J. (2015). Group Cooperation without Group Selection: Modest Punishment Can Recruit Much Cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, default_contrib, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The simulations demonstrate that when punishment is possible and can recruit labor (i.e., induce free riders to cooperate in the future), even modest levels of punishment are sufficient to sustain high levels of cooperation in public goods games. The model relaxes several assumptions of prior models, allowing for probabilistic punishment, continuous cooperation probabilities, individual recognition, and group mixing. The results show that both punishment and cooperation can evolve by individual selection, even in large groups, and that second-order free riders do not prevent the evolution of cooperation. However, the outcomes reported are behavioral (cooperation and punishment rates), not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment in public goods games can substantially increase cooperation rates, especially when punishment is probabilistic and can recruit labor. The findings suggest that game designs allowing for individual recognition and repeated interactions (mixing ecologies) will see higher cooperation supported by lower punishment rates. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. Its value is in supporting the mechanism by which punishment increases cooperation, which is likely to translate into higher efficiency in similar empirical designs, but this is not directly quantified here.

- source: Kang, H. W., Liu, S. X., Chen, Q. Y., Shen, Y., & Sun, X. P. (2024). Bonus-based mercenary punishment promotes cooperation in public goods games. *Heliyon*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that a bonus-based mercenary punishment mechanism can substantially increase the proportion of cooperators in a public goods game, compared to the standard game without punishment. The effect is sensitive to the size of the bonus, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the allocation of fines collected from defectors. There is an optimal range for the bonus: too little fails to support punishers, too much burdens cooperators. Allocating more fines to punishers (rather than cooperators) is more effective in sustaining cooperation. The model is simulated on a network, and the main reported outcomes are the proportions of each strategy over time and under different parameter settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism (with bonus support for punishers) increases cooperation rates in public goods games, especially when the parameters are tuned appropriately. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The findings suggest that, in similar game designs, enabling punishment (with proper support) is likely to increase cooperation, which is often associated with higher efficiency, but this link is not directly quantified here. The paper is most useful for understanding the qualitative and parametric effects of punishment and bonus mechanisms on cooperation prevalence.

- source: Shin, H. C., Vallury, S., Janssen, M. A., & Yu, D. J. (2022). Joint effects of voluntary participation and group selection on the evolution of altruistic punishment. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
