  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of voluntary participation on the evolution of cooperation and altruistic punishment is asymmetric: in large groups and for a moderate range of nonparticipant payoffs, voluntary participation can increase the long-run frequency of cooperation compared to compulsory participation. If the nonparticipant payoff is too low, voluntary participation has little effect; if too high, it sharply reduces cooperation. In small groups, voluntary participation does not increase cooperation for any nonparticipant payoff. The model tracks frequencies of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff, so direct implications for efficiency are not reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It models a public goods game with punishment and voluntary participation, showing that the effect of enabling voluntary participation (an 'exit' option) depends on group size and the outside option's payoff. While the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, higher frequencies of cooperation (especially in large groups with moderate outside options) suggest that efficiency could be higher in those cases. However, because efficiency is not directly measured, the results should be used as informative indirect evidence: enabling voluntary participation may increase efficiency only in large groups and only if the outside option is not too attractive or too poor. The model's structure and parameterization are relevant for mapping to game design dimensions, but the lack of direct efficiency outcomes limits its predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Shimao, H., & Nakamaru, M. (2013). Strict or Graduated Punishment? Effect of Punishment Strictness on the Evolution of Cooperation in Continuous Public Goods Games. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the effectiveness of punishment strictness in promoting cooperation depends on the population structure. In spatially structured populations (lattice), strict punishment (high strictness parameter) leads to higher evolved cooperation levels than graduated punishment. In random-matching (well-mixed) populations, graduated punishment (low strictness parameter) is more effective at increasing cooperation. The results are robust across a range of parameters for public goods efficiency and punishment effectiveness. However, the paper does not report direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff; instead, it focuses on the average cooperation level as the main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, efficiency) is moderated by the population structure: strict punishment is more effective in spatially structured settings, while graduated punishment is more effective in well-mixed settings. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the design dimension corresponding to population structure (e.g., fixed groups vs. random rematching) is a key moderator of punishment's effect. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its findings should be interpreted as informative about contribution/cooperation rates, which are closely related but not identical to efficiency.

- source: Okada, I., Yamamoto, H., Akiyama, E., & Toriumi, F. (2021). Cooperation in spatial public good games depends on the locality effects of game, adaptation, and punishment. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that localizing either the game interaction or the punishment scope in a spatial PGG model leads to robust cooperation, while globalizing both undermines cooperation. The adaptation (learning) process also interacts with these effects: global adaptation can help spread cooperation if the regime is already cooperative, but can also help defectors invade if cooperation is fragile. The model uses expected payoffs to update strategies, but does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. The findings are most directly about cooperation rates and the stability of cooperative regimes under different locality conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that local punishment and local game interactions are important for sustaining cooperation in spatial PGGs, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are useful for understanding which game design features (local vs global interaction, punishment scope) are likely to support high cooperation, which may correlate with higher efficiency, but the lack of explicit payoff or efficiency outcomes limits its direct predictive value for the efficiency task.

- source: Boyd, R., Gintis, H., Bowles, S., & Richerson, P. J. (2003). The evolution of altruistic punishment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that adding punishment to a public goods game model with group selection allows for the maintenance of high levels of cooperation in much larger groups than group selection alone. The effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation depends on the cost structure of punishment (punishment must be more costly to the punished than to the punisher, and costs to punishers must decline as defectors become rare). The model is robust to various parameter changes, but does not directly report efficiency or group payoff; instead, it focuses on the frequency of cooperation and punishment as the main outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that punishment can increase and sustain cooperation in public goods game-like environments, especially in larger groups, under certain cost structures. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, so its value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is indirect. It is most useful for understanding the mechanisms and conditions under which punishment is likely to have a positive effect on cooperation, which is often correlated with higher efficiency, but this link is not explicitly modeled or measured in terms of payoffs.

- source: Traulsen, A., Hauert, C., De Silva, H., Nowak, M. A., & Sigmund, K. (2009). Exploration dynamics in evolutionary games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in finite-population public goods games with punishment, the rate of random exploration (mutation) crucially shapes the evolutionary outcome. For low mutation rates, defectors dominate in compulsory games, and cooperation/punishment only emerge with voluntary participation. For high mutation rates, cooperators can dominate even in compulsory games, with punishers playing a pivotal but not dominant role. The analysis is based on theoretical models and simulations, reporting the average abundance of each strategy type. The paper does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff, but the prevalence of cooperation and punishment is interpreted as beneficial for group outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation in public goods games is highly sensitive to the rate of random exploration (mutation) in the population. It suggests that punishment alone does not guarantee high cooperation or efficiency unless exploration rates are high or participation is voluntary. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is indirect: it supports the idea that punishment's effect on efficiency is contingent on the broader evolutionary or behavioral context, especially the rate of exploration/mutation. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful for understanding when punishment is likely to increase cooperation, but less so for quantifying efficiency changes.

- source: Battu, B. (2021). Evolution of altruistic punishments among heterogeneous conditional cooperators. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated public goods game with a population of heterogeneous conditional cooperators, the evolution of altruistic punishment (where more willing cooperators are also more willing to punish) can stabilize high levels of cooperation. The effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation depends on the inflicted cost of punishment and the frequency with which punishment opportunities arise. When punishment is frequent and the inflicted cost is high, the population evolves toward more cooperative and punitive types, and donation rates are high. When punishment is infrequent or the inflicted cost is low, cooperation collapses. The model also shows that it is not necessary to punish all free riders equally; targeting persistent (selfish) free riders is sufficient. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but higher donation rates are interpreted as indicative of higher group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment in repeated public goods games with conditional cooperators can substantially increase cooperation rates, especially when punishment is sufficiently costly to the target and opportunities to punish are frequent. However, because the model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, its quantitative value for predicting efficiency is limited. The main actionable insight is that punishment is most effective when it is frequent and sufficiently severe, and that selective (generous) punishment targeting persistent free riders is sufficient to stabilize cooperation. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (with high inflicted cost and frequent opportunities) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, but the magnitude cannot be directly inferred from this paper.

- source: Li, J., Liu, Y., Wang, Z., & Xia, H. X. (2021). Egoistic punishment outcompetes altruistic punishment in the spatial public goods game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study shows that egoistic punishment can evolve and promote high levels of cooperation in both peer and pool punishment modes, outperforming altruistic punishment in a wider range of parameter settings (especially at higher punishment costs and lower fines). The mechanism is robust to different strategy updating rules and population structures. However, the paper does not directly report on group efficiency or total payoff; instead, it focuses on the prevalence of cooperation and the survival of different strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the design of the punishment mechanism (egoistic vs. altruistic) and the parameterization of punishment cost and fine can substantially affect cooperation rates in public goods games. However, because the main outcomes are cooperation rates and not group efficiency or total payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency outcomes. The results suggest that egoistic punishment mechanisms may be more effective at sustaining cooperation under a broader set of conditions, which could indirectly imply higher efficiency, but this is not directly measured or reported.

- source: Chen, X. J., Sasaki, T., & Perc, M. (2015). Evolution of public cooperation in a monitored society with implicated punishment and within-group enforcement. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of implicated punishment (where all group members are fined if a defector is detected) can transform the public goods game into a coordination game, making both full cooperation and full defection stable equilibria. Increasing the monitoring probability, the implicated punishment fine, or the within-group enforcement probability all enlarge the basin of attraction for full cooperation, thus promoting higher cooperation rates. Group size has a nonlinear effect: intermediate group sizes are least conducive to cooperation, while both small and large group sizes favor cooperation. The addition of within-group enforcement (peer punishment) further relaxes the conditions for cooperation to thrive. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but higher cooperation rates in standard PGGs are typically associated with higher efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that implicated punishment and within-group enforcement can substantially increase cooperation rates in public goods games, especially when monitoring probability and punishment fines are sufficiently high. While the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the close relationship between cooperation rate and efficiency in standard PGGs suggests that these mechanisms would also increase efficiency. The nonlinear effect of group size is important: intermediate group sizes may be less effective for promoting cooperation than either small or large groups. For prediction tasks, the presence of implicated punishment and within-group enforcement, as well as the values of monitoring probability, punishment fine, and group size, should be considered as key moderators of the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Quan, J., Liu, W., Chu, Y. Q., & Wang, X. J. (2017). Stochastic evolutionary voluntary public goods game with punishment in a Quasi-birth-and-death process. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment and non-participation strategies into a stochastic evolutionary public goods game changes the evolutionary dynamics and the likelihood of the system reaching cooperative, defecting, or non-participation equilibria. Higher punishment probability (alpha) and lower punishment cost (gamma) both increase the parameter regions where the system is likely to reach cooperative/punishing (C+P) equilibria. However, the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff; instead, it reports the probability of the system selecting each type of equilibrium as a function of the parameters. The results suggest that punishment can promote cooperation under a wide range of conditions, but the effect is moderated by the cost and probability of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: it shows that the likelihood of cooperative/punishing equilibria increases with higher punishment probability and lower punishment cost, and that these effects are sensitive to the marginal per-capita return (r/N) and the fixed income for non-participants. However, because the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, its findings must be mapped from the probability of cooperative equilibria to expected efficiency. The results suggest that, in parameter regions where the C+P equilibrium is likely, efficiency would be higher than in regions dominated by defection or non-participation. Thus, the paper supports the idea that punishment can increase efficiency, especially when punishment is effective and not too costly, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Cong, R., Zhao, Q. C., Li, K., & Wang, L. (2017). Individual mobility promotes punishment in evolutionary public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the optimal mobility (migration tendency) of punishers depends on the cost of punishment. For high punishment costs, a medium tendency to migrate (rather than always punishing or always running) maximizes the prevalence of punishers. For low punishment costs, always punishing is optimal. The presence of cooperators (second-order free-riders) changes the dynamics: in harsh environments (low enhancement factor), punishers benefit from moderate migration, but in more favorable environments, cooperators can dominate, and the advantage of punishment diminishes. The model shows that mobility can help punishers survive, but only under certain cost and environmental conditions. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but higher prevalence of punishers and cooperators is interpreted as beneficial for cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for how punishment, its cost, and the mobility of punishers affect the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the results suggest that punishment can promote cooperation under certain conditions, especially when the cost is not too high and punishers have a moderate migration tendency. For prediction tasks, the findings imply that the effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive when punishment is not too costly and when the game environment allows for spatial structure and mobility. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the precision of quantitative predictions.

- source: Nielsen, Y. A., & Pfattheicher, S. (2024). Separating individual and group-level cooperation in the Public Goods Game. *Pnas Nexus*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that group-level convergence in cooperation (as measured by ICC) increases over the first five rounds of repeated PGGs and plateaus between 20% and 50% under standard conditions. The presence of punishment opportunities and detailed feedback (showing all group members' decisions) substantially increases group convergence, sometimes up to 70%. Small groups (≤4 players) and groups with homogenous social preferences also show higher convergence. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment opportunities and detailed feedback increase group-level convergence in cooperation in repeated PGGs, especially in small and homogenous groups. However, because the primary outcome is ICC (convergence in contributions) rather than efficiency or payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The results suggest that punishment is likely to increase behavioral alignment within groups, which may be associated with higher or lower efficiency depending on the equilibrium reached, but the paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Fehr, E., & Schurtenberger, I. (2018). Normative foundations of human cooperation. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that social norms, particularly conditional cooperation norms, are central to explaining cooperation in public goods games. Punishment opportunities increase cooperation rates, but the welfare (efficiency) effects are often limited or negative due to the costs of punishment and the occurrence of antisocial punishment. Normative constraints on punishment—such as those enabled by institutional or group-level norm formation—can reduce antisocial punishment and make punishment more effective, leading to higher cooperation with less collateral cost. The review emphasizes that the presence of punishment alone is not sufficient for efficiency gains; the structure and normative context of punishment matter greatly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and integrative evidence that enabling punishment in public goods games increases cooperation rates, but does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff unless punishment is normatively constrained to reduce antisocial punishment and collateral costs. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the presence of normative or institutional constraints, the prevalence of antisocial punishment, and the cultural context. Game design dimensions that affect the cost, targeting, and normative context of punishment are likely to be critical moderators of efficiency outcomes. The paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency changes, but it cautions against assuming that increased cooperation from punishment always translates into higher efficiency.

- source: Perc, M., Gómez-Gardeñes, J., Szolnoki, A., Floría, L. M., & Moreno, Y. (2013). Evolutionary dynamics of group interactions on structured populations: a review. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The review summarizes that structured populations (lattices, networks, bipartite graphs) and coevolutionary rules can strongly affect the evolution of cooperation in public goods games. Punishment and reward mechanisms, as well as strategic complexity (e.g., conditional cooperation, pool/peer punishment, rewarding), can promote cooperation and solve second-order free-rider problems in structured settings. The review highlights that the effectiveness and stability of punishment and reward depend on the interplay between game parameters (e.g., group size, benefit functions, network structure) and evolutionary dynamics. However, the main outcomes discussed are cooperation rates and the survival/extinction of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This review provides high-level theoretical and simulation-based context for how punishment and other mechanisms affect cooperation in public goods games on structured populations. It is useful for understanding which game design dimensions (e.g., group size, network structure, strategic complexity) are likely to moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation. However, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence or predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report control vs. punishment-enabled efficiency rates. For the downstream prediction task, this review can inform which dimensions are likely to be important moderators, but cannot be used for direct quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Singer, T., & Steinbeis, N. (2009). Differential Roles of Fairness- and Compassion-Based Motivations for Cooperation, Defection, and Punishment. *Values, Empathy, and Fairness Across Social Barriers*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that fairness-based motivation leads to punishment of norm violations, which can sustain cooperation in public goods games, as shown in referenced empirical studies. Compassion-based motivation, in contrast, can prevent the breakdown of cooperation in noisy environments by promoting forgiveness, but may not be effective in the long term against persistent defectors. The emotional and neural mechanisms underlying these motivations are distinct. The paper does not provide new empirical results or direct measures of efficiency or group payoff, but summarizes that punishment options in public goods games are associated with higher cooperation rates and norm enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and literature-based support for the idea that enabling punishment in public goods games increases cooperation rates and helps sustain cooperation, referencing empirical studies that show this effect. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the general expectation that punishment increases cooperation (and thus likely efficiency), but does not allow for fine-grained prediction based on game design dimensions. It is most useful for understanding the motivational and emotional mechanisms behind punishment and cooperation, and for contextualizing why punishment may be effective in sustaining cooperation.

- source: Nakamaru, M., & Yokoyama, A. (2014). The Effect of Ostracism and Optional Participation on the Evolution of Cooperation in the Voluntary Public Goods Game. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in voluntary public goods games, allowing group members to exclude (ostracize) candidates with low reputation (defectors) leads to the evolution and maintenance of high cooperation rates. Optional participation (where individuals can choose whether to join a group based on group reputation) does not sustain cooperation. The strictest exclusion rule (requiring unanimous acceptance for membership) is most effective at promoting cooperation. The model is evolutionary and simulation-based, and outcomes are reported as the proportion of cooperators, not as group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that costless ostracism (exclusion of defectors) is a highly effective mechanism for sustaining cooperation in public goods games, more so than optional participation alone. While the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the implication is that group efficiency would be higher when ostracism is enabled, since the share of cooperators is much higher. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is costless and takes the form of exclusion, rather than costly point-based punishment. The results are less directly applicable to predicting the quantitative effect of standard costly punishment on efficiency, but they support the qualitative prediction that effective exclusion or ostracism can greatly increase group efficiency if measured.

- source: Liu, L. J., Xiao, Z. L., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2022). Early exclusion leads to cyclical cooperation in repeated group interactions. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods games, the ability to exclude defectors early (after monitoring) leads to cyclical dominance among cooperators, defectors, and excluders, and maintains high average cooperation rates. If exclusion is delayed, defectors can dominate. The model shows that the timing of exclusion, monitoring cost, and discount factor are key moderators. While the model defines payoffs and uses them in evolutionary dynamics, the main reported outcomes are behavioral (strategy frequencies), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task: it shows that enabling a form of punishment (social exclusion) can sustain high cooperation rates if implemented early, and that the timing and cost of exclusion are critical moderators. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (not efficiency or group payoff), the findings should be used as informative-indirect evidence. The model's structure and parameterization (group size, rounds, exclusion cost, monitoring cost) can inform how similar design dimensions might affect the efficiency impact of punishment in repeated PGGs, but direct quantitative predictions about efficiency are not available.

- source: Milinski, M., Semmann, D., & Krambeck, H. J. (2002). Reputation helps solve the 'tragedy of the commons'. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when public goods games are alternated with indirect reciprocity games (where players' reputations are visible and affect future help), cooperation in the public goods game remains high and group payoffs are significantly higher than in treatments where public goods games are played in blocks without reputation incentives. When the possibility of future indirect reciprocity is removed (by announcing only public goods rounds remain), cooperation and efficiency quickly decline. Thus, reputation-based incentives can substitute for direct punishment in maintaining cooperation and efficiency in public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mechanisms which link reputation to future rewards (indirect reciprocity) can sustain high efficiency in public goods games, even in the absence of explicit punishment. For prediction tasks, it suggests that the presence of reputation/reward mechanisms (even without punishment) can lead to high efficiency, especially when players expect future interactions where their reputation matters. The findings are most relevant for settings where reputation is salient and can affect future payoffs, and less directly informative for games with explicit costly punishment but no reputation/reward linkage.

- source: Ozono, H., Kamijo, Y., & Shimizu, K. (2016). Institutionalize Reciprocity to Overcome the Public Goods Provision Problem. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated four-person public goods game, embedding a reciprocal strategy as an institution (institutionalized reciprocity, IR) leads to significantly higher group efficiency (total profit) than a standard control game. The effect is strongest when the institution enforces a strict upper bound on contributions (S-IR), which enables reciprocators to coordinate against free riders and prevents excessive cooperation. In S-IR, group profits are significantly higher than in both the control and a less restrictive IR variant (NL-IR). The IR mechanism operates as a collective punishment system: if any player fails to meet the target, the group's future benefit is reduced for all. The study does not implement standard peer punishment, but the IR mechanism provides a collective sanction that is effective at increasing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutionalized, rule-based collective punishment (via reduction of future group benefit after a deviation) can substantially increase efficiency in repeated public goods games, especially when the institution strictly limits contributions to a target. While the study does not test standard peer punishment, it shows that collective, institutionally enforced sanctions can outperform standard repeated games without punishment. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that introducing a credible, enforced institutional sanction (even if not peer punishment) can raise efficiency well above control, particularly when the rules are clear and binding. The results are most directly applicable to games with four players, 20 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions (in increments), and full feedback on others' contributions and payoffs.

- source: Schnell, E., & Muthukrishna, M. (2024). Indirect reciprocity undermines indirect reciprocity destabilizing large- scale cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a nested PGG environment with both local and global public goods, indirect reciprocity (reputation-based conditional aid) can stabilize cooperation at a single scale, but when multiple scales are present, local cooperation is more stable and can invade global cooperation. This occurs even when the global PGG has a higher theoretical maximum payoff. The model demonstrates that the presence of multiple, potentially conflicting reputations leads to a situation where local cooperation undermines global cooperation, reducing overall efficiency at the larger scale. The more fragmented the population (more local groups), the harder it is for global cooperation to be stable. Thus, indirect reciprocity, as typically modeled, cannot by itself sustain efficient, large-scale cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the structure of group membership and the scale at which reputational incentives operate are critical moderators of the efficiency gains from indirect reciprocity-based 'punishment' (here, conditional aid/reward) in PGG-like environments. Specifically, if the game design allows for multiple, nested groups and reputations can be built at the local level, enabling local cooperation, then enabling indirect reciprocity (punishment/reward) may not increase, and can even decrease, efficiency at the global (large-group) level. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of multiple group scales and the ability to build local reputations are key negative moderators of the efficiency effect of punishment/reward mechanisms. The findings are directly relevant for settings where the control (no punishment/reward) game is inefficient, but the addition of indirect reciprocity does not guarantee higher efficiency at the largest scale.

- source: Stewart, A. J., Parsons, T. L., & Plotkin, J. B. (2016). Evolutionary consequences of behavioral diversity. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in iterated public goods games, increasing the number of available contribution levels (behavioral diversity) can either increase or decrease group efficiency, depending on the ratio of public benefit to individual cost (mpcr). With low mpcr, more choices allow for more robust cooperative strategies and higher mean payoffs. With high mpcr, the abundance of suboptimal robust strategies causes populations to get stuck at lower efficiency. The ability to punish (by reducing one's own contribution in response to defection) is necessary for two-choice strategies to be robust, but explicit costly punishment is not modeled. The paper provides analytic conditions for when simple strategies can resist invasion by more complex ones, and uses evolutionary simulations to show the impact of choice set size on mean payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games as a function of the number of contribution options (all_or_nothing, player_count, mpcr, etc.), but it does not model explicit costly punishment as a separate mechanism. Instead, 'punishment' is operationalized as the ability to reduce one's own contribution in response to others' defection. The findings suggest that increasing the number of contribution options (behavioral diversity) can either help or hurt efficiency, depending on mpcr. For prediction, the paper supports the idea that more behavioral options do not always increase efficiency, and that the effect depends on the underlying game parameters. However, because explicit costly punishment is not present, the results are only adjacent to the core prediction task regarding punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled games.

- source: Salahshour, M. (2021). Evolution of cooperation in costly institutions exhibits Red Queen and Black Queen dynamics in heterogeneous public goods. *Communications Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The model demonstrates that introducing a costly institution (with a participation fee) alongside a free institution in a public goods game can increase cooperation and group efficiency. For low enhancement factors (low profitability), only the costly institution supports cooperation, and average payoffs remain close to the entrance cost (i.e., not much higher than the baseline). For higher enhancement factors, cooperation emerges in both institutions, and group payoffs increase above zero. The costly institution is more likely to be populated by cooperators, as defectors avoid the entrance cost unless there are enough cooperators to exploit. The model also shows that the effect of the entrance cost is context-dependent: it is beneficial for cooperation and efficiency at low profitability, but can be detrimental at high profitability if the cost is too high, as it reduces participation. The results hold in both well-mixed and structured populations, though spatial structure removes some bistability and further supports cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that introducing a costly institution (entrance fee) in a public goods game can increase efficiency and group payoff, especially when the baseline profitability (mpcr/enhancement factor) is low. The entrance cost acts as a filter, keeping defectors out and allowing cooperators to achieve higher payoffs. For prediction tasks, the presence of a costly institution (or, by analogy, a punishment mechanism with a cost to participate) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a control with no such cost, particularly when the marginal per-capita return is low. However, if the cost is too high and the game is already highly profitable, the effect may reverse. The model does not include explicit punishment, but the costly institution's effect is adjacent to punishment in that it deters free-riding through a cost barrier. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where institutional participation costs or costly precommitment mechanisms are present, rather than direct peer punishment.

- source: Zhang, J. L., Zhang, C. Y., & Cao, M. (2015). How insurance affects altruistic provision in threshold public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing insurance for contributors in threshold public goods games increases the provision of the public good and can improve the efficiency (group payoff) of the system. The effect is strongest when the insurance compensation is high and the required threshold for provision is high. The model shows that insurance can shift the evolutionary dynamics to favor contributors over defectors, depending on parameter values. The allocation rule for surplus contributions (parameter omega) also moderates the effect. The paper does not analyze punishment or sanctions, but provides a theoretical basis for how risk-mitigation mechanisms (like insurance) can promote cooperation and efficiency in threshold public goods settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that risk-mitigation mechanisms (insurance) for contributors can increase efficiency in threshold public goods games, especially when the risk of loss is high and insurance compensation is generous. While it does not study punishment, it is relevant for predicting efficiency in games where contributors face risk and can be protected from loss. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, adding insurance or similar mechanisms can substitute for punishment in promoting efficiency. The model is most applicable to threshold public goods games with all-or-nothing contributions, no communication, and no explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The results are parameter-dependent and highlight the importance of the threshold, insurance compensation, and allocation rules for surplus contributions.

- source: Hauert, C., De Monte, S., Hofbauer, J., & Sigmund, K. (2002). Volunteering as Red Queen mechanism for cooperation in public goods games. *Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games, allowing voluntary participation (the option to become a 'loner' and receive a fixed outside payoff) leads to persistent coexistence and oscillations among cooperators, defectors, and loners. This mechanism can sustain cooperation even in large groups and without repeated interactions, memory, or identification of defectors. The average population payoff can, depending on parameters, be higher or lower than the loner's payoff. The paper contrasts this with punishment and reward mechanisms, which require identification of defectors. The findings are robust across well-mixed and spatially structured populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it focuses on the alternative mechanism of voluntary participation. However, it provides important context: it notes that punishment and reward can establish cooperation if defectors can be identified, but that voluntary participation can also sustain cooperation without identification or repeated interaction. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of a voluntary participation (loner) option is a strong moderator of efficiency and cooperation, and that the absence of punishment does not necessarily doom cooperation if such an option exists. The paper does not provide parameterized predictions about punishment effects, so its direct value for predicting efficiency with/without punishment is limited.

- source: Salahshour, M. (2021). Evolution of cooperation and consistent personalities in public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in two-round public goods games, both reward-based and assortative mechanisms can promote the evolution of cooperation and the emergence of consistent cooperative or defective personalities. In the 'reward dilemma' scenario, only first-round cooperators are allowed to play a second PGG, which can incentivize cooperation if the second game's enhancement factor is high enough. In the 'assortative' scenario, both cooperators and defectors play a second PGG in separate groups, and competition between these groups can further promote cooperation and personality consistency. The model shows that cooperation is maximized at intermediate values of the second-round enhancement factor, and that consistent personalities (cooperating or defecting in both rounds) naturally evolve. However, the paper does not model punishment or sanctions, and does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is highly relevant for understanding how reward and assortment mechanisms (and the possibility of repeated or conditional interaction) can promote cooperation and personality consistency, but it does not address punishment, sanctions, or their efficiency effects. The findings may be indirectly informative for prediction tasks involving reward or assortment, but not for punishment. The model's outcomes are primarily behavioral (cooperation rates, personality consistency), not efficiency or group payoff, so it should not be used as a primary source for predicting efficiency effects of punishment.

- source: Wei, M., Wang, X., Liu, L. Z., Zheng, H. W., Jiang, Y. S., Hao, Y. J., Zheng, Z. M., Fu, F., & Tang, S. T. (2025). Indirect reciprocity in the public goods game with collective reputations. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with collective reputation and indirect reciprocity, all of the leading-eight social norm strategies can sustain cooperation in social dilemmas when the group assessment criterion (social strictness) is set at a moderate level. Too relaxed or too strict assessment criteria undermine cooperation. The evolutionary success of different strategies and assessment criteria depends on the payoff environment: stricter criteria are favored in low-payoff (high-dilemma) environments, while relaxed criteria are favored as payoffs increase. The model shows that collective reputation can mitigate social dilemmas and that the interplay between game structure and reputation structure is crucial for the evolution of cooperation. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and efficiency is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task by showing that, in public goods games with indirect reciprocity and collective reputation (but without explicit punishment), cooperation and expected payoffs are maximized at moderate levels of social strictness. The findings suggest that the structure of reputation assessment (a design dimension not in the standard list) can play a similar role to punishment in sustaining cooperation, but the absence of explicit punishment means the results cannot be directly transferred to predict the effect of enabling punishment. The paper is most useful for understanding how non-punitive, reputation-based mechanisms affect cooperation and payoffs, and for contextualizing the importance of social norm design. It does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Smaldino, P. E., & Lubell, M. (2011). An Institutional Mechanism for Assortment in an Ecology of Games. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that capacity constraints (limits on the number of players per game) are a simple and effective institutional mechanism for promoting positive assortment of cooperators in an ecology of public goods games. When group size is limited, cooperators can cluster together and achieve higher payoffs relative to defectors, even without punishment or reputation. Budget constraints (limits on the number of games per player) are less effective. The model does not include punishment, but discusses it as a more complex and potentially costly alternative. The main outcome is the relative cooperator payoff, not group efficiency or total welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not model punishment or sanctions. However, it is relevant for understanding how institutional design (e.g., group size limits) can promote cooperation and higher payoffs without the need for punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that capacity constraints can increase the relative payoff of cooperators, but does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is not directly useful for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in the context of punishment.

- source: Moreira, J. A., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2013). Evolution of collective action in adaptive social structures. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can adapt their social ties—specifically, break links with defectors more quickly than with cooperators—cooperation can be sustained at higher levels than in well-mixed or static networks. The evolutionary dynamics shift from defection dominance to a scenario with stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors, depending on the group size, enhancement factor, and network adaptation rates. Stochastic effects (e.g., mutation, exploration) further help populations escape defection traps and maintain cooperation. However, the paper does not analyze the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it report group efficiency or total payoff as a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding how endogenous partner selection and adaptive network structure can promote cooperation in PGGs, which may indirectly inform predictions about efficiency in environments where such mechanisms are present. However, it does not address punishment or sanctions, nor does it provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that adaptive partner selection can increase cooperation rates, but it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment or other interventions.

- source: Yang, C. L., Zhang, B., Charness, G., Li, C., & Lien, J. W. (2018). Endogenous rewards promote cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The endogenous, budget-balanced reward mechanism in a repeated four-player PGG leads to a substantial and sustained increase in contributions compared to the standard PGG. The mechanism works by taxing each player's income and allowing players to allocate their share of the tax fund as rewards to others. Contributions remain high and even increase over time with rewards, while they decline in the control. The paper finds that most subjects are conditional cooperators, and that reward assignment is strongly correlated with contribution. Net profits (payoffs) are higher under the reward mechanism than in the control, and the mechanism is more efficient than punishment-based mechanisms reported in the literature, which often fail to increase net payoffs due to the cost of punishment. The study does not test punishment directly, but positions endogenous reward as a more efficient and socially desirable alternative.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that a budget-balanced, endogenous reward mechanism can substantially increase both contributions and net payoffs in a repeated PGG compared to a standard control. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling such a reward mechanism (with a 20% tax, peer allocation, and 1:1 reward effectiveness) will lead to higher efficiency than the control, and likely higher than typical punishment treatments (based on literature comparisons). However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on punishment, so its value for predicting the effect of punishment per se is indirect: it shows that reward can outperform punishment in terms of efficiency, especially when punishment is costly. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, feedback, etc.) are well specified and can inform parameterization for similar prediction tasks involving reward mechanisms, but not for punishment.

- source: Hauser, O. P., Hendriks, A., Rand, D. G., & Nowak, M. A. (2016). Think global, act local: Preserving the global commons. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that when participants can observe their neighbors' contributions to the public good and can condition their pairwise cooperation (in a PD) on this information, contributions to the public good are sustained at high levels, even in very large groups (up to 1,000 participants). In the control condition, where such information is not available, contributions decline over time. The mechanism is not direct punishment but rather the withholding of cooperation in a separate pairwise game, which acts as a social sanction. The effect is robust to group size and does not require participants to know the overall level of public good provision. The main outcome is sustained contribution, not measured efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mechanisms allowing for targeted social sanctions (withholding cooperation in a pairwise PD) based on observed public good contributions can sustain high contribution rates in large groups. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to punishment (withholding cooperation as a sanction) but does not involve costly punishment or explicit payoff reduction. The findings suggest that enabling targeted, observable social sanctions—even if not costly—can have a strong positive effect on contribution rates, which may translate to higher efficiency in similar designs, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Zhang, H. R., & Perc, M. (2016). Evolution of conditional cooperation under multilevel selection. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in public goods games with continuous contribution and multilevel selection, the presence of conditional strategies (modeled as piecewise linear response functions) enables the emergence and stability of high cooperation levels. The dominant strategy is robust to initial conditions and parameter changes. The study does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on average contribution and strategy prevalence.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that conditional cooperation strategies can sustain high contribution levels in public goods games under multilevel selection, even without punishment or reward. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff, nor does it manipulate or analyze punishment-related dimensions. Its findings are most relevant for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of explicit sanctioning mechanisms, and for interpreting the role of conditional strategies as an alternative to punishment. It is not directly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment.

- source: Kido, Y., & Takezawa, M. (2025). Empirical evidence for the spread of Cooperation through copying successful groups. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments demonstrate that providing information about a successful, cooperative out-group increases participants' contributions in a repeated PGG, but this effect is transient and does not persist or spread within the group. The absence of mechanisms such as punishment means that cooperation is not stably maintained. The authors argue that additional mechanisms (e.g., punishment, reputation) are likely necessary for the stable diffusion and maintenance of cooperation. The cross-cultural survey finds that greater press freedom is associated with higher impersonal prosociality (e.g., general trust), but not with personal prosociality (e.g., family trust). No direct evidence is provided on the effect of punishment or on efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments, as punishment is not present in the experimental design. However, it provides indirect support for the idea that, in the absence of punishment, even interventions that increase cooperation (such as exposure to successful out-groups) do not lead to sustained or spreading cooperation or higher group payoffs. The discussion and literature review suggest that punishment may be necessary for stable, high-efficiency outcomes in repeated PGGs. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, without punishment, efficiency gains from social learning or imitation are likely to be transient. The experimental design and results can help calibrate the expected baseline (control) efficiency in the absence of punishment, but do not inform the treatment effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Shirado, H., & Christakis, N. A. (2020). Network Engineering Using Autonomous Agents Increases Cooperation in Human Groups. *Iscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing bots that facilitate the breaking of ties with defectors ('disengaged' intervention) in dynamic network public goods games leads to a substantial increase in cooperation rates among human participants, compared to controls (no bots, bots that always cooperate, or tit-for-tat bots). The effect is robust even when only a single bot is present and when humans are aware of the bots' presence. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperative players per round. There is some supplementary evidence that, under the disengaged intervention, cooperators earn more than defectors, but group-level efficiency or total group payoff is not the main focus. The study does not involve punishment or sanctions in the standard sense, but the network exclusion of defectors is interpreted as a form of decentralized ostracism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Instead, it provides evidence about the effect of network engineering (ostracism via tie-breaking) on cooperation rates. The findings suggest that enabling players to break ties with defectors (or having bots facilitate this) can increase cooperation and, to some extent, the earnings of cooperators. However, because efficiency (group payoff relative to the cooperative optimum) is not directly measured or reported, and because punishment is not present, the paper's direct value for predicting efficiency under punishment is limited. It may be useful as adjacent evidence for the effects of exclusion or ostracism mechanisms.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2022). Indirect exclusion can promote cooperation in repeated group interactions. *Proceedings of the Royal Society A-mathematical Physical and Engineering Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that indirect exclusion—where a game organizer probabilistically selects cooperators for future participation—can promote the stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors or even lead to the dominance of cooperation in repeated public goods games. The effectiveness of indirect exclusion increases with higher identification probability and more repeated rounds. The model avoids the second-order free-rider problem associated with costly direct exclusion. However, the main reported outcomes are the equilibrium fraction of cooperators and the stationary distribution of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that indirect exclusion mechanisms can increase the equilibrium level of cooperation in repeated public goods games, especially as the number of rounds and the probability of correctly identifying cooperators increase. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment (or exclusion) on efficiency. The results are most relevant for predicting changes in cooperation rates rather than efficiency, and the exclusion mechanism studied is costless and indirect, differing from standard costly punishment treatments.

- source: Xiao, J. F., Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2023). Evolution of cooperation driven by sampling reward. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the sampling reward mechanism can effectively increase the average cooperation level in both PGG and CRD models. Higher reward thresholds and higher reward intensities promote cooperation, but also increase the cost to the institution. The effect of reward intensity is non-linear: above a critical threshold, further increases yield little additional cooperation. Sample size has a non-monotonic effect on cost, with intermediate sizes minimizing cost. The findings are robust across both game types. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that reward mechanisms (specifically, sampling reward) can increase cooperation rates in PGG-like environments, with effects moderated by reward threshold, intensity, and sample size. However, it does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The paper is most useful for understanding how reward design parameters affect cooperation rates and institutional costs, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled. It does not provide empirical or theoretical results on punishment, nor does it analyze efficiency as defined by group payoff relative to full cooperation.

- source: Battu, B. (2023). Co-evolution of conditional cooperation and social norm. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model finds that high initial cooperation and low contribution costs favor the emergence and stability of high cooperation and moderate norm sensitivity. When contribution costs are high or initial cooperation is low, cooperation fails to establish. The noise parameter (imperfect information about past cooperation) can modulate but not destroy cooperation unless it is very high. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward actions; instead, norm enforcement is internalized as psychic costs for free-riding, which depend on the number of cooperators, the cost of contribution, and individual norm sensitivity. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are not directly translatable to efficiency prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how internalized norm enforcement (psychic costs) and conditional cooperation can sustain cooperation in repeated PGGs, especially under low contribution costs and high initial cooperation. However, it does not model or report the effects of explicit punishment mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of initial conditions, contribution cost, and norm sensitivity for the emergence of cooperation, but not for quantifying efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Samu, F., Piras, S., Righi, S., Setti, M., & Takács, K. (2025). Cooperation is not rewarded by friendship, but generous and selfish students repel each other in social networks. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in a sample of 10-year-old students, measured cooperation and prosociality in economic games (including a PGG) do not predict receiving more friendship nominations or being avoided via negative ties. There is no evidence that prosocial students are more popular or that selfish students are more disliked. However, there is a significant tendency for students with dissimilar levels of generosity (especially in the Dictator Game) to form negative ties with each other, suggesting that social network repulsion is structured by differences in prosociality. The study does not analyze or report any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. While it uses a standard PGG to measure individual cooperation, the outcomes are social network nominations (friendship and negative ties), not payoffs or efficiency. The only adjacent relevance is the interpretation of negative ties as a form of informal social punishment, but this is not a formal or incentivized punishment mechanism as in PGG experiments with punishment. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform quantitative predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Kümmerli, R., Burton-Chellew, M. N., Ross-Gillespie, A., & West, S. A. (2010). Resistance to extreme strategies, rather than prosocial preferences, can explain human cooperation in public goods games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: Across four lab experiments, when the public goods game is modified so that full cooperation is individually optimal (e.g., by setting the multiplier above the group size or by adding group rewards), mean contributions and group efficiency increase significantly compared to the standard PGG. However, contributions remain well below 100% and full efficiency is not achieved. The proportion of subjects contributing fully in the last round of the modified games ranges from 35% to 72%, and the mean group contribution is always significantly less than the maximum possible. The results challenge the interpretation that high cooperation in PGGs is due to prosocial preferences, and instead point to psychological resistance to extreme strategies (full defection or full cooperation) as a key driver. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) or otherwise aligning individual and group incentives increases group efficiency in public goods games. However, even when efficiency-maximizing behavior is straightforward, groups do not reach full efficiency, suggesting that psychological factors limit the effect. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency as a function of mpcr and group reward structures, but do not inform the effect of punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning is present in any treatment. Thus, the paper is highly informative for baseline/control efficiency prediction, but does not support prediction of the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Chung, D., Yun, K., Kim, J. H., Jang, B., & Jeong, J. (2011). Different Gain/Loss Sensitivity and Social Adaptation Ability in Gifted Adolescents during a Public Goods Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that gifted adolescents are more cooperative and earn higher total payoffs than average adolescents in a repeated binary public goods game with five players and no punishment or reward mechanisms. Three incentive conditions are tested: (1) standard PGG, (2) no-loss guarantee for cooperators, and (3) equalized bonus for all in successful rounds. Gifted adolescents show higher gain sensitivity and lower loss sensitivity, and are more strategic in maximizing payoffs. Average adolescents are more loss-averse, less strategic, and more influenced by social adaptation behaviors. The study provides detailed evidence on how group composition (gifted vs. average) and incentive structure affect efficiency and cooperation, but does not manipulate or analyze punishment or sanctioning.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on efficiency and payoff outcomes in repeated binary PGGs without punishment or reward. It is directly informative for predicting efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions with known game design parameters (player count, rounds, MPCR, etc.). However, it does not inform the effect of enabling punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present or discussed in the experimental design. Thus, it is useful for establishing baseline efficiency but not for predicting the effect of punishment.

- source: Schmidt, K. M., & Ockenfels, A. (2021). Focusing climate negotiations on a uniform common commitment can promote cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that negotiation design is crucial for promoting cooperation and efficiency in public goods games. Uniform common commitments (UCC) result in group investments averaging 83% of the social optimum with enforcement, compared to 45% (individual commitments, IC) and 48% (complex common commitments, CCC). Even without enforcement, UCC achieves 52% of the optimum, while IC and CCC achieve only 34%. The UCC design is robust to asymmetries and to the absence of enforcement. The paper does not study punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that negotiation design—specifically, requiring a uniform minimum contribution—can dramatically increase group efficiency in public goods games, even in the absence of enforcement. However, it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It is highly relevant for understanding the impact of commitment and negotiation structure, but not for the prediction task focused on punishment.

- source: Alvarez-Rodriguez, U., Battiston, F., de Arruda, G. F., Moreno, Y., Perc, M., & Latora, V. (2021). Evolutionary dynamics of higher-order interactions in social networks. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper shows that in PGGs with higher-order interactions (hypergraphs), the critical synergy factor required for cooperation decreases as group size increases in uniform random hypergraphs, meaning larger groups can foster cooperation more easily (at a given synergy factor). Heterogeneity in group membership (hyperdegree) and group size can either facilitate or hinder cooperation, depending on the structure: scale-free (hub-dominated) hypergraphs inhibit cooperation, while uniform or less heterogeneous structures do not. The framework is validated with empirical data from scientific collaborations, showing that the synergy factor (group benefit) is a non-monotonic function of group size, with an optimal group size for maximum benefit. The paper does not study punishment, rewards, or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency and payoff outcomes in PGG-like environments with varying group size, group structure, and synergy factor, but it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions. It is directly relevant for understanding how group size and network heterogeneity affect the critical conditions for cooperation and group payoff, but cannot inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment. Use this paper to inform baseline efficiency predictions in control (no-punishment) conditions as a function of group size, group structure, and synergy factor, but not for treatment effects of punishment.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Chen, X. J. (2021). Cooperation and competition between pair and multi-player social games in spatial populations. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary competition between 'alone' (pairwise) and 'together' (group) attitudes leads to complex phase behavior. At low public goods multiplication factors, pairwise interactions dominate; at intermediate values, both attitudes coexist but this can paradoxically lower average income; at high multiplication factors, group interactions dominate and cooperation can be sustained. The presence of the 'together' attitude at low multiplication factors can actually reduce average income, contrary to intuition. However, the paper does not study punishment or sanctions, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants.
