It is useful for understanding how the structure of interaction (pairwise vs. group) and the public goods multiplication factor affect efficiency, but it does not address punishment, sanctions, or related mechanisms. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects.

- source: Meloni, S., Xia, C. Y., & Moreno, Y. (2017). Heterogeneous resource allocation can change social hierarchy in public goods games. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that when players in a networked PGG can allocate their contributions unevenly—favoring games where they previously earned higher payoffs—this leads to increased cooperation, a more heterogeneous distribution of investments, and a shift in the structure of wealth production. Specifically, small clusters of cooperators become highly productive, and the overall wealth distribution approaches the Pareto law (80% of wealth produced by 20% of nodes) for sufficiently strong payoff-driven allocation. The study also shows that the classical PGG (equal allocation) does not reproduce these features. The model is robust to different evolutionary update rules and network structures. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are present or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions in PGGs or their impact on efficiency. It is highly relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in resource allocation affects efficiency and wealth distribution in PGGs, but it cannot inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task—estimating the efficiency impact of punishment given game design dimensions—this paper is not directly informative, as punishment is not present in the model.

- source: Santos, F. C., Santos, M. D., & Pacheco, J. M. (2008). Social diversity promotes the emergence of cooperation in public goods games. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing social diversity via heterogeneous (scale-free) network structures and varying individual participation in multiple public goods games can significantly enhance the emergence and stability of cooperation. Two main models are compared: (1) fixed cost per game (each cooperator pays a cost in every game they play), and (2) fixed cost per individual (each cooperator pays a total cost, divided among all games they play). The latter model, combined with network heterogeneity, leads to a dramatic increase in the prevalence of cooperation, even at lower enhancement factors. The paper also shows that in all-cooperator populations, wealth (fitness) distributions are more unequal on scale-free graphs, but the overall number of 'poor' is reduced under the fixed cost per individual model. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or reputation mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff relative to the cooperative optimum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it explicitly excludes punishment and focuses on the effects of social diversity and network structure. It is useful for understanding how heterogeneity in group structure and contribution rules can promote cooperation, but does not inform the impact of punishment or sanctions. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be relevant for understanding baseline cooperation rates in the absence of punishment, especially in heterogeneous network settings.

- source: Pan, X. S., & Houser, D. (2011). Competition for Trophies Triggers Male Generosity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that in a standard linear public goods game, the introduction of a unique and displayable reward (a 'trophy' mug) leads to significantly higher contributions, especially among males, compared to both a baseline (no reward) and a control reward (ice-cream) that is not unique or displayable. The effect is driven by male competitiveness for the trophy. Female behavior is less affected by the trophy. The paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings as outcomes. The main outcomes are behavioral: contribution rates, approval points, and star rewards.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding the effects of non-pecuniary, status-based rewards (trophies) on cooperation, especially among males, but does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The game design dimensions are well described, but the absence of punishment and lack of efficiency or payoff analysis means this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Suri, S., & Watts, D. J. (2011). Cooperation and Contagion in Web-Based, Networked Public Goods Experiments. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that, in a networked public goods game with 24 players and no punishment or reward options, the structure of the network (from highly clustered cliques to random regular graphs) does not significantly affect average contributions. Players do exhibit conditional cooperation (responding to neighbors' contributions), but this does not translate into higher overall cooperation in more clustered networks, nor does it produce multi-step contagion. The absence of punishment or reward means the findings are limited to baseline public goods dynamics. No direct measures of efficiency or group payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence about baseline (control) public goods game behavior in networked settings without punishment or reward. It is directly relevant for predicting control efficiency in such games, but does not inform the effect of enabling punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present or discussed in the experimental design. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, network structure alone does not improve cooperation or efficiency. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is useful for estimating control efficiency in networked PGGs, but provides no evidence on the effect of punishment or its moderators.

- source: Isler, O., Maule, J., & Starmer, C. (2018). Is intuition really cooperative? Improved tests support the social heuristics hypothesis. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that time-pressure (intuitive decision-making) increases contributions to the public good compared to forced-delay (reflective decision-making), especially under certain instructional conditions. The effect is robust to compliance and understanding confounds. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include a punishment condition or analyze payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on contribution rates under different cognitive conditions may be contextually informative for understanding cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency with or without punishment. The design dimensions reported (e.g., player count, MPCR, no punishment, no reward, no chat) may help contextualize the baseline, but the absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes limits its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Fosgaard, T. R., & Piovesan, M. (2015). Nudge for (the Public) Good: How Defaults Can Affect Cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that while most subjects do not simply accept the default contribution values, the presence of a default (either free rider or perfect conditional cooperator) significantly influences their stated cooperation strategies and actual contributions in a subsequent repeated public goods game. Specifically, exposure to a cooperative default increases later contributions, while a free rider default decreases them. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning is present. It does, however, show that non-binding defaults can shift contribution behavior, which may indirectly inform models of cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, as it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes.

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that in public goods games simulated with deep Q-learning agents, the rate of cooperation increases as group size increases, and also as MPCR increases. However, the cooperation rate plateaus at around 50% for large group sizes or high MPCR. The model does not include punishment, reward, or communication mechanisms. The outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff. The results are consistent with some human experiments, but the model is limited to binary (all-or-nothing) contribution choices and does not address payoff-based efficiency directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism. It is useful for understanding baseline cooperation rates in the absence of punishment, especially as a function of group size and MPCR, but cannot inform the effect of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper may help estimate control (no-punishment) efficiency in all-or-nothing, no-punishment, no-reward, no-chat settings, but provides no evidence on the effect of punishment or moderators of punishment's effect.

- source: Cone, J., & Rand, D. G. (2014). Time Pressure Increases Cooperation in Competitively Framed Social Dilemmas. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that time pressure increases cooperation (measured as contribution to the public good) in a competitively framed public goods game, but not in the standard cooperative frame. The effect is robust across two datasets (the authors' and a prior study). The results support the Social Heuristics Hypothesis, suggesting that intuitive responses favor cooperation, especially in novel or less familiar contexts. However, the study does not include any punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding moderators of cooperation (such as time pressure and framing), but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The game design dimensions are well specified, but the absence of punishment and lack of payoff-based outcomes mean this paper should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Johnson, T. (2024). Empirically testing a relationship between cooperation and the prime numbers. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that, in a 27-round strangers public goods game (no punishment, no reputation), contributions generally decline over time, but a minority of participants continue to contribute even in late rounds. There is modest evidence that contributions are slightly higher at prime-numbered rounds, but this effect is driven by early rounds (2 and 3) and disappears when they are excluded. Placebo tests suggest the observed effect is unlikely to be spurious, but exploratory analysis casts doubt on the substantive importance of the prime-number effect. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, and the experimental condition does not include punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as the analyzed condition lacks any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide a clear description of a standard PGG baseline (strangers, 4 players, 27 rounds, no punishment, no chat, continuous contributions, MPCR=0.1), which can be used as a control condition in comparative studies. The findings on contribution decline and the absence of efficiency or payoff analysis mean this paper is only useful for establishing baseline behavior in the absence of punishment.

- source: Wang, G. R., Li, J. B., Wang, W. H., Niu, X. F., Wang, Y., & Selten, R. (2024). Confusion cannot explain cooperative behavior in public goods games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that, after ensuring participants understand the public goods game, most still do not behave as pure free riders, especially when playing with humans. Social preferences and social norms, rather than confusion, explain much of the observed cooperation. The difference in contributions between human and computer treatments is attributed to social preferences. However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. It is useful for understanding baseline cooperation and the role of confusion and social preferences in standard PGGs, but does not inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only relevant as a control/baseline for PGGs without punishment, and does not provide evidence about any game design dimension's moderation of punishment effects.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., El Mouden, C., & West, S. A. (2016). Conditional cooperation and confusion in public-goods experiments. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a standard linear public-goods game (4 players, 1.6 multiplier, no punishment or reward), most participants do not understand that the income-maximizing strategy is to contribute nothing, regardless of others' actions. Many participants classified as 'conditional cooperators' are actually confused, not prosocial. The same behavioral patterns are observed when playing with computers (where cooperation cannot benefit others) as with humans. There is no evidence that a subpopulation of 'comprehenders' (those who understand the game) are more cooperative with humans than with computers. The paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides no direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatment. However, it is highly informative about baseline (control) PGGs without punishment: it shows that observed cooperation in such games may be largely due to misunderstanding, not prosocial preferences. For prediction tasks, this suggests that control group efficiency in standard PGGs may overestimate the true baseline level of prosociality, especially if participants misunderstand the payoff structure. The paper does not inform how punishment would affect efficiency, nor does it provide evidence on moderators of punishment effects.

- source: Burton-Chellew, M. N., & West, S. A. (2013). Prosocial preferences do not explain human cooperation in public-goods games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that (1) cooperation levels in public-goods games are not explained by prosocial preferences, as participants contribute similarly even when unaware their actions affect others; (2) providing enhanced information about others' earnings reduces cooperation; (3) even when cooperation is profitable, participants do not reach full cooperation. The main outcome is contribution level, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or sanctioning is present in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments. However, it does provide high-quality evidence on how information and framing affect cooperation rates in standard and variant PGGs. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does reinforce the importance of information structure and participant understanding as moderators of cooperation, which may interact with punishment in other studies.

- source: Lamba, S., & Mace, R. (2011). Demography and ecology drive variation in cooperation across human populations. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds significant variation in cooperative behavior (measured by PGG contributions and salt-sharing) across villages of the same cultural group. Demographic factors such as age, social network size, village population size, and number of adult sisters in the village are associated with these cooperative behaviors. The results challenge the view that stable cultural norms alone explain cross-population differences in cooperation, highlighting the role of local demography and ecology. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms were present in the games, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes were reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism was included. However, it is informative for understanding baseline variation in cooperation in one-shot PGGs without punishment, and for the influence of demographic and ecological moderators. For the downstream prediction task, this paper can inform estimates of control (no-punishment) efficiency in similar field settings, but provides no evidence on the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Kurzban, R., & Houser, D. (2005). Experiments investigating cooperative types in humans: A complement to evolutionary theory and simulations. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a standard public goods game (no punishment or reward), individuals fall into stable behavioral types: cooperators, reciprocators, and free-riders. The composition of these types within a group strongly predicts the group's contribution level. Groups with more cooperative types contribute more to the public good, and this relationship is robust across in-sample and out-of-sample games. Despite large behavioral differences, individual earnings are statistically indistinguishable across types. The study provides strong evidence for the existence and stability of cooperative types, and for the predictive power of group composition on cooperation, but does not address the effects of punishment or efficiency changes due to institutional design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding baseline behavior in public goods games without punishment or reward. It provides evidence that group outcomes (contributions, and thus efficiency/payoff) are strongly determined by the distribution of behavioral types. However, it does not provide any evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions, nor does it manipulate or analyze any institutional design features related to punishment, reward, or efficiency interventions. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with and without punishment), this paper is only useful as a control/baseline reference for games without punishment, and for understanding the role of type composition. It does not inform the effect of enabling punishment or any moderators of that effect.

- source: Horita, Y., Takezawa, M., Inukai, K., Kita, T., & Masuda, N. (2017). Reinforcement learning accounts for moody conditional cooperation behavior: experimental results. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that moody conditional cooperation (MCC) patterns are present in both repeated multiplayer PDG and PGG, though the specific form differs from previous literature. In the PGG, conditional cooperation is observed in both fixed and mixed group treatments. Reinforcement learning models (Bush-Mosteller and Roth-Erev) fit the observed behavioral data as well as or better than CC and MCC models, especially when direct reciprocity is absent. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It is useful for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics and the explanatory power of behavioral models in repeated PGGs with and without stable group composition. For the prediction task, this paper may inform expectations about baseline (control) cooperation rates in the absence of punishment, but provides no evidence about the effect of punishment or moderators of punishment efficacy.

- source: van den Berg, P., Dewitte, P., Aertgeerts, I., & Wenseleers, T. (2020). How the incentive to contribute affects contributions in the one-shot public goods game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that average contributions in a one-shot public goods game increase with the marginal per capita return (MPCR), with most of the increase occurring at lower MPCR values (below 0.58–0.64). At higher MPCR, contributions plateau well below full cooperation. The experiment uses groups of three, no punishment or reward, and no communication. The findings provide a high-resolution mapping of how MPCR shapes contribution behavior, but do not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting baseline (control) efficiency in public goods games as a function of MPCR, group size, and the absence of punishment or reward. It does not provide any evidence about the effect of punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is useful for estimating control efficiency (without punishment) given MPCR and group size, but does not inform the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Yam, P. P. C., Ng, G. T. T., Au, W. T., Tao, L., Lu, S., Leung, H., & Fung, J. M. Y. (2018). The effect of subgroup homogeneity of efficacy on contribution in public good dilemmas. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that arranging participants into homogeneous or heterogeneous subgroups of efficacy does not significantly affect the number or proportion of contributions to the public good. This is because two mediating mechanisms—perceived efficacy and expectation of subgroup contribution—operate in opposite directions and cancel each other out. The experiment does not include any punishment, sanction, or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are limited to contribution behavior and psychological mediators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present or discussed. The design dimensions studied (subgroup homogeneity, efficacy) are not among the core prediction dimensions for punishment effects. The outcome measures are contribution rates and psychological variables, not efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Bose, N., & Sgroi, D. (2022). The role of personality beliefs and small talk in strategic behaviour. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Allowing 4 minutes of pre-game chat (small talk) between paired subjects leads to higher contributions in a two-person public goods game compared to a control group with no chat. This effect is mediated by beliefs about the partner's extraversion, which are formed during the chat. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding the effect of pre-game communication and personality beliefs on cooperation, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It does provide strong evidence that pre-game chat (communication) increases cooperation in a two-person public goods game, mediated by personality beliefs, but does not report group payoff or efficiency. For the downstream prediction task (punishment effect on efficiency), this paper is not directly informative, but it does support the importance of communication as a moderator of cooperation. It cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Gong, X., & Sanfey, A. G. (2017). Social rank and social cooperation: Impact of social comparison processes on cooperative decision-making. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that social comparison, operationalized as being assigned a high or low rank in a prior task, significantly affects subsequent contribution decisions in a standard public goods game. High rank leads to increased contributions, low rank to decreased contributions, compared to a baseline with no ranking. These effects are robust to whether the PGG group is the same as the ranking group, and are more pronounced in individuals with high social comparison orientation. The experiment does not include any punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to the prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are limited to how social comparison affects contribution behavior. While the game design (standard PGG) is relevant, the absence of punishment means it cannot inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. The paper may be useful for understanding baseline contribution behavior or the impact of social comparison, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Lotz, S. (2015). Spontaneous Giving under Structural Inequality: Intuition Promotes Cooperation in Asymmetric Social Dilemmas. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that priming participants to use intuition (vs. reflection) increases their contributions to the public good in a one-shot, four-player PGG with asymmetric MPCRs (structural inequality). The effect of intuition is robust to controls for age, gender, comprehension, and paragraph length. There is no significant main effect of MPCR (high vs. low) on cooperation, nor an interaction between intuition and MPCR. The study does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is relevant as a baseline for understanding cooperation rates in asymmetric, one-shot PGGs without punishment or reward. The findings may inform the expected control (no-punishment) efficiency in similar asymmetric games, but cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment. The absence of punishment and the focus on contribution rates (not payoffs) limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Battu, B., & Srinivasan, N. (2020). Evolution of conditional cooperation in public good games. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that high and stable cooperation (measured by donation rates) can be achieved in repeated public good games when agents' social learning is sufficiently biased toward prestige (social value) or when both prestige and payoff are weighted but with some noise. When imitation is driven mainly by payoff, cooperation collapses. The model does not include punishment or reward, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding how social learning mechanisms (prestige vs. payoff) affect the evolution of cooperation, but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public good games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, suggesting that cooperation can be sustained by prestige-biased social learning even in the absence of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper does not provide usable quantitative or qualitative evidence about the efficiency impact of punishment or about moderators of punishment effects.

- source: Cárdenas, J. C., Janssen, M. A., Ale, M., Bastakoti, R., Bernal, A., Chalermphol, J., Gong, Y. Z., Shin, H., Shivakoti, G., Wang, Y. B., & Anderies, J. M. (2017). Fragility of the provision of local public goods to private and collective risks. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when the returns from the public good are risky (collective risk), participants invest less in the public good compared to when only private returns are risky or when there is no risk. Communities more integrated with the market (higher share of crops sold externally, more members with outside income) show lower cooperation, especially under collective risk. Risk aversion is associated with higher cooperation in the baseline, but not under risk treatments. The main outcome is the rate of investment in the public good; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed. No punishment or sanctioning is present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any treatment. It does provide evidence on how risk and market integration affect cooperation rates in public good games, but not on efficiency or payoff outcomes, and not on the effect of punishment. For the downstream prediction task (treatment efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper is not directly relevant.

- source: Rand, D. G., Kraft-Todd, G., & Gruber, J. (2015). The Collective Benefits of Feeling Good and Letting Go: Positive Emotion and (dis)Inhibition Interact to Predict Cooperative Behavior. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across two large-sample studies using one-shot PGGs (and some continuous PDs), the presence of positive emotion in participants' narratives predicts higher contributions, while the presence of inhibition predicts lower contributions. There is a significant interaction: the positive effect of emotion is strongest when inhibition is absent. These effects are robust to controls for negative emotion and demographic variables. The paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or any efficiency or payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment. It does not manipulate or analyze any game design dimensions related to punishment, nor does it report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare. Its findings on psychological predictors of cooperation may be of indirect interest for understanding baseline cooperation rates in one-shot games, but are not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Tomassini, M., & Antonioni, A. (2021). Computational behavioral models in public goods games with migration between groups. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that allowing unsatisfied players to migrate between groups increases average contribution in public goods games, especially for lower enhancement factors (r). The effect of group size and update timing (synchronous vs asynchronous) is minor. Heterogeneity in migration propensity has little effect on average contribution compared to a homogeneous population. The amount of information available to players (own payoff/contribution vs also knowing the number of contributors in the group) does not substantially change the results. However, the paper does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings on migration as an alternative to punishment may be contextually informative for understanding other forms of exit or mobility, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The reported outcomes are average contribution rates, not efficiency or payoff, and thus are only adjacent to the prediction task.

- source: Chang, S. H., Zhang, Z. P., Li, Y., Wu, Y. E., & Xie, Y. Y. (2018). Investment preference promotes cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that allowing cooperators to preferentially invest in groups with more cooperators (higher alpha) and to use longer memory of past group composition increases the fraction of cooperators in both well-mixed and structured populations. The mechanism leads to the formation of larger and more robust cooperation clusters, which can dominate the population under favorable conditions. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, as they show that mechanisms which allow for adaptive investment based on group composition can promote cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as providing only contextual or adjacent evidence, not direct input.

- source: Huang, K. K., Wang, T., Cheng, Y., & Zheng, X. P. (2015). Effect of Heterogeneous Investments on the Evolution of Cooperation in Spatial Public Goods Game. *Plos One*.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneity in cooperative investments (modeled as a uniform distribution around a mean) increases the steady-state fraction of cooperators in a spatial public goods game. This effect is stronger with greater variance in investment. When both strategy and investment coevolve, the promotion of cooperation is weaker because investment heterogeneity is reduced. The main dependent variable is the fraction of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, as they show that heterogeneity in investment can promote cooperation (as measured by the fraction of cooperators), but do not address payoff-based outcomes or the effect of punishment. It should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Roca, C. P., & Helbing, D. (2011). Emergence of social cohesion in a model society of greedy, mobile individuals. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial public goods game with voluntary contributions and no punishment or reward, cooperation and social cohesion can emerge if individuals have moderate levels of greediness (aspiration). Too little greediness leads to stagnation in noncooperation, while too much greediness destabilizes cooperation and social ties. The model demonstrates that these beneficial levels of greediness can be evolutionarily selected. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, agglomeration, instability), not efficiency or group payoff. The model does not include or analyze punishment, and thus provides no direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It models a PGG environment without punishment or reward, and focuses on the role of individual learning and mobility. While it is useful for understanding alternative mechanisms for cooperation, it does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects. The game design dimensions are partially covered (e.g., player count, group size, mpcr), but the absence of punishment means this paper cannot be used to predict efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Inaba, M., & Akiyama, E. (2023). Evolution of cooperation in multiplex networks through asymmetry between interaction and replacement. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in multiplex network models of public goods games, the symmetry or asymmetry between the scope of interaction and the scope of strategy replacement can have non-monotonic effects on the evolution of cooperation. Specifically, in some cases, increasing asymmetry (e.g., local interaction with global replacement) can partially promote cooperation, contrary to previous findings that symmetry always promotes cooperation. The main outcome is the cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as punishment is not modeled. However, it is relevant for understanding how network structure and the symmetry of interaction and learning/replacement can affect cooperation rates. The findings may be useful for contextualizing the role of network and update rules in cooperation, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment or sanctions.

- source: Battu, B., & Rahwan, T. (2023). Cooperation without punishment. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods games, cooperation cannot be sustained by conditional cooperators alone, nor by simply injecting altruists if there is no social noise. However, when a moderate level of noise is present, a small but critical fraction of altruists can trigger and sustain high levels of cooperation among conditional cooperators. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and instead focuses on the role of positive reciprocity and social learning. The main outcome is the level of cooperation (contribution rate), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism in its model. Its findings are relevant for understanding alternative mechanisms to punishment for sustaining cooperation, but do not inform the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. The paper does, however, argue that punishment can reduce social welfare, but this is a theoretical and literature-based claim, not a result of the presented model. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as providing context for non-punishment mechanisms, but not as a source of quantitative or comparative evidence on punishment's effect on efficiency.

- source: Wu, Z. H., Huang, H., & Liao, Q. H. (2021). The study on the role of dedicators on promoting cooperation in public goods game. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The presence of dedicators in a PGG model can strongly promote and stabilize cooperation, leading to higher total contributions. Dedicators' willingness to donate is more important than the amount they donate. Emotional effects—whereby others are inspired to cooperate by dedicator actions—further enhance cooperation. The system is robust to several parameter changes, but a too-high budget requirement can collapse cooperation. No punishment or sanctioning is modeled or analyzed. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in PGG-like environments, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome. It is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. However, it does provide indirect evidence that mechanisms other than punishment (such as the presence of prosocial 'dedicators' and emotional contagion) can promote cooperation and higher contributions. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant, as it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency outcomes in the required sense.

- source: Kim, J., Chae, H., Yook, S. H., & Kim, Y. (2015). Spatial evolutionary public goods game on complete graph and dense complex networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The SEPGG with voluntary participation exhibits distinct stationary states depending on the multiplication factor (r), group size (N), and network density (mean degree). On complete graphs (all-to-all), either defectors or loners dominate depending on r, with no stable cooperator-dominant state. On sparse networks, cooperation can dominate for sufficiently low mean degree. The paper provides scaling laws for the transitions between these states. However, the study does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff; it focuses on the prevalence of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how group size and network structure affect cooperation and opting out in voluntary participation PGGs, but not for the effect of punishment. The results may be useful as a baseline for the behavior of voluntary participation PGGs without punishment, but cannot inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Nishi, A., Christakis, N. A., & Rand, D. G. (2017). Cooperation, decision time, and culture: Online experiments with American and Indian participants. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that American participants are more likely to cooperate than Indian participants in a repeated public goods game, both in the first round and in subsequent rounds, regardless of the social environment. Decision time for cooperation versus defection varies by culture: Americans decide faster when cooperating (except in non-cooperative environments), while Indians decide faster when defecting. The experiment does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any related payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or efficiency outcomes are present. It does, however, provide contextual evidence about baseline cooperation rates in repeated public goods games with different cultural groups, which may be useful for understanding baseline control conditions in similar designs. However, it cannot inform the effect of enabling punishment or any moderators of that effect.

- source: Heine, F., & Strobel, M. (2020). Reward and punishment in a team contest. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares reward and punishment mechanisms in a team contest game (with and without between-group competition). Groups of four play 15 rounds, deciding how much to contribute to a group lottery (contest) and then can reward or punish teammates at a cost. The main findings are: (1) Both punishment and reward mechanisms lead to over-contribution relative to Nash equilibrium, causing significant efficiency losses (rent dissipation). (2) Punishment does not increase efficiency; it reduces it, as punishing is costly and does not significantly increase contributions. (3) Rewarding increases contributions more than punishment, but is welfare-neutral (cost to giver equals benefit to receiver). (4) The contest environment (with an opponent group) leads to even greater inefficiency and lower average payoffs than the non-contest environment. (5) The R&P treatment (both reward and punishment available) in the contest environment is the least efficient, with the highest overspending and lowest mean income. (6) Receiving reward increases subsequent contributions, but punishment does not. (7) Overall, the presence of punishment does not solve the efficiency problem in this environment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a team contest environment with public-good-like features, enabling punishment does not improve efficiency and may worsen it, especially when both reward and punishment are available. The effect of punishment is less positive (more negative) on efficiency compared to reward or baseline. The contest structure (between-group competition) amplifies inefficiency. For prediction, if the control game (no punishment) is already inefficient due to over-contribution, enabling punishment is likely to further reduce efficiency, not improve it. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contribution, known MPCR, punishment cost and tech, no identification of punishers, no communication) are all specified and match standard lab contest/PGG designs. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled in similar environments.

- source: Bravo, G., & Squazzoni, F. (2013). Exit, Punishment and Rewards in Commons Dilemmas: An Experimental Study. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a modified public goods game with six players per group and ten rounds per treatment, the introduction of punishment (a 5 MU fine for non-cooperation) significantly increased cooperation and efficiency compared to the control (no incentives). However, the highest efficiency (profit as a proportion of the theoretical optimum) was achieved when both voluntary participation (exit option) and positive incentives (5 MU reward for cooperation) were present. Punishment alone (No-Neg) led to 67% of the optimum, while reward plus exit (Ex-Pos) led to 69%. The control (No-Null) and punishment plus exit (Ex-Neg) both yielded 58%. Thus, while punishment increases efficiency over the control, it is less effective than the reward-plus-exit combination. The paper also finds that exit alone has little effect, and that the interaction between exit and incentives is important for maximizing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a repeated public goods game with six players, ten rounds, no communication, and fixed institutional punishment (5 MU fine per defection), enabling punishment increases efficiency relative to the control (no punishment), but the efficiency gain is smaller than that achieved by combining rewards and voluntary participation. The efficiency effect of punishment is positive but not maximal. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contribution, fixed MPCR, fixed punishment cost/tech, no reward in punishment-only treatments, no identification of punishers, and full information about group outcomes) are all specified, making this paper highly informative for predicting efficiency under similar conditions.

- source: van Miltenburg, N., Przepiorka, W., & Buskens, V. (2017). Consensual punishment does not promote cooperation in the six-person prisoner's dilemma game with noisy public monitoring. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three punishment institutions (individual decision rule, CDR2, CDR3) in a six-person prisoner's dilemma, with and without 20% noise in public monitoring of contributions. In the absence of noise, all punishment institutions increase cooperation and earnings relative to the baseline, with collective decision rules (especially CDR3) performing best. With noise, however, all punishment institutions result in lower earnings than the baseline (no-punishment) condition. The individual decision rule (IDR) produces slightly higher cooperation than collective rules under noise, but still does not improve earnings. The main reason is that noise leads to both mistaken punishment of cooperators and missed punishment of defectors, so resources are wasted and cooperation is not effectively enforced. Thus, punishment does not promote efficiency in noisy environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a six-person all-or-nothing prisoner's dilemma with 20% noise in public monitoring, enabling punishment (regardless of whether it is individual or requires consensus) reduces group efficiency compared to the control (no-punishment) condition. The effect is robust across different punishment decision rules. The findings suggest that, when noise is present, the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be lower than the control efficiency. The results are directly relevant for settings with similar group size, binary contribution, and noisy monitoring, and less so for linear PGGs or settings with perfect information.

- source: Lohse, J., & Waichman, I. (2020). The effects of contemporaneous peer punishment on cooperation with the future. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a multigenerational public goods game, voluntary cooperation is difficult to sustain across generations. When peer punishment is available within a generation, the likelihood that the group meets the contribution threshold and sustains future generations more than doubles compared to the baseline. However, the increase in group earnings from more sustained generations is offset by the costs incurred through punishment, resulting in no significant difference in average aggregate earnings (efficiency) between the punishment and no-punishment conditions. The effect of punishment on individual contribution levels is small, but it is sufficient to increase the rate at which groups meet the threshold. Punishment is mainly directed at those who contribute less than their peers, especially when the group fails to reach the threshold.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling peer punishment in a threshold-based, multigenerational public goods game increases the probability of group success (i.e., reaching future generations and thus group-level payoff), but the efficiency gains are offset by the costs of punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of peer punishment can increase the likelihood of group success, but unless punishment is low-cost or highly effective, the net effect on efficiency may be limited or null. The design dimensions—such as group size, threshold, punishment cost and effectiveness, and the absence of communication—are all well-specified and should be used to inform predictions. The results are most relevant for settings with threshold public goods, no communication, and contemporaneous peer punishment.

- source: Janssen, M. A., Holahan, R., Lee, A., & Ostrom, E. (2010). Lab Experiments for the Study of Social-Ecological Systems. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a dynamic, spatially explicit CPR lab experiment with five players per group, costly punishment is used by participants but does not increase group payoffs or efficiency unless combined with communication. When punishment is available without communication, net group earnings decrease due to the cost of punishment, and resource depletion is not prevented. Communication alone leads to higher group payoffs and more sustainable resource use. When both communication and punishment are available, the positive effects of communication are not enhanced, and the use of punishment can undermine cooperative agreements once communication is removed. The study highlights that in this more complex, dynamic environment, punishment without communication is not an effective tool for increasing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in dynamic, spatially explicit CPR games with real-time harvesting and five players, enabling costly punishment without communication is likely to reduce efficiency relative to the control (no punishment, no communication) due to the direct costs of punishment. Communication is a much more effective intervention for increasing efficiency. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled (but no communication) should be lower than the control, and that the presence or absence of communication is a critical moderator. The punishment mechanism here (cost of 1 token to remove 2 tokens from another) is not sufficient to offset the cost in terms of group payoff. These results are most applicable to dynamic, spatially explicit CPR games rather than standard static PGGs, but the payoff-based outcome is directly relevant.

- source: DeCaro, D. A., DeCaro, M. S., Janssen, M. A., Lee, A., Graci, A., & Flener, D. (2024). Learning from regulatory failure: How Ostrom's restorative justice design principle helps naïve groups create wiser enforcement systems to overcome the tragedy of the commons. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Facilitated groups (those guided to discuss enforcement using restorative justice principles) achieved higher group efficiency (net tokens harvested) than unfacilitated groups. The highest efficiency was observed in groups that first experienced regulatory abuse (punishment without communication) and then received facilitation (PFN treatment). Groups that experienced regulatory failure but did not receive facilitation often failed to develop effective enforcement systems and had lower efficiency. The presence of coordinated punishment systems (as opposed to independent or no punishment) was associated with the highest group efficiency. The study demonstrates that both the structure of punishment opportunities and the process of group learning and facilitation are critical moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on group efficiency in public-goods-like games depends not only on the presence of punishment but also on the context in which it is introduced (e.g., prior experience with regulatory failure/abuse) and whether groups receive facilitation to discuss enforcement principles. In prediction tasks, the presence of facilitated discussion (restorative justice), the opportunity for communication, and the structure of the punishment system (cost, effectiveness, coordination) should be considered as moderators. The control efficiency (without punishment) is lower than the treatment efficiency (with punishment and facilitation), especially when groups experience regulatory abuse and are then guided to reflect and coordinate. The findings suggest that punishment can increase efficiency, but only under certain institutional and learning conditions.

- source: Koike, S., Nakamaru, M., Otaka, T., Shimao, H., Shimomura, K. I., & Yamato, T. (2018). Reciprocity and exclusion in informal financial institutions: An experimental study of rotating savings and credit associations. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compared four treatments in a fixed-order ROSCA game: baseline (no sanctions), punishment rule (forfeiture), voting (exclusion), and both punishment and voting. Exclusion by voting led to significantly higher contribution rates and mean payoffs than the other treatments. The punishment rule, which prevented non-contributors from receiving payouts, did not increase contributions or payoffs relative to the baseline. The combination of voting and punishment was less effective than voting alone. The results suggest that social exclusion based on reputation is a more effective mechanism for sustaining cooperation and improving group efficiency than the explicit punishment rule tested here.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, this paper provides strong evidence that a costless, rule-based punishment (forfeiture of payout for non-contributors) does not improve efficiency or mean payoff compared to a baseline with no punishment. In contrast, exclusion by peer voting (reputation-based exclusion) substantially increases both contributions and efficiency. The game structure is a close variant of a public goods game (ROSCA), with fixed group size, known rounds, and full information about others' actions. The findings suggest that not all punishment mechanisms are equally effective: exclusion based on group voting is effective, but a non-costly, rule-based punishment is not. This distinction is important for predicting the effect of punishment in similar environments.

- source: Sigmund, K., Hauert, C., & Nowak, M. A. (2001). Reward and punishment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in two-player public goods minigames with punishment or reward, evolutionary dynamics without reputation lead to asocial equilibria with low efficiency, regardless of the presence of punishment or reward options. When a small chance of reputation is introduced (players can sometimes observe others' punishment/reward behavior), the system can support a stable, high-efficiency equilibrium with cooperation and punishment. This effect is stronger and more robust for punishment than for reward. The model predicts that punishment with reputation is much more effective at promoting group efficiency than reward with reputation, and that without reputation, neither mechanism is effective in the long run.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment alone does not increase efficiency in public goods games unless players have some information about others' punishment behavior (i.e., reputation). In the absence of reputation, enabling punishment does not improve efficiency; the system converges to defection and non-punishment. With even a small probability of reputation, enabling punishment can create a bistable system where high-efficiency outcomes are possible and can be risk-dominant. The effect is parameter-dependent (e.g., punishment cost, effectiveness, benefit/cost of cooperation), but the qualitative result is robust. For prediction, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the presence of reputation or information about others' actions. If the game design does not allow for reputation, enabling punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency. If reputation is present, punishment can have a strong positive effect on efficiency, more so than reward.

- source: Ozono, H., Jin, N., Watabe, M., & Shimizu, K. (2016). Solving the second-order free rider problem in a public goods game: An experiment using a leader support system. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when leaders are given the ability to punish followers using resources provided by follower support (support-present condition), both the frequency and effectiveness of punishment increase compared to a no-support condition. This leads to a higher probability of groups achieving high cooperation (as measured by contribution rates) and higher average profits for both leaders and followers, especially under 'linkage' punishment leaders who punish both non-cooperators and non-supporters. The no-support condition, where leaders have externally provided punishment resources, results in weaker punishment and lower group cooperation. The findings suggest that the structure of punishment funding and the type of leader punishment strategy are critical moderators of the efficiency gains from punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling leaders to punish using resources provided by group members (rather than external resources) increases the efficiency of punishment in public goods games, as measured by group and individual profits and the frequency of high-cooperation groups. The effect is especially pronounced when leaders punish both first- and second-order free riders. For prediction tasks, the presence of a leader support system, the type of punishment strategy, and the source of punishment capital are key moderators. The control (no-support) condition provides a baseline for efficiency, and the support-present condition demonstrates a substantial positive shift in efficiency when punishment is enabled and properly incentivized.

- source: Jiang, L. L., Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2013). If Cooperation Is Likely Punish Mildly: Insights from Economic Experiments Based on the Snowdrift Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This study uses repeated group snowdrift games (a close variant of public goods games) with and without punishment to test the effects of mild versus severe punishment on cooperation and average payoff. In groups of five, with 25 rounds per session, the authors manipulate the cost-to-benefit ratio (r = 0.2 or 0.8) and the punishment fine (p = 2.0 or 4.0, with constant cost to punisher). When cooperation is likely (r = 0.2), both mild and severe punishment increase cooperation, but severe punishment does not further increase cooperation and reduces average payoff compared to mild punishment. When cooperation is unlikely (r = 0.8), only severe punishment substantially increases cooperation and average payoff; mild punishment is less effective and can even reduce average payoff compared to no punishment. Thus, the efficiency effect of punishment is context-dependent: mild punishment is optimal when cooperation is already likely, while severe punishment is only justified in highly defection-prone environments.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the underlying difficulty of cooperation (cost-to-benefit ratio, analogous to MPCR in PGGs). If the control game (no punishment) already achieves high efficiency, adding severe punishment will not improve and may reduce efficiency; mild punishment is sufficient. If the control game has low efficiency (cooperation is rare), only severe punishment can increase efficiency. The cost and effectiveness of punishment, as well as the baseline cooperation rate, are critical moderators. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions and control efficiency in snowdrift- or PGG-like games with peer punishment.

- source: Gross, J., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2019). Individual solutions to shared problems create a modern tragedy of the commons. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can choose between solving a shared problem collectively (public good) or individually (private solution), many opt for the individual solution even when collective action would be more efficient. This leads to inefficient resource allocation and coordination failure, especially at intermediate cost-benefit ratios. When peer punishment is introduced, it increases the rate of collective solutions but also triggers mutual punishment between individualists and collectivists, which reduces overall group welfare. Punishment does not reduce free riding but instead is used in feuds between incompatible strategies, amplifying inefficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that in public-goods-like games where individual solutions are available, enabling peer punishment can reduce efficiency (group welfare), even if it increases collective action. The effect is driven by wasteful punishment exchanges rather than improved cooperation. For prediction, if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is known, enabling punishment is likely to decrease efficiency in similar settings, especially when individual solutions are a viable alternative to collective action. The findings are most relevant when the game allows both individual and collective solutions, and punishment is peer-based, costly, and has a strong impact.

- source: Garrido, C. M., Santos, F. C., Domingos, E. F., Nunes, A. M., & Pacheco, J. M. (2025). A new electoral bottom-up model of institutional governance. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a collective risk dilemma (CRD) modeled as a threshold public goods game, the introduction of local, bottom-up institutions—created when a minimum number of group members contribute to their formation, and whose nature (reward or punishment) is decided by majority vote—substantially increases group efficiency (η_G) compared to no institution, reward-only, or punishment-only institutions. Reward-only institutions are generally more efficient than punishment-only, but the electoral (mixed) institution is superior to both, especially at lower risk levels. The model shows that the critical risk threshold for achieving a given level of group efficiency is lower under the electoral institution, and the transition from defection to cooperation is steeper. The model is theoretical and does not use empirical or experimental data, but provides direct predictions for how institutional design affects efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in threshold public goods games with risk of group failure, the presence and design of institutional punishment and/or reward mechanisms have large effects on group efficiency. Specifically, enabling local, flexible institutions that can implement either punishment or reward (as decided by group vote) leads to higher efficiency than fixed punishment-only or reward-only institutions. The model quantifies these effects using the η_G metric, which is directly analogous to efficiency. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the presence of a flexible, locally decided institution (with both punishment and reward possible) should be expected to yield higher efficiency than either punishment-only or reward-only, and much higher than no institution. The model also provides parameterizations for group size, number of rounds, risk, and institutional costs, which can inform predictions when these dimensions are known.

- source: Gavrilets, S., & Richerson, P. J. (2017). Collective action and the evolution of social norm internalization. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
