  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper uses agent-based simulations to show that norm internalization evolves more readily and has larger effects on cooperation when peer punishment of free riders is promoted. In public-goods-like ('us-vs.-nature') games, the evolution of norm internalization and punishment increases cooperation and material payoffs (efficiency). In intergroup contest ('us-vs.-them') games, norm internalization and punishment increase cooperation but reduce material payoffs due to overproduction and rent dissipation. The effect of punishment on efficiency is therefore positive in public-goods-like settings and negative in intergroup contest settings. The model also predicts that group size, punishment cost, and the strength of social norms moderate these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends on the type of collective action problem. In standard public goods (us-vs.-nature) games, enabling punishment increases efficiency by reducing free riding. In intergroup contest (us-vs.-them) games, enabling punishment can reduce efficiency due to excessive effort and resource dissipation. The model also suggests that group size and punishment cost are important moderators. For prediction, the context of the collective action (public goods vs. contest), group size, and punishment parameters should be considered.

- source: Góis, A. R., Santos, F. P., Pacheco, J. M., & Santos, F. C. (2019). Reward and punishment in climate change dilemmas. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in collective-risk dilemmas (threshold public goods games with risk of group failure), both reward and punishment policies improve group achievement (η_G) compared to no policy. Reward is particularly effective at initiating cooperation when the risk of failure is low, while punishment is more effective at sustaining high levels of cooperation, especially when the risk is high or the threshold for group success is stringent. The optimal policy is to use reward when the fraction of cooperators is low and switch to punishment when cooperation is sufficiently high. The model provides explicit formulas for the optimal switching point and shows that combining both policies synergistically yields the highest group achievement. The results are robust across a range of parameter values, including group size, population size, and policy efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment (and/or reward) in threshold public goods games will increase group achievement (a proxy for efficiency) compared to a control without such mechanisms. The model allows for direct mapping of game design dimensions such as player count, group size, number of rounds (in evolutionary time), presence and efficiency of punishment and reward, and the structure of the public goods dilemma (threshold, risk). The findings suggest that punishment is most effective at maintaining cooperation once it is established, while reward is more important for initiating cooperation. The optimal policy is a dynamic combination of both. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that adding punishment (especially with sufficient budget and efficiency) will generally increase efficiency, but the effect size and optimality depend on the risk, threshold, and whether reward is also available. The model is theoretical, so empirical calibration may be needed for quantitative predictions.

- source: Galan, J. M., Latek, M. M., & Rizi, S. M. M. (2011). Axelrod's Metanorm Games on Networks. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the ability of punishment and metapunishment to sustain cooperation in a public-goods-like environment is highly sensitive to the underlying network structure. Specifically, higher clustering and interconnectedness (more triplets per agent) make it more likely that the population will spend most of its time in a cooperative, norm-enforcing state, which is associated with higher expected payoffs. In contrast, higher average degree (more neighbors per agent) can make norm collapse (defection, low punishment) more likely, reducing group welfare. The results are derived from both mathematical analysis of expected payoffs and extensive agent-based simulations, but the main simulation outcomes are behavioral (frequency of norm establishment/collapse) rather than explicit efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, especially when considering the role of network structure. It suggests that punishment (and metapunishment) can lead to higher efficiency (more time in cooperative states) when the network has high clustering and interconnectedness, but may be less effective or even counterproductive in networks with high average degree and low clustering. However, because the paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff values, its support for quantitative prediction is limited to qualitative or relative effects based on network features. The findings are most relevant when the game design includes networked interactions, costly punishment, and possibly metapunishment.

- source: Gao, L., Wang, Z., Pansini, R., Li, Y. T., & Wang, R. W. (2015). Collective punishment is more effective than collective reward for promoting cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that collective punishment is more effective than collective reward in promoting and stabilizing cooperation in multiplayer snowdrift games, especially for small groups or high initial cooperation. When the punishment-to-benefit ratio exceeds a threshold, a stable state of full cooperation emerges for both small and large groups, while such a state does not appear for large groups with only reward. The main outcome is the frequency of cooperation, which is closely related to group payoff but not a direct measure of efficiency. The results suggest that, in environments where individual-level discrimination is not possible, collective punishment can maintain high cooperation and thus higher group fitness or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in multiplayer public-goods-like environments (specifically, snowdrift games), enabling collective punishment (even when it cannot be targeted at individuals) can substantially increase the frequency and stability of cooperation, especially in small groups or when the punishment intensity is high. This implies that, for prediction tasks, the presence and strength of collective punishment should be expected to increase efficiency or group payoff, particularly in small groups. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions of efficiency as defined by total group payoff relative to full cooperation, so the findings should be interpreted as indicating a strong positive effect on efficiency-related outcomes via increased cooperation rates and stable cooperative equilibria.

- source: Sun, W. W., Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., Szolnoki, A., & Vasconcelos, V. V. (2021). Combination of institutional incentives for cooperative governance of risky commons. *Iscience*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in collective-risk social dilemma games, institutional pure reward strategies (where executors only reward cooperators) are more effective than pure punishment or hybrid strategies in promoting group achievement (i.e., the fraction of groups that reach the collective target). This result holds for both fixed and flexible incentive schemes, and is robust to changes in risk level, mutation rate, and enhancement factor. Local sanctioning schemes are more effective than global ones. The main outcome is group achievement, not efficiency or total group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency effects is not possible from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. It suggests that, in collective-risk social dilemmas, institutional punishment alone is less effective than reward or hybrid schemes in promoting group achievement (a proxy for cooperation). However, because the main outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, but rather the fraction of groups reaching a behavioral target, the findings should be used with caution for efficiency prediction. The game structure (collective-risk, institutional incentives, evolutionary dynamics) is close but not identical to standard PGGs. The results suggest that institutional punishment may not increase efficiency as much as reward, but do not provide quantitative efficiency estimates.

- source: Lee, J. H., Iwasa, Y., Dieckmann, U., & Sigmund, K. (2019). Social evolution leads to persistent corruption. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This theoretical study shows that while institutional punishment can, in principle, sustain cooperation and high payoffs in public-goods-like settings, the possibility of corruption (i.e., punishment institutions accepting bribes) leads to cycles or stable states of reduced cooperation and lower average payoffs. The model demonstrates that transparency and monitoring are necessary to maintain honest punishment, but these are costly and thus not always sustained. As a result, the efficiency gains from punishment are not robust: corruption can cause efficiency to fall well below the cooperative optimum, and the system may oscillate between periods of high and low efficiency depending on the prevalence of corruption and monitoring.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a cautionary theoretical perspective: enabling punishment in a public-goods-like game does not guarantee increased efficiency if the punishment institution can be corrupted. The efficiency benefit of punishment depends critically on the integrity of the institution and the cost and prevalence of monitoring. In prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on whether corruption is possible and how transparency is maintained. If the game design allows for institutional corruption or lacks mechanisms for transparency and monitoring, enabling punishment may not improve—and may even reduce—efficiency relative to the control. The model also highlights the importance of considering dynamic and cyclical effects, not just static outcomes.

- source: Mussweiler, T., & Ockenfels, A. (2013). Similarity increases altruistic punishment in humans. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that perceived similarity (induced by a cognitive priming task) increases the willingness to engage in costly punishment of uncooperative behavior in a two-person cooperation game with a punishment phase. In contrast, group identity (in-group vs. out-group) reduces altruistic punishment toward in-group members. The effect of similarity is not explained by group identity, and the two have opposite effects on punishment. The study does not directly measure or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the cooperation phase is single-shot, so there is no opportunity for punishment to feed back into increased cooperation or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that psychological framing (similarity focus vs. group identity) can significantly alter the frequency and intensity of costly punishment in a cooperation game with a punishment phase. However, it does not provide direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate or report most of the structural game design dimensions relevant for predicting efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding psychological moderators of punishment behavior, rather than for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design parameters. Thus, its direct value for predicting treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game design is limited.

- source: Diekmann, A., & Przepiorka, W. (2015). Punitive preferences, monetary incentives and tacit coordination in the punishment of defectors promote cooperation in humans. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when only one group member is needed to punish a defector (step-level public good), groups with punishment cost heterogeneity (one strong, low-cost punisher) achieve higher rates of punishment and lower rates of defection than symmetric groups. Tacit coordination on the strong punisher leads to more efficient punishment (i.e., punishment is more often carried out by only one group member). The presence of a penalty for defectors (i.e., punishment that also harms the defector) is necessary to reduce defection rates. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the behavioral results suggest that punishment cost heterogeneity can improve the effectiveness and efficiency of norm enforcement in such games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment cost heterogeneity (i.e., having a designated or focal punisher with lower costs) increases the effectiveness and efficiency of punishment in step-level public good games, as measured by behavioral outcomes (punishment and defection rates). While it does not report group efficiency or total payoff, the findings suggest that game designs enabling tacit coordination on a single, low-cost punisher are likely to yield higher efficiency when punishment is enabled, especially when a penalty for defectors is present. The results are most directly relevant for step-level or volunteer's dilemma variants of public goods games, rather than standard linear PGGs. The absence of explicit efficiency or payoff data limits direct quantitative prediction, but the qualitative pattern is clear: punishment is more effective and less wasteful when cost heterogeneity allows for coordination on a single punisher.

- source: Hilbe, C., & Traulsen, A. (2012). Emergence of responsible sanctions without second order free riders, antisocial punishment or spite. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that responsible punishment and cooperation can co-evolve and become stable in populations when interactions are non-anonymous and reputation can be built. In anonymous settings, cooperation and responsible punishment do not emerge, and antisocial punishment can persist. The model provides a threshold for the amount of information (reputation) needed for responsible punishment to be adaptive. The results are robust to errors in reputation and extend to public goods games with more than two players, though larger group sizes raise the threshold for reputation needed. The paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes, but the emergence of cooperation and responsible punishment implies higher group welfare than in the absence of these behaviors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games can increase cooperation and, by implication, efficiency, but only when reputation or non-anonymity is present. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is strongly moderated by whether players can observe each other's actions and reputations. In anonymous settings, punishment does not promote cooperation or efficiency. The model also suggests that larger group sizes make it harder for punishment to be effective. However, because the paper does not report explicit efficiency or payoff outcomes, its value for direct quantitative prediction is limited; it is most useful for understanding the qualitative role of reputation, anonymity, and group size as moderators.

- source: Korman, A., & Vacus, R. (2021). On the role of hypocrisy in escaping the tragedy of the commons. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study shows that in large networked public goods games with negligible MPCR, cooperation can emerge from a population of mostly defectors if mild but appropriately calibrated punishment (social-pressure) is applied, especially when an intermediate behavioral type (hypocrisy) is present. The emergence of cooperation is highly sensitive to the level of punishment against hypocritical players: too little or too much punishment leads to persistent defection or hypocrisy, while an intermediate level enables a transition to widespread cooperation. The model is theoretical and supported by numerical simulations, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into the conditions under which punishment can promote cooperation in large public goods games with negligible MPCR. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation depends critically on the calibration of punishment levels, especially toward intermediate types like hypocrites. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in experimental or empirical settings. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of punishment calibration and the role of intermediate behavioral types in the dynamics of cooperation.

- source: Johnson, S. (2015). Escaping the tragedy of the commons through targeted punishment. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in large-N social dilemmas (modeled after public goods games), targeted punishment strategies—where only a small, strategically chosen subset of defectors are punished at a time—can enable a small number of punishers to shift a population from full defection to full cooperation. This is in contrast to standard punishment strategies, which require much higher punishment capacity to achieve the same result. The main outcome is the stationary proportion of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The model is robust to heterogeneity in player predispositions and to some randomness in the ordering of players. The findings are relevant for understanding how to design punishment institutions or strategies to escape the tragedy of the commons, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that the structure and targeting of punishment can strongly affect the ability to achieve cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially when initial cooperation is low. However, because the model's outcome is the proportion of cooperators rather than efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes when punishment is enabled. The results suggest that, all else equal, targeted punishment strategies may be more effective at increasing cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) than uniform punishment, but the lack of direct payoff analysis limits quantitative prediction. The model assumes binary (all-or-nothing) contribution choices, no explicit payoff or MPCR parameters, and does not model communication, reward, or information features. Thus, its main value is in highlighting the importance of punishment targeting and heterogeneity, rather than providing parameter-specific efficiency predictions.

- source: Sarkar, S. (2023). Managing ecological thresholds of a risky commons. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops a coupled replicator-resource model with inspection and punishment, showing that punishment and monitoring can avert the tragedy of the commons (TOC) and sustain cooperation and resource abundance, but only under certain conditions. At low resource growth rates, even full cooperation (driven by punishment) cannot prevent resource collapse. At moderate growth rates, increasing punishment and monitoring can shift the system from oscillatory TOC to sustainable coexistence. At high growth rates, the resource persists even with high defection, but cooperation is harder to sustain. The model highlights the importance of ecological parameters (growth rate, predation) in moderating the effectiveness of punishment and monitoring. However, the paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes in the sense of total group earnings or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation and resource abundance depends on ecological parameters (especially resource growth rate and predation) as well as institutional parameters (punishment severity and monitoring probability). However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. It is most useful for understanding the conditions under which punishment can prevent resource collapse or sustain cooperation in public-goods-like environments with ecological feedbacks. The model structure and qualitative findings may inform priors about when punishment is likely to be effective, but quantitative efficiency predictions are not possible from this paper.

- source: Chierchia, G., Lesemann, F. H. P., Snower, D., & Singer, T. (2021). Cooperation across multiple game theoretical paradigms is increased by fear more than anger in selfish individuals. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing
  findings: Induced fear, compared to anger, increases cooperation (as measured by a latent factor across multiple games, including the public good game) in individuals with selfish value orientations, bringing their cooperation levels up to those of prosocial individuals. No significant effect of emotion induction is found on punishment behavior. The effect is robust to controls for mood, risk attitudes, and other confounds. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that emotional context (fear vs. anger) can moderate cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially among selfish individuals. However, because the outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, punishment) rather than efficiency or payoff, the findings are only indirectly relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a treatment (i.e., enabling/disabling punishment), but rather includes punishment games as part of a behavioral factor. Thus, while the paper supports the idea that context and individual differences can moderate cooperation and punishment, it does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Seip, E. C., van Dijk, W. W., & Rotteveel, M. (2009). On Hotheads and Dirty Harries The Primacy of Anger in Altruistic Punishment. *Values, Empathy, and Fairness Across Social Barriers*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that anger, rather than the perception of unfairness, is the primary driver of altruistic punishment in social dilemma games. Experimental evidence from a one-shot, three-person public goods game with punishment shows that lower contributions from others evoke more anger and lead to harsher punishment, and that the effect of others' contributions on punishment is fully mediated by experienced anger. Priming participants with anger increases their tendency to punish defectors. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. However, it does suggest that the presence and intensity of punishment are strongly influenced by emotional responses (especially anger) to defection or low contribution. For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment is likely to be used when players experience anger at norm violations, but the paper does not address whether this increases or decreases group efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding when and why punishment is deployed, not its payoff consequences.

- source: Fehr, E., & Fischbacher, U. (2003). The nature of human altruism. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that altruistic punishment (costly punishment of norm violators) is a robust phenomenon in laboratory experiments, including public goods games and third-party punishment games. It reviews evidence that, in the absence of punishment, cooperation in public goods games typically decays over repeated rounds, but that the opportunity for punishment can sustain higher levels of cooperation. The paper also discusses the limits of altruism, noting that higher costs of punishment or contribution reduce the frequency of these behaviors. While the paper provides strong theoretical and empirical support for the importance of punishment in sustaining cooperation, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff effects from punishment interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that enabling punishment in public goods games and similar environments can sustain higher cooperation and deter free-riding, especially in repeated or group settings. However, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it systematically analyze how game design dimensions (such as player count, MPCR, punishment cost, etc.) moderate the effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is most useful for contextualizing the likely direction of punishment effects (i.e., more positive for cooperation and, by implication, possibly for efficiency), but it does not provide parameter estimates or direct outcome data for model training or calibration.

- source: García, J., & Traulsen, A. (2025). Picking strategies in games of cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of the strategy set and the microeconomic assumptions in evolutionary models of cooperation (including public goods games with punishment) can have a large impact on the predicted prevalence of cooperation. For example, allowing for antisocial punishment or for loners to be punished can lead to the collapse of cooperation, while more restrictive strategy sets can support high levels of cooperation. The paper emphasizes that these modeling choices can bias results and that more systematic, unbiased inclusion of strategies is needed for robust predictions. However, the paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on the abundance of cooperation and the evolutionary stability of different strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important theoretical caution for interpreting the effects of punishment in public goods games: the predicted effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, efficiency) can depend strongly on the details of the strategy set and microeconomic assumptions. If a model or experiment restricts who can punish or be punished, or omits antisocial punishment, it may overestimate the positive effect of punishment on cooperation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it does not provide direct quantitative support for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of modeling assumptions and the potential for qualitative shifts in cooperation prevalence, which may translate to efficiency in some settings.

- source: Tsvetkova, M., Wagner, C., & Mao, A. (2018). The emergence of inequality in social groups: Network structure and institutions affect the distribution of earnings in cooperation games. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that enabling punishment in networked cooperation games (including PGG-like games) increases inequality in the distribution of final wealth, as measured by the Gini coefficient. This is because the cost of punishment is disproportionately borne by poorer players, who become even poorer as a result. However, the paper does not find a systematic relationship between the presence of punishment and group average wealth (i.e., efficiency or total payoff). Thus, while punishment increases cooperation and inequality, it does not reliably increase or decrease group efficiency. The main focus is on the distributional consequences (inequality), not on efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in PGG-like networked cooperation games increases inequality in final wealth, but does not provide evidence that punishment systematically increases or decreases group efficiency (total payoff relative to full cooperation). For the downstream prediction task, this means that the presence of punishment should not be assumed to improve or worsen efficiency based on this evidence alone. The main effect is on the distribution of payoffs, not their sum. If the prediction task is about efficiency, this paper suggests that punishment's effect is likely neutral or context-dependent, and that other design dimensions (e.g., network structure, fluidity) may matter more for efficiency than for inequality.

- source: Chierchia, G., Lesemann, F. H. P., Snower, D., Vogel, M., & Singer, T. (2017). Caring Cooperators and Powerful Punishers: Differential Effects of Induced Care and Power Motivation on Different Types of Economic Decision Making. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: Inducing a Care motive increases a latent cooperation factor (which includes public goods contributions), while Power induction increases a punishment factor (punishment in 2nd/3rd party punishment games, ultimatum, impunity). The two factors are largely orthogonal. Care increases cooperation relative to control, but does not increase punishment. Power increases punishment relative to Care, but not relative to control. No direct efficiency or payoff outcomes are reported, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that punishment and cooperation are distinct behavioral tendencies, and that motivational context (Care vs. Power) can differentially affect them. However, it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does suggest that punishment behavior can be increased by Power motives, but this is not linked to group payoff or efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by clarifying that punishment and cooperation are separable and can be independently manipulated.

- source: Itao, K., & Kaneko, K. (2025). Self-organized institutions in evolutionary dynamical-systems games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a minimal two-player harvesting game with environmental feedback, the evolution of decision-making functions leads to the self-organization of social institutions (shared norms and punishment rules). Players evolve to monitor both their own and their opponent's states, and punish selfish overharvesting by retaliatory overharvesting, which degrades the environment and reduces both players' payoffs. This punitive mechanism stabilizes cooperation and leads to the emergence of efficient, periodic harvesting patterns (modes) that maximize group fitness. The model shows that the most efficient (highest fitness) cooperative modes are only evolutionarily stable if players' strategies are sufficiently plastic to punish defectors. The results are robust across a range of parameter values. The paper provides a formal framework for understanding how punishment and norm evolution can sustain high efficiency in common-pool resource settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment (here, context-dependent punitive overharvesting) in a repeated common-pool resource game will increase group efficiency, provided that players can monitor each other's states or actions. The model shows that the emergence of punishment mechanisms is necessary for the stability of efficient cooperative outcomes, and that the efficiency gains are robust to parameter changes. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, binary-action resource games with environmental feedback, but the underlying principles are likely to generalize to other public goods or common-pool resource games with similar structure. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the presence of punishment, monitoring, and plasticity in strategies are key moderators of efficiency gains from punishment.

- source: Szolnoki, A., Mobilia, M., Jiang, L. L., Szczesny, B., Rucklidge, A. M., & Perc, M. (2014). Cyclic dominance in evolutionary games: a review. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The review highlights that cyclic dominance can emerge in evolutionary games with three or more strategies, including PGG variants with volunteering, punishment, and reward. In structured populations, spatial structure and mobility play crucial roles in maintaining coexistence and generating complex patterns (e.g., spirals, waves). Punishment and reward can stabilize cooperation through cyclic dominance, but the outcomes are typically described in terms of coexistence and oscillatory dynamics rather than efficiency or group payoff. The review also notes that alliances and subsystem solutions can act as effective strategies, further complicating the dynamics. Theoretical and simulation results are emphasized, with limited direct reference to empirical payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and qualitative evidence that the introduction of punishment (and reward) in PGG-like environments can promote the coexistence of cooperative strategies through cyclic dominance, especially in structured populations. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions or direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this review supports the idea that punishment can stabilize cooperation and prevent collapse to all-defection, but it does not inform the magnitude or direction of efficiency changes. The evidence is most useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators (e.g., spatial structure, mobility, network topology) rather than for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Chiba-Okabe, H., & Plotkin, J. B. (2024). Can institutions foster cooperation by wealth redistribution?. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) A prosocial institution (maximizing average population payoff) can increase cooperation and efficiency, but only if it is sufficiently forward-looking (low discount rate); if it is myopic, it fails to intervene and the outcome is the same as anarchy. (2) A pro-cooperator institution (maximizing cooperator payoff) always increases cooperation and efficiency above anarchy, even if it is myopic. (3) A selfish institution (maximizing its own revenue) can sometimes increase efficiency above anarchy if it is forward-looking, but if it is myopic, it exploits the population and reduces efficiency below anarchy. The model provides explicit analytical conditions for when each regime leads to higher or lower efficiency than the no-institution baseline.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of institutional interventions (taxation and redistribution) on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It shows that the effect of 'punishment' or institutional intervention is not universally positive or negative for efficiency; rather, it depends on the institution's objective and its ability to value future outcomes (discount rate). For prediction tasks, the model suggests that institutional efficiency (analogous to punishment/reward effectiveness), the institution's foresight, and the alignment of institutional objectives with group welfare are critical moderators. The results are most directly applicable to settings where the 'punishment' is implemented as a tax-and-redistribute mechanism, rather than direct costly punishment between individuals.

- source: Pal, S., & Hilbe, C. (2022). Reputation effects drive the joint evolution of cooperation and social rewarding. *Nature Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that reputation effects are crucial for the evolution of cooperation and social rewarding in PGG-like environments. When individuals can observe or infer others' rewarding strategies (i.e., reputation is possible), social rewarding and cooperation co-evolve and are stable, leading to higher group payoffs and efficiency. Without reputation, cooperation and rewarding do not emerge, and antisocial rewarding may arise under certain conditions (e.g., high assortment or mutually beneficial rewards). In multiplayer (PGG) settings, similar results hold: reputation enables stable cooperation and social rewarding, while its absence leads to defection and low efficiency. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation and rewarding are payoff-maximizing, and shows that the presence of reputation mechanisms is a key moderator of efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling reputation effects (e.g., making rewarding behavior observable) in PGG-like games will increase efficiency when social rewarding is possible. The model directly links game design dimensions such as group size, marginal per-capita return, reward cost and effectiveness, and information structure (reputation) to the emergence of efficient cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that the presence of observable rewarding (reputation), sufficiently high reward effectiveness relative to cost, and the ability to target rewards to cooperators are critical for efficiency gains from reward mechanisms. The absence of reputation or the presence of antisocial rewarding can undermine efficiency. While the paper does not model punishment directly, it provides indirect evidence that the structure and observability of incentives (including punishment, by analogy) are key moderators of efficiency effects.

- source: Gross, J., & Böhm, R. (2020). Voluntary restrictions on self-reliance increase cooperation and mitigate wealth inequality. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals can solve a collective action problem either individually (self-reliance) or collectively (public good), the availability of self-reliance crowds out cooperation and increases inequality, especially when self-reliance is relatively cheap. However, when groups are given the opportunity to vote to abolish the individual solution, they overwhelmingly do so under medium and high interdependence, which leads to higher rates of public goods provision, lower resource waste, higher group earnings, and reduced inequality. The effect is particularly strong when there is a risk of miscoordination (medium interdependence) or when group members differ in their ability to be self-reliant (wealth heterogeneity). The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms in the standard sense, but self-reliance by richer group members can function as a coercive device, pressuring poorer members to cooperate.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutional design features that restrict individual opt-out/self-reliance options can substantially increase efficiency and group earnings in public-goods-like environments, especially when the efficiency gains from cooperation are significant (i.e., when self-reliance is costly relative to the public good). While the study does not manipulate punishment, it shows that endogenous institutional restrictions (analogous to mandatory participation or exclusion of exit options) can have large positive effects on efficiency, similar to or greater than those observed for punishment in standard PGGs. For prediction, the presence or absence of self-reliance/exit options, and the ability to restrict them, should be considered as key moderators of efficiency, alongside standard game design dimensions.

- source: Tverskoi, D., Ember, C. R., Gelfand, M. J., Jones, E. C., Skoggard, I., Toutee, L., & Gavrilets, S. (2024). Cultural tightness and resilience against environmental shocks in nonindustrial societies. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that group efficiency (average payoff) and cooperation are maximized at intermediate levels of cultural tightness when facing severe environmental shocks. Loose cultures (low tightness) are more resilient and efficient under mild, slow-onset shocks, while very tight cultures often fail to adapt to new shocks and experience lower efficiency. However, very tight cultures can maintain high efficiency in the face of regular, severe shocks if cooperative norms are already established. Increasing group size undermines cooperation and efficiency unless benefit parameters scale accordingly. Synergistic effects between different types of collective action (public goods) enhance both cooperation and efficiency. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning, but norm compliance is enforced via conformity in the utility function.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-like environments with norm-based conformity (but no explicit punishment), efficiency is maximized at intermediate levels of norm strength (tightness) under severe shocks, and at low tightness under mild shocks. The absence of explicit punishment means the results are not directly transferable to settings where punishment is enabled or disabled, but the findings are highly informative for predicting efficiency as a function of group size, benefit structure, and norm strength. The model's structure and results are most relevant for public goods games with strong norm compliance or conformity-based incentives, and less so for games where punishment is the main enforcement mechanism. The model also shows that increasing group size reduces efficiency unless benefits scale up, and that synergy between collective actions increases the range of conditions for high efficiency.

- source: Panchanathan, K., & Boyd, R. (2004). Indirect reciprocity can stabilize cooperation without the second-order free rider problem. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that linking collective action (public goods contribution) to indirect reciprocity (mutual aid based on reputation) can stabilize cooperation through exclusion of non-contributors from future aid. This mechanism avoids the second-order free rider problem because punishers (Shunners) do not incur a direct cost; instead, they withhold aid from defectors, which is socially sanctioned. The model identifies conditions under which this strategy is evolutionarily stable and shows that both full cooperation (Shunner) and full defection (Defector) can be stable equilibria, depending on initial frequencies and assortment. The model also shows that strategies linking collective action and indirect reciprocity cannot easily invade populations practicing only indirect reciprocity. While the model discusses group payoff and welfare in the context of equilibrium selection, it does not report explicit efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that indirect punishment via exclusion (rather than direct costly punishment) can stabilize cooperation in public goods games, especially when reputation is visible and linked across domains. It suggests that such mechanisms can lead to higher group payoff and welfare, and that equilibrium selection processes (e.g., group selection, random drift) may favor these strategies. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions about efficiency or the effect of enabling punishment in a standard PGG. Its relevance is strongest for settings where punishment is implemented as exclusion from future benefits rather than direct payoff reduction. The model's insights are most applicable to predicting when and why punishment-like mechanisms (especially exclusion) might be effective, but less so for precise efficiency prediction in standard experimental PGGs with direct costly punishment.

- source: Milinski, M., Semmann, D., Krambeck, H. J., & Marotzke, J. (2006). Stabilizing the Earth's climate is not a losing game: Supporting evidence from public goods experiments. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that cooperation in a climate public goods game can be substantially increased by making contributions public (enabling reputation effects) and by providing expert information about climate change. Social rewards (support in the indirect reciprocity game) reinforce cooperative behavior, and non-cooperators are socially 'punished' by being less likely to receive support. However, the game design does not include direct monetary punishment, and the outcome measured is not group efficiency or total earnings but rather the behavioral willingness to contribute to a public good with real-world impact. The findings suggest that reputation and information can be powerful motivators for cooperation in public goods dilemmas, even when the benefits are not directly redistributed to the contributors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that making contributions public (enabling reputation/reward) and providing expert information can increase cooperation rates in a public goods context, even when direct monetary punishment is absent and the public good is not redistributed. However, because the outcome is not group efficiency or total earnings, and because the 'punishment' is social (withholding support in a separate game) rather than direct, the findings are only adjacent to the core prediction task. The results suggest that enabling reputation or reward mechanisms may have a positive effect on cooperation, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes in standard PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Howe, E. L., Murphy, J. J., Gerkey, D., & West, C. T. (2016). Indirect Reciprocity, Resource Sharing, and Environmental Risk: Evidence from Field Experiments in Siberia. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment found that voluntary sharing was substantial and directed toward those in need, especially when reputations could be built. Sharing was conditioned on cooperative behavior when such information was available. However, the presence of sharing, rewards, and reputation mechanisms did not increase contributions to the public good (i.e., did not increase cooperation in the production domain). The expected returns from contributing to the group activity, even with the possibility of receiving sharing, were not high enough to incentivize greater cooperation. Thus, the mechanisms studied did not improve group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that, in environments where sharing is voluntary and not highly efficient, and where rewards/punishments are not strong or direct, the introduction of sharing and reputation mechanisms may not increase group efficiency or cooperation in public goods games. The findings imply that unless the payoff structure makes rewards or punishments highly effective, simply enabling sharing or reputation is unlikely to boost efficiency. For prediction, if the control game has low efficiency and the only change is the addition of a voluntary, non-committal sharing/reward mechanism (with no direct punishment and no high-impact reward), the efficiency in the treatment is unlikely to increase. The paper provides indirect evidence that the effectiveness of punishment/reward mechanisms depends on their efficiency and observability.

- source: Harrell, A., & Traeger, M. L. (2025). Reputation-based reciprocity in human-bot and human-human networks. *Pnas Nexus*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The presence of bots in indirect reciprocity networks reduces human generosity, even when bots are programmed to be strong reciprocators. This reduction is driven by humans' reluctance to help bots, due to weaker norms about bots deserving help, not by a lack of reputational concern. Both 'reputational giving' (giving when observed by a third party) and 'rewarding reputations' (helping those with a reputation for helping) are less effective when bots are present. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the reduction in giving implies lower group welfare in hybrid networks compared to human-only networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence of non-human agents (bots) in indirect reciprocity games reduces generosity and, by implication, group welfare. However, it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes directly. For prediction tasks about punishment effects on efficiency, this paper is only adjacent: it suggests that social context (e.g., presence of bots) can moderate the effectiveness of reciprocity-based mechanisms, but does not provide direct evidence about punishment or efficiency. The design and findings may be informative for understanding how social norms and recipient identity affect cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of punishment effects.

- source: Shirado, H., Fu, F., Fowler, J. H., & Christakis, N. A. (2013). Quality versus quantity of social ties in experimental cooperative networks. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing dynamic partner selection in a networked cooperation game leads to the highest levels of cooperation at intermediate rates of network rewiring. At low rewiring rates, players accumulate many ties, including to defectors, which undermines cooperation. At very high rewiring rates, defectors can re-attach to cooperators faster than cooperators can detach, also undermining cooperation. The ability to break ties with defectors (interpreted as a form of social punishment) helps maintain cooperation, but only when the rate of change is not too high or too low. The main outcome is the cooperation rate; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of dynamic partner selection (ostracism-like social punishment) on cooperation in networked public goods games. It suggests that enabling players to break ties with defectors can sustain cooperation, but only at intermediate rates of network change. However, because the study does not include explicit costly punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in standard PGGs is limited. The findings are most relevant for settings where social exclusion or partner choice is the main sanctioning mechanism, rather than costly punishment. The results suggest that the design dimension of network dynamism (rewiring rate) can have a non-monotonic effect on cooperation, which may translate to efficiency if payoff outcomes were measured.

- source: Rand, D. G., Arbesman, S., & Christakis, N. A. (2011). Dynamic social networks promote cooperation in experiments with humans. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that in large groups playing a repeated networked cooperation game, cooperation decays over time in static, random, or slowly updating networks, but is stably maintained when players can frequently update their social ties. Players preferentially break links with defectors and form links with cooperators, which disciplines defectors and incentivizes cooperation. This dynamic network updating acts as a decentralized ostracism mechanism, supporting cooperation without explicit costly punishment. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are most directly about behavioral cooperation, not payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that dynamic, fluid social networks can sustain high cooperation rates in repeated social dilemma games, even in large groups. However, it does not directly address the effect of explicit punishment mechanisms (costly punishment) on efficiency or payoff. Instead, it studies a form of endogenous, non-costly ostracism (link breaking), which is adjacent to but not the same as standard punishment in public goods games. For prediction tasks about the effect of enabling costly punishment on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that non-costly, decentralized exclusion can promote cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or about the effect of costly punishment. The game design dimensions are partially informative (e.g., player count, network updating rules), but the absence of explicit payoff/efficiency outcomes and standard punishment limits direct applicability.

- source: Yoeli, E., Hoffman, M., Rand, D. G., & Nowak, M. A. (2013). Powering up with indirect reciprocity in a large-scale field experiment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that making participation in a voluntary demand response program observable to neighbors nearly triples participation rates compared to an anonymous condition. The effect of observability is much larger than that of a $25 monetary incentive. The effect is strongest in contexts where reputational concerns are likely to matter more (e.g., apartment buildings, homeowners vs. renters, and when the program is framed as a public good). The results support the theory that indirect reciprocity and reputational incentives can drive large-scale cooperation in real-world public goods dilemmas. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that increasing observability (reputational incentives) can dramatically increase participation in a real-world public goods context, especially when social ties and public good framing are salient. However, because the outcome is participation rate (not efficiency or group payoff), the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based efficiency, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment mechanisms. It does, however, suggest that non-monetary, reputational incentives can be more effective than small monetary incentives in increasing cooperation.

- source: Quan, J., Zheng, J. J., Wang, X. J., & Yang, X. K. (2019). Benefits of asynchronous exclusion for the evolution of cooperation in stochastic evolutionary optional public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that asynchronous exclusion (where excluders act sequentially and avoid redundant costs) is more effective at promoting cooperation than synchronous exclusion (where all excluders act simultaneously and pay redundant costs). This effect is strongest when the probability of exclusion success is high and the public goods multiplier is low (well-mixed) or intermediate (structured). The main reported outcomes are the probability of the system reaching a cooperative state and the frequency of different strategies at equilibrium. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment-like mechanisms in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effectiveness of exclusion (a sanctioning mechanism) in promoting cooperation in optional public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, the increased probability of reaching cooperative states under asynchronous exclusion suggests that, under some parameter regimes, more effective or less costly sanctioning can increase cooperation rates. However, because efficiency is not measured, and because exclusion differs from standard punishment (it removes defectors from benefit sharing rather than imposing a direct cost), the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in standard PGGs. The results are most relevant for settings where exclusion is the main sanction and where the cost structure of sanctioning can be varied.

- source: Tverskoi, D., Senthilnathan, A., & Gavrilets, S. (2021). The dynamics of cooperation, power, and inequality in a group-structured society. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation and efficiency in group-structured societies depend strongly on group size, the degree of rivalrousness of goods, and the strength of institutional checks and balances (modeled by the incumbency parameter ε). Stronger checks and balances (lower ε) promote cooperation and reduce inequality. Larger groups tend to be more powerful and successful in between-group cooperation, except when goods are highly rivalrous, in which case smaller groups may outperform larger ones (Olson's paradox). The model predicts multiple stable equilibria and non-equilibrium dynamics depending on parameter values. However, the model does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; these are only discussed as possible future extensions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence on how game design dimensions such as player count, group size, benefit parameters, and rivalrousness affect efficiency and payoff outcomes in nested public-goods-like environments. However, it does not address the effect of punishment or sanctions, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. It is useful for understanding baseline efficiency and the role of structural and institutional moderators, but not for predicting the marginal effect of punishment.

- source: Majolo, B., & Maréchal, L. (2017). Between-group competition elicits within-group cooperation in children. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that simulated between-group competition (BGC) increases within-group cooperation among children aged 3-10, as measured by the number of stickers shared with group members (experiment 1) and the number of stickers contributed to a public good (experiment 2). The effect is robust to age (especially for children 6+), and is not explained by gender, kinship, or familiarity. However, the experiments do not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and do not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The main dependent variables are behavioral measures of cooperation, not payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does provide indirect evidence that between-group competition can increase cooperative behavior in children, but since punishment is not manipulated or measured, and efficiency or group payoff is not reported, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, MPCR) are partially reported, but the absence of punishment and efficiency outcomes means this paper should not be weighted heavily for the prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Waite, A. J., & Shou, W. Y. (2012). Adaptation to a new environment allows cooperators to purge cheaters stochastically. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a well-mixed, engineered yeast system where cooperators pay a cost to produce a common good and cheaters do not, adaptation to a novel, nutrient-limited environment can allow cooperators to stochastically dominate and purge cheaters in some replicate cultures. This outcome is driven by the chance acquisition of highly beneficial mutations in cooperators, which can offset the initial fitness advantage of cheaters. The frequency of cooperator-dominated cultures is determined by the initial abundance of cooperators. The paper does not study or manipulate punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff in the sense relevant to public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or related mechanisms, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its findings about evolutionary adaptation and stochastic outcomes in microbial cooperation are only weakly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in human or animal public goods games. The main value is in illustrating that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., adaptation to novel environments) can allow cooperation to persist, but this is not directly transferable to the prediction task.

- source: Skatova, A., Spence, A., Leygue, C., & Ferguson, E. (2017). Guilty repair sustains cooperation, angry retaliation destroys it. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated social dilemma game without punishment, group efficiency (measured as group earnings and energy savings) declines over time due to increased defection and retaliation. Retaliation (increasing usage after being exploited) is associated with lower group payoffs, while repair (reducing usage after overusing) is associated with higher group payoffs. Groups with more prosocial members (who are more likely to repair and less likely to retaliate) achieve higher efficiency. Emotional responses (anger and guilt) mediate these behaviors: anger leads to retaliation, guilt leads to repair. The absence of punishment means that emotional self-regulation, especially guilt-driven repair, is the main mechanism for sustaining cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in repeated public-goods-like games without punishment, efficiency tends to decline over time due to retaliation and lack of repair. The presence of prosocial individuals who engage in guilty repair can partially sustain efficiency, but overall, the lack of punishment leads to lower group payoffs. For prediction tasks, this study suggests that in the absence of punishment, efficiency will be lower and decline over time, especially in groups with more selfish individuals. The findings are most relevant for control conditions (no punishment), and highlight the importance of emotional and social preference heterogeneity. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment, so it cannot inform the incremental effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Lo Iacono, S., Sonmez, B., & Fairbrother, M. (2024). The effect of trusting contexts in social dilemmas with collective and individual solutions. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Groups in high-trust contexts are more likely to coordinate on the collective solution, resulting in higher efficiency and less resource waste. In low-trust contexts, participants invest more in individual solutions, leading to lower efficiency and more waste. Free-riding rates are similar across trust contexts. The presence of both individual and collective solutions introduces coordination challenges and inefficiency, especially in low-trust settings. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or variants. It is informative about how trust context affects efficiency and coordination in a threshold public goods game with individual fallback, but since no punishment or sanctioning is present, it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to enabling punishment. The design and efficiency results may be useful as a control or baseline for similar games, but not for estimating the marginal effect of punishment.

- source: Mori, R., Hanaki, N., & Kameda, T. (2024). An outside individual option increases optimism and facilitates collaboration when groups form flexibly. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing an outside individual option (voluntary participation) in a threshold public goods game increases the efficiency (average payoff) and group success rate compared to mandatory participation, particularly when group formation is flexible and loners do not affect group outcomes. The mechanisms are (1) self-selection of optimistic individuals into groups and (2) belief updating that increases cooperation among those who stay. The positive effect on efficiency is strongest when the baseline (mandatory) group success rate is not already near ceiling. The effect reverses if loners' externality is high (i.e., if loners still affect group outcomes, as in fixed group boundaries). No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in threshold public goods games with flexible group formation and no punishment or reward mechanisms, introducing an outside individual option (voluntary participation) increases efficiency and group success, except when the baseline success rate is already very high. The effect is mediated by self-selection and belief updating. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with and without voluntary participation, but not for games with punishment. The absence of punishment or reward means the results are not directly transferable to settings where those mechanisms are present, but the paper is highly informative for the effect of voluntary participation and group boundary flexibility.

- source: Semmann, D., Krambeck, H. J. R., & Milinski, M. (2003). Volunteering leads to rock-paper-scissors dynamics in a public goods game. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing players to opt out of the public goods game (become 'loners') leads to rock-paper-scissors dynamics among cooperators, defectors, and loners. This dynamic sustains a stable, nonzero level of cooperation and prevents the collapse to universal defection typically seen in standard public goods games. Average payoffs for cooperators and defectors converge to the loner payoff over time, as predicted by theory. The presence of the loner option increases the average frequency of cooperation compared to standard PGGs, but does not produce overwhelming cooperation. There is no punishment or reward mechanism in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it provides evidence that introducing an opt-out (volunteering/loner) option can sustain cooperation and stabilize payoffs in public goods games. For the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding alternative mechanisms (volunteering) that can also affect efficiency.

- source: Nockur, L., Arndt, L., Keller, J., & Pfattheicher, S. (2020). Collective choice fosters sustainable resource management in the presence of asymmetric opportunities. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: In a lab common resource game with groups of four, participants played under three systems: individual choice, median choice, and majority voting. In both symmetric and asymmetric opportunity conditions, collective choice systems (median and majority voting) led to lower average extraction rates and higher profits than individual choice. The effect was especially pronounced for disadvantaged group members in asymmetric groups, whose profits increased substantially under collective choice. Advantaged group members' extraction was curtailed under collective choice, but their profits did not increase. Satisfaction and fairness ratings were also higher under collective choice. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms were present in any condition.
