  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in common resource games with or without asymmetric extraction opportunities, implementing collective choice mechanisms (median or majority voting) increases efficiency and group payoff compared to individual choice. However, the study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of collective decision rules (not punishment) on efficiency, and less relevant for the downstream task of predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The game structure is close to a public goods dilemma, but the absence of punishment means the results are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled settings.

- source: Yamamoto, T., Ito, H., Nii, M., Okabe, T., Morita, S., & Yoshimura, J. (2019). A single 'weight-lifting' game covers all kinds of games. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper demonstrates mathematically that the weight-lifting game can represent all major two-player cooperation/defection games by adjusting three parameters: the incremental success probabilities from cooperation and the cost-to-benefit ratio. It provides a unified payoff matrix and phase diagrams showing how efficiency and cooperation depend on these parameters. The model is shown to encompass the standard public goods game as a special case when the success probability increases linearly with the number of cooperators. The paper does not consider punishment, sanctions, or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding how efficiency and group payoff outcomes in public-goods-like games depend on the underlying payoff structure, especially the cost/benefit ratio and the synergy of cooperation. However, it does not address punishment, sanctions, or any intervention mechanism, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful for baseline modeling of efficiency in the absence of punishment, or for understanding how the structure of the game (e.g., synergy, cost/benefit) sets the stage for potential intervention effects.

- source: Brown, S. P., & Taddei, F. (2007). The Durability of Public Goods Changes the Dynamics and Nature of Social Dilemmas. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that when the public good is durable (i.e., changes slowly relative to the frequency of cooperators), the dynamics of cooperation and group payoff can be substantially altered compared to classic models. For example, durable public goods can lead to multi-generational oscillations in cooperation and payoff, shift the thresholds for invasion and stability of cooperation, and even allow for transient increases in cooperation in settings where defection would otherwise dominate. The explicit modeling of public good dynamics reveals that group payoff and efficiency are not solely determined by current cooperation rates, but also by the history and durability of the public good. However, the paper does not address punishment, sanctions, or any intervention mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding how the dynamics and durability of the public good itself can affect efficiency and group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. It highlights that efficiency is not always a simple function of current cooperation rates, especially when the public good is durable or slow to change. However, because the paper does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is most relevant for understanding baseline dynamics and for considering how lagged or durable public goods might interact with other interventions in future models.

- source: Hackel, J., Yamamoto, H., Okada, I., Goto, A., & Taudes, A. (2021). Asymmetric effects of social and economic incentives on cooperation in real effort based public goods games. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment finds that social incentives (charity donations) significantly increase the proportion of participants who fully cooperate (complete 90+ tasks), while economic incentives (personal monetary reward) significantly reduce the proportion of full defectors (complete 10 or fewer tasks). The effects are asymmetric: social incentives promote high cooperation, economic incentives reduce non-cooperation. No direct measures of efficiency or group payoff are reported. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, focusing instead on positive incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. The design is a close variant of a PGG, using real effort instead of resource allocation, and focuses on the effects of positive (economic and social) incentives on cooperation and defection rates. For prediction tasks about punishment and efficiency, this paper is not directly informative, but it does suggest that incentive structures (other than punishment) can have asymmetric effects on cooperation and defection. The absence of punishment and the focus on non-payoff behavioral outcomes limit its direct applicability to the prediction of efficiency under punishment.

- source: Capraro, V., Jordan, J. J., & Rand, D. G. (2014). Heuristics guide the implementation of social preferences in one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma experiments. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main findings are that in a one-shot, continuous-strategy PD, cooperation is trimodally distributed (none, half, all). Increasing the benefit-to-cost ratio (b/c) increases the average amount of cooperation by shifting people from no cooperation to full cooperation, but does not affect the proportion of 50% cooperators. The results suggest that both efficiency concerns and heuristic reasoning (such as fairness heuristics) guide cooperation. There is no analysis of punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. The study is relevant for understanding how b/c affects cooperation in two-player, one-shot, continuous public goods settings, but does not inform about punishment or efficiency effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it does provide informative evidence about how the marginal per-capita return (b/c) affects cooperation rates in a two-player, one-shot, continuous-contribution setting without punishment. It shows that higher b/c increases cooperation, but a substantial fraction of participants use heuristics (e.g., giving half) regardless of b/c. Since there is no punishment or efficiency outcome reported, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation as a function of b/c in the absence of punishment.

- source: Jordan, J. J., Rand, D. G., Arbesman, S., Fowler, J. H., & Christakis, N. A. (2013). Contagion of Cooperation in Static and Fluid Social Networks. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in fixed and viscous networks, both cooperation and defection are contagious: individuals are more likely to switch to cooperation (or defection) if their neighbors did so in the previous round. In fluid networks, where players can more frequently change their partners, cooperation is not initially contagious—players are likely to cooperate regardless of their neighbors' behavior, likely to attract new cooperative partners. As the network stabilizes and becomes less fluid, contagion of cooperation emerges. Defection remains contagious in all network types. The paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes, and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or variants, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its main contribution is to show how network structure and partner choice affect the contagion of cooperation and defection in binary cooperation games. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, except as context for how network fluidity and partner choice may interact with behavioral contagion. It does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Buchan, N. R., Grimalda, G., Wilson, R., Brewer, M., Fatas, E., & Foddy, M. (2009). Globalization and human cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both country-level and individual-level globalization are strongly positively associated with contributions to the global public good (World account) in a multilevel public goods experiment. There is no evidence that globalization is associated with increased local cooperation. The experiment does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. The main finding is that globalization predicts broader, more cosmopolitan cooperation, as measured by willingness to contribute to a global public good.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it analyze efficiency, group payoff, or related payoff-based outcomes. Its findings are about the relationship between globalization and willingness to contribute to global versus local public goods, not about the effect of game design dimensions or punishment on efficiency. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Otten, K., Frey, U. J., Buskens, V., Przepiorka, W., & Ellemers, N. (2022). Human cooperation in changing groups in a large-scale public goods game. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that the number of newcomers in a group is negatively associated with the average percentage of resources contributed to the public good. This effect is strongest in small groups and at later time periods. The lower group contribution is primarily due to newcomers contributing less than incumbents, though their contributions increase as they spend more time in the group. The difference between newcomers and incumbents is partially explained by differences in private good levels and public good levels, but remains significant even after controlling for these factors. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment, and there is no evidence of sanctioning mechanisms being central to the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide informative context about how group composition changes (newcomer entry) can reduce cooperation (as measured by contribution rates), especially in the absence of punishment or exclusion. For prediction tasks involving efficiency, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not report payoff-based outcomes or manipulate punishment. It may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in large, dynamic groups without punishment.

- source: Chen, X. J., & Perc, M. (2014). Excessive abundance of common resources deters social responsibility. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in a collective-risk social dilemma is most sustainable when the initial amount of common resources and the effectiveness of cooperative contributions are at intermediate levels. Excessive abundance of resources or overly effective cooperation allows defectors to free-ride, leading to the eventual collapse of cooperation and resource depletion. Conversely, too little resource or ineffective cooperation also leads to collapse. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The main reported outcomes are the stationary fraction of cooperators and the sustainability of the common resource pool, not direct efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as defined in the prediction task. Its findings about resource abundance and cooperation may be contextually informative for understanding resource dynamics, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Kelly, D., Claessens, S., Sibley, C. G., Chaudhuri, A., & Atkinson, Q. (2022). Cooperative phenotype predicts climate change belief and pro-environmental behaviour. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that individuals who behave more cooperatively in a suite of economic games (including a public goods game) are more likely to believe in climate change and to report pro-environmental behaviors. The relationship between cooperative phenotype and climate change belief/behavior is robust to many demographic and personality controls, but is attenuated by political party support. Mediation analyses suggest that climate change belief may mediate the effect of cooperative phenotype on pro-environmental behavior. However, the study does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or sanctions in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it uses a public goods game as part of a battery to estimate cooperative phenotype, it does not manipulate or report on punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Wu, J. J., Zhang, B. Y., Zhou, Z. X., He, Q. Q., Zheng, X. D., Cressman, R., & Tao, Y. (2009). Costly punishment does not always increase cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that, in a repeated two-player Prisoner's Dilemma with Chinese participants, the introduction of costly punishment either had no significant effect or reduced the frequency of cooperation, and led to a significant decrease in average group payoff (efficiency) in one treatment. In the other treatment, the decrease in payoff was not statistically significant. The results are contrasted with previous US-based studies, where costly punishment increased cooperation but did not improve group payoff. The authors attribute the difference to cultural factors, particularly attitudes toward reputation and authority. The study provides direct evidence that costly punishment does not always increase cooperation or efficiency, and may even reduce them depending on context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in repeated two-player Prisoner's Dilemma games (an adjacent but not exact variant of the public goods game), enabling costly punishment can reduce group efficiency (average payoff), especially when cooperation does not increase. The effect of punishment on efficiency is not universally positive and may depend on cultural context and game structure. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency, and in some settings (e.g., repeated dyadic games, certain cultures), it may even lower it. The paper also highlights that increased cooperation does not always translate to higher efficiency if punishment costs offset cooperative gains.

- source: Dreber, A., Rand, D. G., Fudenberg, D., & Nowak, M. A. (2008). Winners don't punish. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing a costly punishment option in a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma increases the frequency of cooperation but does not increase the average payoff of the group. In both parameterizations (benefit/cost ratios of 2 and 3), there is no significant difference in average payoff between the control (no punishment) and treatment (punishment allowed) conditions. Individuals who use punishment more frequently have lower total payoffs, and the most successful players do not use punishment at all. The results suggest that, in repeated dyadic cooperation games, punishment is maladaptive from an efficiency perspective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated dyadic cooperation games with direct reciprocity, enabling costly punishment does not improve group efficiency compared to a control without punishment. The cost of punishment cancels out the gains from increased cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar repeated dyadic games, the efficiency with punishment enabled will be similar to or lower than the control efficiency, even if cooperation rates rise. The findings are most directly applicable to repeated Prisoner's Dilemma designs, but the mechanism (costs of punishment offsetting cooperation gains) is likely relevant for other repeated social dilemma games with similar structure.

- source: Ule, A., Schram, A., Riedl, A., & Cason, T. N. (2009). Indirect Punishment and Generosity Toward Strangers. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compares two treatments of an indirect helping game: one where indirect punishment is only symbolic (no material effect on the recipient), and one where punishment is harmful (recipient loses 250 francs). In both, helping is costly and beneficial, and donors can observe recipients' recent behavior. The main finding is that, although the frequency of helping does not differ significantly between treatments, the availability of harmful punishment reduces the payoffs of defectors and increases the payoffs of rewarders and cautious defectors. In the control, defectors earn most; with harmful punishment, rewarders and cautious defectors earn more than defectors. Harmful punishment is rare but targeted at defectors, and this is sufficient to change the payoff structure in favor of cooperation-supporting strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling costly, effective punishment in an indirect reciprocity environment (adjacent to PGG) increases the average payoff of cooperative strategies relative to defectors, even if overall cooperation rates do not rise. For prediction, this suggests that, in games where punishment is enabled and is materially costly to the recipient, group efficiency or average payoff can increase relative to a control with only symbolic or no punishment, primarily by reducing defectors' payoffs. The effect is not driven by increased cooperation rates but by the deterrent effect of punishment on defectors' earnings. The design is not a standard PGG but is closely related in its focus on cooperation, punishment, and payoff outcomes.

- source: Han, T. A. (2022). Institutional incentives for the evolution of committed cooperation: ensuring participation is as important as enhancing compliance. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional incentives (punishment or reward) can promote the evolution of cooperation and commitment compliance in a social dilemma with a prior commitment stage. Reward is generally more effective than punishment at promoting cooperation, especially when the cost of commitment is high or the benefit-to-cost ratio is large. Allocating part of the incentive budget to encourage participation in the commitment (not just compliance) further increases cooperation, particularly when participation is costly. The presence of noise in participation decisions can also stabilize cooperation. The results are robust across a range of parameter values and are based on explicit payoff calculations and evolutionary stability analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in social dilemma games with a prior commitment stage, enabling punishment (or reward) increases group efficiency (cooperation frequency) compared to no incentives, provided the incentive budget is sufficient. Reward is more effective than punishment, especially as the cost of participation increases. Allocating part of the incentive budget to encourage participation (not just compliance) further increases efficiency, especially when participation is costly. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in games with a commitment or opt-in stage, and the model's explicit payoff calculations can be mapped to efficiency outcomes. However, the model is based on a two-player, one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma with a commitment stage, not a standard repeated public goods game, so predictions should be transferred with caution to multi-player, repeated settings.

- source: Li, X. L., Jusup, M., Wang, Z., Li, H. J., Shi, L., Podobnik, B., Stanley, H. E., Havlin, S., & Boccaletti, S. (2018). Punishment diminishes the benefits of network reciprocity in social dilemma experiments. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compared three treatments: (1) a well-mixed repeated PD (control), (2) a static network PD with two neighbors (network reciprocity), and (3) a static network PD with punishment available. Network reciprocity increased both cooperation and payoff relative to the well-mixed control. However, adding punishment did not further increase cooperation and actually reduced the median payoff per round back to the control level. Punishment also reduced assortment (clustering of cooperators) and, in larger neighborhoods, erased the positive correlation between cooperation and payoff. Thus, punishment in this setting is ineffective or even counterproductive for group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated prisoner's dilemma games on static networks (with two or four neighbors), enabling costly punishment (with a 1:4 cost-to-impact ratio) does not improve and may even reduce group efficiency compared to the same game without punishment. The negative effect is especially clear in payoff-based outcomes: median payoff per round is lower with punishment than without, and the benefit of network reciprocity is erased. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar networked PD or adjacent PGG-like environments, adding punishment may not increase efficiency and could reduce it, especially as neighborhood size increases. The findings are most directly applicable to static network PDs with small groups, no communication, and fixed payoff matrices.

- source: Lergetporer, P., Angerer, S., Glätzle-Rützler, D., & Sutter, M. (2014). Third-party punishment increases cooperation in children through (misaligned) expectations and conditional cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that the threat of third-party punishment in a one-shot PD with children increases cooperation rates from 25% (control) to 58% (punishment treatment). This leads to an increase in average payoffs from 2.49 tokens (control) to 2.98 tokens (punishment), a 20% improvement in efficiency. The effect is not due to frequent actual punishment (which is rare), but rather to children's (over-)expectation of punishment and to conditional cooperation based on higher expected partner cooperation. The effect is robust across age cohorts (7-11 years).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling third-party punishment in a two-player, one-shot PD with children substantially increases efficiency, even when actual punishment is rare. The effect is mediated by beliefs and conditional cooperation. For prediction, the presence of a third-party punishment mechanism (with a strong, binary punishment technology) in a simple, anonymous, one-shot, two-player game with no communication and known payoffs can be expected to increase efficiency by a substantial margin (here, ~20%). The effect is robust to age (within 7-11), and is not dependent on actual punishment frequency. The design is adjacent to a PGG, but the payoff structure and mechanism are closely related.

- source: Jia, S. H., & Wang, Z. Y. (2025). An evolutionary game model with reputation threshold and reputation score to promote trust in the sharing economy. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that implementing a dynamic, reputation-based reward and punishment mechanism in a trust game on a square lattice network increases the proportion of trustworthy participants and can increase total group revenue (efficiency) compared to a baseline without such mechanisms. The effect depends on the reputation threshold: moderate thresholds maximize both trust and total revenue, while very high thresholds reduce revenue due to excessive punishment. The model demonstrates that, for a wide range of initial conditions, appropriate punishment/reward settings can drive the system to full trust and higher efficiency. The results are robust to population size. The model is not a standard PGG but is adjacent, and the efficiency outcome is directly comparable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in networked trust games with dynamic reputation-based punishment and reward, enabling punishment (and reward) can increase group efficiency compared to a baseline with no sanctions, provided the punishment/reward parameters are set appropriately. The effect is non-monotonic: too much punishment (high threshold) reduces efficiency, while moderate levels maximize it. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence and calibration of punishment/reward mechanisms are critical moderators of efficiency gains from sanctions. The model is adjacent to PGGs but the efficiency outcome is directly relevant.

- source: Wang, Z., Jusup, M., Wang, R. W., Shi, L., Iwasa, Y., Moreno, Y., & Kurths, J. (2017). Onymity promotes cooperation in social dilemma experiments. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma with three available actions (cooperate, defect, punish), the use of costly punishment is negatively correlated with individual payoffs in both anonymous and onymous conditions. Onymity (making opponents identifiable) increases cooperation and median payoffs, but punishment does not promote efficiency or cooperation. Instead, those who punish more tend to earn less ('winners don't punish' effect). Control trials without punishment show similar qualitative effects of onymity, suggesting that punishment is not necessary for increased cooperation or efficiency. The paper provides direct evidence that punishment, as implemented here, does not improve group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma games with costly punishment, enabling punishment does not increase efficiency or group payoff. In fact, punishment is associated with lower payoffs for those who use it. The findings suggest that, all else equal, adding a costly punishment option to a similar game design is unlikely to increase efficiency, and may even reduce it. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, discrete-choice social dilemmas with costly punishment, and should be interpreted with caution when generalizing to multi-player public goods games. However, the negative effect of punishment on efficiency is robust in this setting.

- source: Zhou, X., Belloum, A., Lees, M. H., van Engers, T., & de Laat, C. (2022). Costly incentives design from an institutional perspective: cooperation, sustainability and affluence. *Proceedings of the Royal Society A-mathematical Physical and Engineering Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma with institutional (third-party) incentives, pure punishment is always sustainable and, when sufficiently strong, increases both the market size and the accumulated wealth of participants and the third party. Light punishment can lead to market shrinkage or collapse. Mixed incentives (reward plus punishment) can maximize the sum of accumulated wealth for both parties at moderate strength, but their sustainability depends on both incentive strength and agent rationality. Pure reward increases participant wealth but is unsustainable for the third party in this model. The results are robust across different levels of agent rationality, though higher rationality makes it easier to achieve high cooperation and affluence.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in repeated pairwise social dilemmas with institutional punishment, increasing the strength of punishment (i.e., higher fines for defection) leads to higher efficiency (measured as accumulated wealth and market size), provided the punishment is not too light. Light punishment can reduce efficiency by shrinking the market. Mixed incentives can further optimize total efficiency at moderate strength. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar institutional settings, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low due to defection. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related, so results should be transferred with some caution.

- source: Han, T. A., Duong, M. H., & Perc, M. (2024). Evolutionary mechanisms that promote cooperation may not promote social welfare. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary models of the Prisoner's Dilemma with peer or institutional incentives, punishment mechanisms (both peer and institutional) are often detrimental to social welfare, even when they increase cooperation. Peer punishment increases cooperation more than peer reward, but peer reward leads to higher social welfare. For institutional incentives, reward achieves positive social welfare across a broader range of parameters than punishment, even when both achieve similar cooperation levels. There is often an optimal, moderate level of incentive impact for maximizing social welfare, rather than maximizing cooperation or punishment strength. These findings are robust across mutation rates, selection intensities, and game configurations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment in public-goods-game-like environments (here, the Prisoner's Dilemma) is likely to reduce efficiency (social welfare) compared to reward mechanisms, even if punishment increases cooperation. The effect is robust across a range of model parameters. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (especially with high impact-to-cost ratio) should be expected to have a less positive or even negative effect on efficiency relative to control, unless the punishment is extremely cost-effective and moderate in impact. The findings suggest that maximizing cooperation via punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency, and that reward mechanisms are generally preferable for social welfare. The results are based on theoretical models, not empirical data, but are directly relevant to the efficiency prediction task.

- source: Mohlin, E., Rigos, A., & Weidenholzer, S. (2023). Emergence of specialized third-party enforcement. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that specialized third-party punishment can enable and sustain high-efficiency cooperative equilibria in social dilemmas where direct and indirect reciprocity fail. When enforcers are incentivized (via reputation and meta-enforcement) to punish defectors, producers cooperate, and both groups achieve higher payoffs. The efficiency gain from punishment depends on the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the cost of information, and the destructiveness of enforcer conflict. If punishment is too costly or weak, or if enforcer conflict is not destructive enough, the system collapses to a low-efficiency defection equilibrium. The model also shows that the cooperative equilibrium is robust to the presence of alternative (non-cooperation-enforcing) reputation systems, but only if the key parameter thresholds are met.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment by specialized third-party enforcers can substantially increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, but only if the punishment is sufficiently severe, not too costly, and enforcers are themselves disciplined (e.g., via meta-enforcement and reputation). The model clarifies which game design dimensions (punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, information cost, severity of punishment, and the structure of enforcement interactions) are critical moderators of the efficiency effect. For prediction, the presence of third-party punishment should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline, provided the relevant costs and incentives are favorable. However, if punishment is weak, costly, or undisciplined, efficiency gains may not materialize.

- source: Han, T. A., Pereira, L. M., Lenaerts, T., & Santos, F. C. (2021). Mediating artificial intelligence developments through negative and positive incentives. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment (sanctions) and rewards can increase group welfare and promote safety compliance in a repeated innovation race game, but their effects depend on the underlying risk and incentive parameters. Punishment is generally more effective at suppressing unsafe (defective) behavior and increasing group welfare in the 'dilemma' region where individual and collective interests diverge, but if punishment is too strong or too efficient, it can lead to over-regulation and reduce innovation even when risk is low and unsafe behavior would be collectively optimal. Rewards can also promote safety and welfare, especially when highly cost-efficient, and do not suffer from the risk of over-regulation, but are generally less effective than punishment. The model provides explicit conditions for when each incentive is beneficial or harmful for group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like innovation races is highly context-dependent. If the risk of disaster from unsafe behavior is high, punishment increases efficiency by promoting safe behavior. If the risk is low, strong punishment can reduce efficiency by suppressing innovation. The cost-effectiveness of punishment (punishment cost and impact) is critical: punishment is most beneficial when its cost to the punisher is low relative to its impact on the punished. Reward mechanisms can also increase efficiency, especially when highly cost-effective, and are less likely to reduce efficiency through over-regulation. For prediction tasks, the model suggests that the effect of enabling punishment depends on the risk/benefit structure, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the baseline efficiency of the control game. The findings are most directly applicable to innovation race or winner-take-all settings, but the mechanisms are adjacent to public goods games.

- source: Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2020). Five rules for friendly rivalry in direct reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that strategies combining partner and rival features (friendly rivals) exist in the repeated prisoner's dilemma, with the CAPRI strategy as a prime example. CAPRI achieves mutual cooperation (maximal efficiency) when both players use it, and punishes defection to prevent exploitation. Evolutionary simulations show that CAPRI dominates other strategies in terms of abundance and average payoff across a wide range of benefit-to-cost ratios, error rates, and population sizes. The paper also shows that memory length (m >= 3) is crucial for such strategies. While the main results are for the two-player PD, the authors note that similar strategies exist for the three-person public goods game, suggesting potential generalizability.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that punishment strategies designed to be both efficient and defensible (like CAPRI) can substantially increase group efficiency and average payoff in repeated prisoner's dilemma settings, and by extension, in closely related public goods games. The findings suggest that, when punishment is implemented with sufficient memory and recovery mechanisms, it can robustly improve efficiency regardless of population size, error rate, or benefit-to-cost ratio. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the view that enabling well-designed punishment in repeated games will increase efficiency, especially when players can remember and respond to past actions. However, the results are most directly applicable to two-player repeated PDs, with only indirect evidence for multi-player PG games.

- source: Grimalda, G., Pondorfer, A., & Tracer, D. P. (2016). Social image concerns promote cooperation more than altruistic punishment. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a one-shot, two-player prisoner's dilemma, the presence of a local authority (Big Man) as an observer increases cooperation and leads to the highest average payoffs, exceeding baseline. Introducing a costly punishment stage (where players can spend to reduce the other's payoff) does not increase cooperation above the social image condition and results in lower average payoffs than baseline, due to the costs incurred by both punisher and punished. When both social image and punishment are present, efficiency is still lower than with social image alone and remains below baseline. The presence of an external (outgroup) authority does not increase cooperation or payoffs. Thus, punishment is not an efficient mechanism for promoting cooperation in this setting, and social image concerns are more effective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a one-shot, two-player PD with costly punishment, enabling punishment reduces efficiency compared to baseline (no punishment). The effect is robust: both punishment alone and punishment combined with social image concerns yield lower payoffs than baseline. The design is not a standard public goods game but is closely related (two-player PD with all-or-nothing contribution). The findings suggest that, for similar game designs (one-shot, small group, all-or-nothing, high punishment cost), enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency, even if it increases cooperation rates. The control (baseline) efficiency is higher than the punishment treatment efficiency. This evidence should be weighted heavily when predicting the effect of punishment in similar environments.

- source: Honjo, K., & Kubo, T. (2020). Social Dilemmas in Nature-Based Tourism Depend on Social Value Orientations. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of outcomes in a two-player common-pool resource game depends critically on the social value orientations (SVOs) of the players. When both players are competitive, the Nash equilibrium is often Pareto-inefficient (i.e., group efficiency is low), and this inefficiency is robust to the introduction of fair punishment. In fact, punishment can increase the parameter space in which inefficiency occurs for competitive players. When at least one player is individualistic or prosocial, the Nash equilibrium is almost always Pareto efficient (i.e., high group efficiency). The model demonstrates that punishment does not always improve efficiency and can backfire in certain social contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games is highly sensitive to the social preferences of the players. In environments where players are competitive (i.e., care about outperforming others), introducing fair punishment can worsen efficiency, increasing the likelihood of Pareto-inefficient outcomes. In contrast, with individualistic or prosocial players, efficiency is generally high even without punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee improved efficiency and may even reduce it in competitive settings, especially in small, binary-choice, one-shot games. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, single-round, all-or-nothing, common-pool resource dilemmas, but the underlying logic may transfer to similar public goods environments.

- source: Berger, U., & De Silva, H. (2021). Evolution of deterrence with costly reputation information. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study shows that in a repeated two-player deterrence game (with roles of challenger and defender), the possibility of costly punishment (retaliation) can lead to higher defender payoffs and social welfare, but only when reputation information is minimal (e.g., only the last action is observable and costly to obtain). When reputation information is rich (e.g., challengers can observe a defender's empirical fighting frequency over many rounds), deterrence breaks down, punishment loses its deterrent effect, and defender payoffs collapse. Agent-based simulations confirm that defender payoffs decline as the amount of reputation information increases, with a sharp drop when information becomes too rich. Thus, the efficiency effect of punishment is context-dependent: positive under minimal information, negative or null under rich information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the efficiency effect of punishment in public-goods-like environments depends critically on the information structure: punishment increases efficiency (defender payoffs) when reputation information is minimal and costly, but not when information is rich and easily available. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the design dimension 'information about others' past actions' (analogous to 'show_other_summaries' or 'show_punishment_id') is a key moderator: enabling rich, low-cost information can undermine the efficiency benefits of punishment. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player deterrence games, but the mechanisms may generalize to PGG variants where punishment and reputation interact.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2017). A remarkable effect of the combination of probabilistic peerpunishment and coevolutionary mechanism on the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing probabilistic peer-punishment based on payoff differences, together with a coevolutionary mechanism allowing players to break and form connections, leads to a substantial increase in the average payoff of all players (i.e., group efficiency) in evolutionary prisoner's dilemma games on networks. This effect is especially pronounced in random and scale-free networks and for intermediate values of the punishment coefficient. The combination of these mechanisms expands the parameter range (punishment coefficient r) over which high cooperation and high average payoff are sustained, compared to models with only one or neither mechanism. The effect is less pronounced or absent in regular networks with high average degree.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in evolutionary networked prisoner's dilemma games, enabling a punishment mechanism (specifically, probabilistic peer-punishment based on payoff differences) increases group efficiency (average payoff), especially when combined with a coevolutionary mechanism for network rewiring. The effect is robust across a range of network topologies and average degrees, but is most pronounced in random and scale-free networks and for intermediate punishment cost/effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in similar networked games is likely to increase efficiency, particularly when players can also adjust their social ties. However, the results are from a simulation model, not a human experiment, and the game is a prisoner's dilemma on a network, not a standard public goods game.

- source: Köster, R., Hadfield-Menell, D., Everett, R., Weidinger, L., Hadfield, G. K., & Leibo, J. Z. (2022). Spurious normativity enhances learning of compliance and enforcement behavior in artificial agents. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: Introducing a punishment regime for norm violations (e.g., eating a poisonous berry) enables agents to learn to avoid harmful actions more quickly, resulting in higher group efficiency (collective return) than in the absence of punishment. Adding a second, arbitrary 'silly' rule (punishment for eating a harmless berry) further increases group efficiency during the learning process, as it provides more opportunities for agents to practice enforcement and compliance, leading to faster and more robust avoidance of genuinely harmful actions. The effect is strongest during the intermediate learning phase, with the silly rule condition yielding the highest group payoffs. The study demonstrates that third-party punishment, even when applied to arbitrary rules, can enhance the emergence of efficient, norm-compliant behavior in artificial agent societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a multiagent environment with norm violations increases group efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust even when punishment is costly and decentralized. The design is not a standard public goods game, but the core mechanism—costly third-party punishment of norm violations—closely parallels PGG punishment treatments. The results suggest that, for similar environments, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency, especially when the environment presents a credit assignment problem (delayed or opaque consequences of harmful actions). The effect is further enhanced if the environment provides more opportunities for agents to practice enforcement (e.g., via additional, even arbitrary, punishable actions). The findings are most directly applicable to settings where learning and credit assignment are challenging, and where punishment is visible and incentivized. The results are less directly transferable to standard PGGs with immediate, transparent payoffs, but the qualitative direction (punishment increases efficiency) is likely to hold.

- source: Perry, L., & Gavrilets, S. (2020). Foresight in a Game of Leadership. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a two-player leadership/inspection game, standard rational or learning protocols do not support the emergence of punishment or cooperation, leading to inefficient outcomes (no production, no inspection). However, if leaders use foresight (weighting future payoffs and anticipating subordinates' best responses), then punishment and production can become stable equilibria, resulting in higher payoffs for both leader and group (i.e., higher efficiency). Selective imitation can also support this outcome if subordinates use best response. The effect depends on the weight placed on future payoffs (the foresight parameter) and the cost-benefit structure of punishment and production.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated or multi-round games where leaders (punishers) value future payoffs and anticipate others' responses, punishment can increase group efficiency by enabling cooperation/production that would not occur otherwise. The effect is sensitive to the cost of punishment, the benefit of production, and the degree of foresight. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in games with forward-looking or strategic agents (or mechanisms that mimic foresight/selective imitation) can substantially increase efficiency, especially when the cost of punishment is not too high relative to the benefit. However, in the absence of such mechanisms, punishment may not emerge and efficiency remains low.

- source: Ezeigbo, I. C. (2017). How costly punishment, diversity, and density of connectivity influence cooperation in a biological network. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study uses agent-based models of repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games on networks (18 players, 1000 rounds per game) to examine how costly punishment, diversity, and network connectivity affect cooperation and average payoff. Costly punishment (paying 1 unit to reduce another's payoff by 4 units) reduces defection but also significantly reduces average payoff compared to no-punishment conditions. Decreasing network connectivity (lower density of connections) increases average payoff when punishment is absent, but not when punishment is present. Diversity (randomizing connections and neighbors) can promote cooperation, but only in synergy with low connectivity. The main efficiency result is that costly punishment, while reducing defection, comes at a substantial cost to group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that enabling costly punishment in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-like network games reduces group efficiency (average payoff), even though it reduces defection. The effect is robust across different network densities and diversity conditions. If the control (no-punishment) game has higher efficiency, enabling punishment is likely to lower efficiency, unless other mechanisms (like diversity and low connectivity) are also present and interact synergistically. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in networked social dilemmas with similar payoff structures.

- source: Mondal, S. S., Pathak, M., & Chakraborty, S. (2022). Reward versus punishment: averting the tragedy of the commons in eco-evolutionary dynamics. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment and reward can prevent the tragedy of the commons in a dynamic resource game, but their effects on efficiency differ. Sufficiently strong punishment leads to a stable equilibrium with all cooperators and a fully replete resource, achieving the social optimum (maximum possible group payoff). In contrast, even with strong reward, some defectors persist, so the group does not reach the maximum efficiency. The model also shows that the effect of punishment or reward depends on the initial state and the intensity of the intervention, and that bistability (multiple possible long-run outcomes) can occur. The analysis is theoretical, using replicator dynamics and stability analysis, and is not based on empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in dynamic resource games with feedback (and by extension, in public-goods-like environments), enabling punishment (with sufficient intensity) can increase group efficiency and achieve the social optimum, while enabling reward alone cannot fully eliminate inefficiency due to persistent defectors. The results are most directly applicable to settings where the game is a prisoner's dilemma with resource feedback, and where punishment or reward is implemented as a direct modification of payoffs. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment should be expected to increase efficiency more than enabling reward, especially when the goal is to reach the maximum possible group payoff. However, the model is theoretical and does not address practical implementation details or empirical constraints.

- source: Okada, I. (2020). Two ways to overcome the three social dilemmas of indirect reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity models with private reputation assessment, punishment can increase group efficiency and maintain stable cooperation if assessment norms are designed to (1) detect free riders, (2) justify punishment of bad actors, and (3) generously assess cooperation toward badly reputed recipients. Two main solutions are identified: one based on recipient-focused justified punishment (with generosity toward bad recipients), and another based on doer-focused justified punishment (with high cooperation efficiency required). The stability and efficiency of cooperation depend on the benefit-to-cost ratio (b/c), with more generous norms required at lower efficiency. The presence of punishment, when justified and properly assessed, leads to higher average payoffs and stable cooperative regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends critically on the assessment norm and the efficiency of cooperation (b/c). If the punishment system is designed so that justified punishment is recognized and not misinterpreted (i.e., the intention is correctly communicated), and if generosity is applied in assessing cooperation toward bad recipients, then enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to the control. The benefit-to-cost ratio (mpcr analog) is a key moderator: at higher efficiency, more norms can sustain cooperation. The findings suggest that, for prediction, the presence and design of punishment, the assessment norm, and the efficiency parameter are crucial for forecasting the efficiency gain from punishment.

- source: Duéñez-Guzmán, E. A., & Sadedin, S. (2012). Evolving Righteousness in a Corrupt World. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a small benefit to punishers (such as status or a payment from cooperators) destabilizes corrupt equilibria and enables the evolution and stability of 'righteousness'—a state where cooperative punishers dominate and both corruption and defection are suppressed. Righteousness is associated with higher collective payoffs (efficiency/wellbeing) than either corruption or defection. The model demonstrates that egalitarian and harsh punishment (i.e., similar and sufficiently high penalties for defectors and corrupt punishers) are key to achieving righteousness. Power inequalities that favor corrupt punishers stabilize corruption, but removing or reducing these inequalities (and/or providing small benefits to punishers) can shift the population to righteousness. Once established, righteousness is highly stable and resistant to invasion by defectors or corrupt punishers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in public-goods-like environments, enabling punishment (especially when punishers receive even small additional benefits) can increase group efficiency/wellbeing compared to control conditions without punishment or with only corrupt punishment. The model suggests that the design dimensions most relevant for maximizing efficiency gains from punishment are: (1) ensuring punishers are not disadvantaged relative to cooperators (e.g., via small payments or status), (2) making punishment of defectors and corrupt punishers sufficiently harsh, and (3) minimizing power inequalities that allow corrupt punishers to avoid punishment. While the model is not an exact PGG, its structure and outcomes are highly relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games, especially regarding the role of corruption, punishment severity, and benefits to punishers.

- source: Nirjhor, M. S. A., & Nakamaru, M. (2023). The evolution of cooperation in the unidirectional division of labour on a tree network. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a division-of-labour game on a tree network, without sanctions, the only stable equilibrium is full defection (premierD), resulting in low efficiency. Introducing a defector sanction system (where defectors are punished with probability and severity) can make full cooperation or mixed equilibria stable, depending on the punishment parameters and cost/loss structure. As the effective punishment (probability times fine) increases, the system transitions from defection to mixed states to full cooperation, thus increasing efficiency. The premier sanction system (punishing the root node for any defection) is less effective at promoting cooperation. The model provides explicit stability conditions for each equilibrium, showing how efficiency depends on the sanction system and network structure. Adding more levels to the network (making it deeper) hinders cooperation and efficiency, while adding more parallel branches has an indeterminate effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in public-goods-like division-of-labour games on tree networks, enabling a defector-targeted punishment system increases group efficiency compared to the no-punishment baseline, provided the punishment is sufficiently strong (high probability and/or fine). The effect is mediated by the cost of cooperation, the loss from defection, and the network structure. The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency (no punishment), introducing punishment will increase efficiency if the punishment is strong enough, but the effect may be partial (mixed equilibria) if punishment is moderate. The model also predicts that deeper (more levels) networks make it harder for punishment to achieve full cooperation. These insights can inform predictions of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency, especially in settings with hierarchical or supply-chain-like structure.

- source: Wang, X. L., Wu, J., Shu, G., & Li, Y. (2014). Punishment Based on Public Benefit Fund Significantly Promotes Cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment funded by a public benefit fund (taxes on high earners) significantly increases both cooperation and average payoff (efficiency) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. Slight punishment (low intensity, broad coverage) is more effective than severe punishment (high intensity, narrow coverage) in raising average payoff. The effect of punishment is stronger when the temptation to defect is higher. The results are robust across square lattice and small world network topologies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong simulation-based evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism (with costs funded by a public benefit fund) increases group efficiency (average payoff) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game, compared to no punishment. The effect is stronger for slight punishment (low intensity, broad coverage) than for severe punishment (high intensity, narrow coverage). The findings are most directly relevant to spatial PD games with local interactions, not standard PGGs, but the efficiency outcome is directly reported. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (especially with broad coverage and moderate intensity) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, especially as the temptation to defect increases.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2016). Evolution of cooperation by the introduction of the probabilistic peer-punishment based on the difference of payoff. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a probabilistic peer-punishment mechanism, where punishment is more likely and more severe when the payoff difference between punisher and target is larger, leads to a substantial increase in both cooperation and average payoff in spatial prisoner's dilemma simulations. This effect is robust across regular, random, and scale-free network topologies, and for different average degrees of connectivity. The model demonstrates that, contrary to some previous findings with standard peer-punishment, this form of punishment can increase group welfare (average payoff) rather than decrease it.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments, a punishment mechanism that is probabilistic and based on payoff differences can increase average payoff, in contrast to standard fixed-cost peer punishment which often reduces group payoff. While the model is not a public goods game, the findings are relevant for predicting the effect of punishment in adjacent game structures, especially where punishment is adaptive or conditional on payoff differences. The results suggest that the design of the punishment mechanism (e.g., making it proportional to payoff differences and probabilistic) is a key moderator of its effect on efficiency. However, direct quantitative predictions for PGGs should be made with caution due to the difference in game structure.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., Iwasa, Y., & Nowak, M. A. (2009). Indirect reciprocity provides only a narrow margin of efficiency for costly punishment. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in models of indirect reciprocity, strategies that use costly punishment (punishing defectors at a cost to oneself) are almost always less efficient than strategies that simply withhold cooperation from defectors. Costly punishment can only be the most efficient equilibrium in a very small region of parameter space, requiring fine-tuned values of benefit, cost, punishment cost, punishment impact, and reputation accuracy. In most cases, the highest group payoff (efficiency) is achieved by not using costly punishment. The results are robust to errors in reputation assignment and action execution, as long as these errors are not too frequent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in indirect reciprocity settings (which are adjacent to public goods games), enabling costly punishment will almost always reduce group efficiency compared to not enabling punishment, except in a very narrow set of parameter values. For prediction tasks, this suggests that unless the game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, punishment impact, benefit/cost ratio, reputation accuracy) are in the narrow region identified, enabling punishment should be expected to lower efficiency. The findings are most directly applicable to indirect reciprocity games with binary reputation and second-order norms, but the logic may transfer to PGG-like environments with similar structure.

- source: Nirjhor, M. S. A., & Nakamaru, M. (2023). The evolution of cooperation in the unidirectional linear division of labour of finite roles. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a linear division-of-labor game, the introduction of a defector sanction system (punishing defectors with a fine, with some probability of detection) can stabilize the all-cooperation equilibrium and thus maximize group efficiency, provided the expected punishment is sufficiently high relative to the cost of cooperation. The first role sanction system (punishing only the first role regardless of who defects) is less effective and only promotes cooperation when the cost of cooperation decreases downstream. The benefit from cooperation does not affect the stability of cooperation or efficiency. The model provides explicit conditions under which each equilibrium is stable, showing that punishment can transform a system from one where defection is stable (and efficiency is low) to one where cooperation and high efficiency are stable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical support for predicting that, in public-goods-like environments with sequential or asymmetric roles, introducing a punishment mechanism that targets defectors (with sufficient expected punishment) will increase group efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust to the number of roles (player count) and the cost structure, provided the expected punishment exceeds the maximum cooperation cost. The findings suggest that the design of the punishment system (who is punished, detection probability, and fine size) is critical for achieving efficiency gains. The results are most directly applicable to settings with sequential dependencies and asymmetric roles, but the logic may transfer to some PGG variants. The model does not address repeated finite rounds, communication, or stochastic group sizes, so predictions should be cautious if those features are present.

- source: Ito, H., & Yoshimura, J. (2015). Social penalty promotes cooperation in a cooperative society. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a social penalty (punishment) for non-cooperative actions in already-cooperative societies universally increases cooperation and group welfare in theoretical models of the hawk-dove and prisoner's dilemma games. The penalty reduces the payoff of non-cooperators, shifting the equilibrium toward more cooperation. The effect is robust to the size of the penalty: larger penalties can lead to full cooperation. The results are generalized to non-cooperative games, with the claim that social penalties always promote cooperation and never reduce it. The paper also discusses the implications for real-world legal systems and public goods provision, but does not model or simulate public goods games directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that introducing or increasing punishment for non-cooperation will increase group efficiency and average payoff in non-cooperative games, including those adjacent to public goods games. The results are derived analytically and show that the effect of punishment is always non-negative for cooperation and group welfare. However, the models are not public goods games per se, and do not address multi-player, repeated, or more complex institutional features. The findings are most directly applicable to settings where punishment is externally imposed and not subject to strategic choice by players. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to control, especially as punishment becomes more severe or certain.

- source: De Dreu, C. K. W., Gross, J., Méder, Z., Giffin, M., Prochazkova, E., Krikeb, J., & Columbus, S. (2016). In-group defense, out-group aggression, and coordination failures in intergroup conflict. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Peer punishment increases contributions to out-group aggression but does not improve the aggressor group's success rate or overall group payoffs; it leads to more wasted resources and lower individual wealth. In contrast, sequential decision-making (a coordination mechanism) increases coordination within aggressor groups, nearly doubles their success rate, and reduces wealth loss for aggressors (but increases it for defenders). The results suggest that punishment can motivate higher contributions but does not solve the coordination problem or improve efficiency, while coordination mechanisms can enhance group success and payoffs in competitive intergroup settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in intergroup contest games with costly contributions and the possibility of peer punishment, enabling punishment increases contributions but does not improve group efficiency or payoffs—indeed, it can reduce them due to the direct costs of punishment. Coordination mechanisms (like sequential decision-making) are more effective at improving group success and payoffs. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment (with the cost/impact structure used here) should not be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, and may even decrease it. The effect of punishment is highly sensitive to the coordination context and the cost/impact structure of punishment.

- source: Fehr, E., & Rockenbach, B. (2003). Detrimental effects of sanctions on human altruism. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
