  findings: In a one-shot sequential trust game, allowing the investor to impose a fine on the trustee for insufficient back-transfer leads to lower back-transfers from trustees and lower payoffs for investors, compared to both a baseline with no sanctions and to cases where the investor refrains from using the fine. The negative effect of sanctions is especially pronounced when the investor's desired back-transfer is high (i.e., when the sanction is used to enforce an unfair distribution). The paper interprets this as evidence that sanctions perceived as self-interested or unfair undermine altruistic cooperation, and thus can reduce overall efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a sequential trust game (not a standard PGG), enabling punishment (sanctions) can reduce efficiency-related outcomes (investor payoff, group surplus) compared to a no-punishment baseline, especially when punishment is perceived as self-interested or unfair. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is context-dependent: in trust games with one-shot interactions and potential for perceived unfairness, punishment may reduce efficiency. The findings are less directly transferable to standard PGGs, but highlight the importance of the perceived legitimacy and purpose of punishment as a moderator.

- source: Wang, S. X., Chen, X. J., Xiao, Z. L., Szolnoki, A., & Vasconcelos, V. V. (2023). Optimization of institutional incentives for cooperation in structured populations. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both institutional punishment and reward can be tuned to achieve near-full cooperation in structured populations, with the optimal protocol being time-invariant and identical for both types of incentives under a given update rule. The minimal cumulative cost to the institution depends on the initial fraction of cooperators: punishment is more cost-effective when initial cooperation is high, while reward is more cost-effective when initial cooperation is low. The results are supported by analytical derivations and simulations across regular, random, small-world, and scale-free networks. The main outcome is the cumulative cost to the institution, which is a proxy for efficiency from the perspective of implementing cooperation-promoting interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how the cost-effectiveness of punishment (and reward) depends on game parameters such as network structure, update rule, and initial cooperation level. While it does not report group efficiency in the standard sense, its focus on the cumulative cost to achieve high cooperation is closely related and can inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in similar structured games. The results suggest that, with optimally tuned punishment, high cooperation (and thus high group efficiency) can be achieved at lower institutional cost when initial cooperation is already substantial. The findings are most directly applicable to structured Prisoner's Dilemma settings with institutional (not peer) punishment or reward, and may be less directly transferable to standard public goods games.

- source: CLUTTONBROCK, T. H., & PARKER, G. A. (1995). PUNISHMENT IN ANIMAL SOCIETIES. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Theoretical models show that punishment can be stable and effective in animal societies, especially when dominants can punish subordinates at low cost and with high impact. Punishment can enforce cooperation, deter cheating, and maintain social order. The models identify conditions under which punishment is likely to evolve, such as strong asymmetries in power or fighting ability. Empirical examples illustrate that punishment is widespread in animal societies and can modify behavior, but the paper does not report quantitative effects on group efficiency or welfare. The findings suggest that punishment can increase the fitness of punishers by reducing the frequency of damaging actions by others, but do not directly address group-level efficiency as in PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical support for the idea that punishment can enforce cooperation and deter selfishness, which is relevant to predicting the effects of punishment in PGG-like games. However, the models are dyadic, focus on fitness rather than explicit group payoff, and do not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes. The paper suggests that punishment is more likely to be effective when the cost to the punisher is low and the impact on the punished is high, and when there are clear asymmetries in power. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the plausibility of positive effects of punishment on cooperation and (by analogy) efficiency, but does not provide direct empirical or quantitative evidence for use in predictive modeling.

- source: Helbing, D., Yu, W. J., Opp, K. D., & Rauhut, H. (2014). Conditions for the Emergence of Shared Norms in Populations with Incompatible Preferences. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment (sanctioning) is a powerful mechanism for establishing shared norms in heterogeneous populations, even when preferences are incompatible. Behavior-based punishment is more effective than preference-based punishment in creating conformity. However, the resulting norm is not always efficient: sometimes a minority can establish an unpopular or suboptimal norm, and average payoff may not be maximized. Adaptive group pressure (where local majorities punish minorities) can lead to spontaneous norm emergence, but again, the content of the norm may be arbitrary and not welfare-maximizing. The model highlights the importance of path dependence, local interaction, and randomness in norm formation. Payoff (welfare) is explicitly tracked, and the model demonstrates that sanctioning can both increase and decrease average payoff depending on the scenario.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency (payoff) in public-goods-like environments is highly context-dependent. Punishment can increase conformity and create shared norms, but does not guarantee higher efficiency: the emergent norm may be suboptimal. The model suggests that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its cost, effectiveness, and whether it is behavior- or preference-based. Local interaction and adaptive group pressure can facilitate norm emergence, but do not ensure welfare maximization. For prediction, this paper suggests that enabling punishment will often increase conformity but may have mixed or even negative effects on efficiency, especially if the norm that emerges is not aligned with group welfare. The model's adjacent structure to PGGs means its findings are suggestive but not directly quantitative for standard PGG efficiency prediction.

- source: Zhao, K., Ferguson, E., & Smillie, L. D. (2017). Politeness and Compassion Differentially Predict Adherence to Fairness Norms and Interventions to Norm Violations in Economic Games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that politeness and honesty-humility predict fair allocations in the dictator game, while compassion predicts third-party recompensation (helping the victim) and, to a lesser extent, third-party punishment (punishing the offender). Most third-party punishers are 'prosocial' (they also behave fairly as dictators), and both punishment and recompensation increase with the degree of unfairness observed. However, the study does not analyze or report group efficiency, total welfare, or surplus, and the game structure is not a public goods game but a dictator/third-party punishment/recompensation design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it offers insight into the psychological predictors of punishment and recompensation behaviors in third-party games, it does not report group-level efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or analyze public goods game design dimensions such as MPCR, player count, or rounds. Its findings are only indirectly relevant for understanding the motivations behind punishment and helping behaviors, not their impact on group efficiency.

- source: Bone, J. E., Wallace, B., Bshary, R., & Raihani, N. J. (2016). Power Asymmetries and Punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma with Variable Cooperative Investment. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a two-player iterated prisoner's dilemma with variable cooperative investment and a punishment option, punishment did not increase the likelihood that targets would increase their cooperation, regardless of whether the punisher was strong or weak, or whether the game was symmetric or asymmetric. Both weak and strong players retaliated against punishment, and the threat of punishment from strong players may have led to higher baseline cooperation from weak players, but actual punishment had no effect on subsequent cooperation. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral responses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in two-player games with variable investment and a punishment option, enabling punishment does not increase cooperation or efficiency, even when punishment is more effective for some players (power asymmetry). However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The behavioral results suggest that, in similar two-player designs, enabling punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency and may provoke retaliation. The findings are most relevant for adjacent game designs (iterated PD with variable investment) and may be less transferable to standard public goods games or multiplayer settings.

- source: Martin, J. W., & Cushman, F. (2015). To Punish or to Leave: Distinct Cognitive Processes Underlie Partner Control and Partner Choice Behaviors. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a dyadic allocation game with stochastic outcomes, punishment (partner control) is influenced by both the allocator's intent and the actual outcome, while partner choice (switching partners) is influenced almost exclusively by intent. The design allows for the separation of intent and outcome by having allocators choose between dice with different probabilities of fair/selfish outcomes. Responders in the punishment condition could add or subtract money (costlessly, with low probability of effect), while in the partner choice condition they could choose to stay or switch partners. The main dependent variables are the behavioral responses and their sensitivity to intent/outcome. No group-level efficiency or payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or similar games. Its main contribution is to clarify the psychological determinants of punishment and partner choice behaviors, showing that punishment is sensitive to both intent and outcome, while partner choice is intent-driven. The game is not a standard public goods game, and efficiency or total payoff is not analyzed. Thus, the paper offers little direct support for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments, but may inform understanding of when punishment is likely to be used and what cues drive it.

- source: Philippsen, A., Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2024). Time pressure and deliberation affect moral punishment. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The main findings are that (1) time pressure reduces the probability of moral punishment, (2) deliberation increases the probability of moral punishment, and (3) the content of deliberation matters: deliberating fairness increases, while deliberating self-interest decreases, moral punishment. These effects are specific to moral punishment and do not consistently affect other types of punishment or cooperation. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so the direct impact of these manipulations on group efficiency is not assessed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how cognitive constraints and deliberation affect the likelihood of moral punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma with punishment, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results suggest that manipulations increasing deliberation (e.g., removing time pressure, encouraging fairness-based reflection) are likely to increase the use of moral punishment, which in other literature is often associated with higher cooperation and potentially higher efficiency. However, because efficiency is not measured, the paper's findings should be used as indirect evidence about the behavioral mechanisms (punishment rates) rather than direct evidence about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Philippsen, A., Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2024). People punish defection, not failures to conform to the majority. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiments show that people are more likely to cooperate and to punish defectors (moral punishment) when the majority of partners are cooperative. Antisocial punishment (punishing cooperators) is much less common and does not increase when defection is the majority behavior, contradicting a pure conformity account. The presence of partner punishment (Experiment 1) increases cooperation rates above the base rate, while its absence (Experiment 2) leads to cooperation rates more closely matching the environment. The study does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but the final account balances suggest that punishment and cooperation patterns affect individual earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how punishment is used in dyadic Prisoner's Dilemma games, showing that punishment is primarily directed at defectors and is sensitive to the prevalence of cooperation. However, it does not directly report or analyze efficiency or group payoff, so its value for predicting efficiency in public goods games is limited. The design is adjacent to PGGs: it is a repeated, one-shot, dyadic PD with a costly punishment option, not a multi-player PGG. The punishment mechanism is well specified (costly, with a fixed cost-to-impact ratio). The findings suggest that enabling punishment increases cooperation, especially when the environment is cooperative, but the effect on efficiency is not directly measured. For prediction, this paper supports the idea that punishment can increase cooperation and likely efficiency in similar dyadic games, but caution is needed in extrapolating to multi-player PGGs.

- source: Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2021). Cognitive load decreases cooperation and moral punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma game with punishment option. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that cognitive load reduces both cooperation and moral punishment in a one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma game with punishment. Hypocritical and antisocial punishment are not significantly affected by cognitive load, but a general punishment bias (punishing regardless of partner behavior) increases under cognitive load. The amount spent on punishment, when punishment occurs, does not differ between conditions. The results suggest that both cooperation and moral punishment require cognitive resources and are not purely intuitive. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a two-player one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment option, the presence of punishment (and its purposeful use) depends on cognitive resources. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most relevant for understanding how cognitive load moderates the behavioral use of punishment and cooperation, not their impact on group efficiency. The game is a Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game, so transfer to PGG settings should be cautious.

- source: Arai, S., Tooby, J., & Cosmides, L. (2022). Motivations to reciprocate cooperation and punish defection are calibrated by estimates of how easily others can switch partners. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that participants who perceive higher relational mobility (i.e., that others can easily switch partners) reciprocate more and punish less in a dyadic trust game with punishment. The opportunity to switch partners (experimental manipulation) did not affect punishment, but did interact with relational mobility to affect reciprocation in American participants. Punishment did not increase subsequent cooperation and made punished partners more likely to leave. The main outcomes are behavioral (reciprocation, punishment, switching), not efficiency or group payoff. Payoff tables are provided, but efficiency is not a dependent variable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting efficiency in punishment-enabled games: higher perceived partner choice (relational mobility) leads to more reciprocation and less punishment, which could plausibly increase efficiency, but efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured or analyzed. The game is a dyadic trust game with punishment, not a standard public goods game, so findings are adjacent but not directly transferable. The paper is most informative about how punishment and partner choice cues affect individual behavior, not aggregate efficiency.

- source: Pansini, R., Shi, L., & Wang, R. W. (2016). Women Tend to Defect in a Social Dilemma Game in Southwest China. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated dyadic Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment option, women in Southwest China defect more and cooperate less than men, a pattern that contrasts with previous findings from Western and other Asian samples. Both genders earned less when they punished, and there was no significant gender difference in overall monetary earnings. The use of punishment did not increase individual earnings, and the paper does not analyze group efficiency or total welfare. The main findings are about behavioral strategies and gender differences, not about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in a dyadic iterated Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment option, punishment is used more frequently than in comparable Western samples, but its use is associated with lower individual earnings. The design is not a public goods game but a two-player PD, so direct prediction for PGG efficiency is limited. The main decision support is that punishment, as implemented here, does not increase individual payoffs and may reduce them. The paper does not report group efficiency or the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments is limited.

- source: Bone, J. E., Wallace, B., Bshary, R., & Raihani, N. J. (2015). The Effect of Power Asymmetries on Cooperation and Punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a two-player iterated prisoner's dilemma with punishment, punishment did not increase cooperation rates. Instead, cooperation from a partner was more effective at eliciting cooperation than punishment. Punishment, especially from strong players, sometimes provoked retaliation and further defection. Weak players never retaliated against punishment, but both weak and strong players retaliated against strong punishers. Players were more likely to opt out after being punished or when facing a strong partner. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, and the authors explicitly state their focus is on behavioral rather than payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in two-player iterated prisoner's dilemma games with punishment, enabling punishment does not increase cooperation and may even have detrimental effects on cooperative behavior, especially when power asymmetries exist. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings suggest that, at least in two-player, all-or-nothing, binary-choice games with possible opting out, punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency and may worsen behavioral outcomes. Caution should be used in generalizing to multi-player or continuous-contribution public goods games.

- source: Loustau, T., Glassman, J., Martin, J. W., Young, L., & Mcauliffe, K. (2024). The impact of group membership on punishment versus partner rejection. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across two preregistered online experiments, participants responded to resource allocation decisions by either punishing (subtracting from the allocator's bonus) or rejecting (choosing not to interact again). In both a minimal group paradigm and a real-world political group context, participants punished outgroup members more than ingroup members, especially when the allocator's intent was selfish and the outcome was unequal. However, group membership did not affect the likelihood of partner rejection. The main outcomes are punishment and rejection rates; no efficiency or group payoff measures are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods or similar games with and without punishment. The design is adjacent to PGGs but focuses on dyadic allocation games with punishment and partner rejection as responses. The main contribution is to show that group membership increases punishment of outgroup members, but this is not linked to efficiency or welfare outcomes. Thus, the paper is not directly informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments, but it does provide evidence that group membership can moderate punishment behavior.

- source: Makovi, K., Sargsyan, A., Li, W. D., Bonnefon, J. F., & Rahwan, T. (2023). Trust within human-machine collectives depends on the perceived consensus about cooperative norms. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a third-party punishment game followed by a trust game, humans earn less trust by sharing with or punishing bots than with humans. Trust-gain is attenuated but not eliminated in mixed human-bot collectives. Perceived consensus about cooperative norms predicts trust-gain, and experimentally making norm consensus salient increases trust-gain in some conditions. The main outcomes are trust and norm perception, not group efficiency or total payoff. The design is adjacent to public goods games, but the outcome focus is on trust and norm-following rather than efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its design is adjacent (third-party punishment game with binary sharing), and the main outcomes are trust-gain and norm perception, not efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that punishment can increase trust in norm-followers, especially when norm consensus is salient, but do not quantify effects on group efficiency. The paper may inform mechanism design around norm salience and trust, but is not directly informative for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Bernhard, H., Fischbacher, U., & Fehr, E. (2006). Parochial altruism in humans. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment is strongly parochial: punishers spend more to punish norm violations when the victim is from their own group, regardless of the violator's group. Dictators (the potential norm violators) expect more punishment and transfer more to the recipient when the recipient and the third party are from the same group. When the third party is from the dictator's group, dictators expect less punishment and transfer less. The results suggest that the threat of punishment is not impartial but is shaped by group membership, leading to greater protection for ingroup victims. The paper does not report on efficiency or total group payoff, so its findings are not directly about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness and application of punishment in norm enforcement are highly sensitive to group affiliations, with punishment being much more likely and severe when the victim and punisher are from the same group. However, because the experiment is a one-shot dictator game with third-party punishment (not a repeated public goods game), and because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments is limited. It is informative about the social and psychological moderators of punishment, especially parochialism, but not about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Spadaro, G., Molho, C., Van Prooijen, J. W., Romano, A., Mosso, C. O., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2023). Corrupt third parties undermine trust and prosocial behaviour between people. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across five experimental studies, participants exposed to a corrupt (dishonest) third-party punisher (TPP) trusted strangers less and cooperated less in subsequent economic games (trust game, prisoner's dilemma, dictator game) with TPP present. The effect is robust to both second-hand and first-hand exposure to corruption, and to whether the TPP's corruption harms only the experiment or also a collective good. The negative effect of perceived TPP corruption on cooperation is mediated by reduced interpersonal trust. The studies do not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect of TPP corruption on efficiency is not measured. The design always includes a TPP; there is no comparison to a no-punishment baseline, so the effect of enabling punishment per se on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the perceived integrity of a third-party punisher moderates the effectiveness of punishment institutions in promoting trust and cooperation. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of enabling punishment (vs. no punishment) on group efficiency or payoff, nor does it report efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that if punishment is administered by corrupt or untrusted third parties, its positive effect on cooperation may be undermined or even reversed, but the paper does not quantify efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper is most informative about the moderating role of institutional trustworthiness, not about the baseline effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games.

- source: Chen, H. Z., Zeng, Z. J., & Ma, J. H. (2020). The source of punishment matters: Third-party punishment restrains observers from selfish behaviors better than does second-party punishment by shaping norm perceptions. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment (TPP) of unfair behavior in a dictator game leads observers to perceive higher descriptive and injunctive norms for sharing, and to share more themselves, compared to second-party punishment (SPP). Mediation analyses show that the effect on sharing is primarily through changes in descriptive norm perception. The effect is robust across two experiments with different manipulations (verbal blame and monetary punishment). However, the outcome is individual sharing behavior, not group efficiency or payoff, and the context is a dictator game, not a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the source of punishment (third-party vs. second-party) can affect observers' prosocial behavior by shaping norm perceptions, especially descriptive norms. While the findings are robust for sharing behavior in dictator games, they do not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. The results suggest that punishment mechanisms that are perceived as norm-expressive (e.g., third-party punishment) may have stronger effects on prosocial behavior, but the transferability to efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments is indirect and should be treated with caution.

- source: Bone, J. E., McAuliffe, K., & Raihani, N. J. (2016). Exploring the Motivations for Punishment: Framing and Country-Level Effects. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment behavior in a two-player stealing game is strongly influenced by country of origin. US-based participants are more likely to punish in response to both losses and disadvantageous inequality, while India-based participants are more punitive overall and engage in more antisocial punishment, but only increase punishment when experiencing disadvantageous inequality. Methodological factors such as stake size and punishment cost (within the tested range) do not significantly affect punishment rates. The study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, and all outcome measures are behavioral (punishment frequency) rather than payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report or analyze efficiency, welfare, or total earnings. However, it provides informative evidence about how punishment behavior varies with country of origin and with the presence of disadvantageous inequality, which may be relevant for understanding when punishment is likely to be used. The design is adjacent to PGGs (two-player stealing game with punishment), but not a PGG or threshold public goods game. The findings suggest that punishment rates and motives can vary substantially by context and population, which may moderate any efficiency effects in related games.

- source: D'Orsogna, M. R., Kendall, R., McBride, M., & Short, M. B. (2013). Criminal Defectors Lead to the Emergence of Cooperation in an Experimental, Adversarial Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiments show that the availability of the Informant strategy (criminals who also report/punish other criminals) is necessary for the emergence and stability of high cooperation in an adversarial game. When Informants are allowed, the system reliably transitions to and maintains a high-cooperation state (Utopia), regardless of initial conditions or parameter profile. When Informants are not allowed, the system converges to high defection (Dystopia). The results are robust across different initializations, parameter profiles (costs of reporting), and levels of behavioral inertia. However, the study does not report group payoffs, efficiency, or welfare as outcome variables.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the availability of a punishment mechanism (specifically, the Informant strategy) is crucial for the emergence and maintenance of cooperation in adversarial games. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of behavioral strategies rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The game is adjacent to PGGs, and the behavioral results suggest that enabling punishment (especially by defectors) can transform a high-defection environment into a high-cooperation one, which would likely increase efficiency if measured. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the precision of this inference.

- source: Li, J., Xiao, E., Houser, D., & Montague, P. R. (2009). Neural responses to sanction threats in two-party economic exchange. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a two-player repeated trust game, the threat of monetary sanctions (punishment) leads trustees to return significantly less to investors compared to when no sanction is threatened. This effect is robust across fair and unfair investor requests. Neural data show that the presence of a sanction threat reduces activation in brain regions associated with social reward (VMPFC, LOFC, amygdala) and increases activation in regions associated with rational, utility-based decision making (parietal cortex). The findings support the 'perception shift' hypothesis: sanction threats crowd out norm-based motivations and shift behavior toward self-interested, utility-maximizing choices. The study does not report group efficiency or total earnings, but the reduction in reciprocity implies a likely reduction in efficiency relative to the no-sanction condition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-player repeated trust game, the introduction of a punishment (sanction) threat reduces cooperative behavior (reciprocity) by trustees. While the design is not a standard public goods game, the mechanism—punishment crowding out intrinsic motivation—may generalize to PGG-like settings. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment can reduce efficiency or group payoff in environments where cooperation is norm-driven and where punishment is perceived as a signal of distrust or as crowding out intrinsic motivation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or total earnings, the effect on efficiency must be inferred from the reduction in reciprocity. The findings are most relevant for two-player, repeated, sequential games with optional punishment, and less directly for standard multi-player PGGs.

- source: Philippsen, A., Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2023). Communicating emotions, but not expressing them privately, reduces moral punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across two experiments, the authors find that giving participants the opportunity to communicate their emotions to their partner after each round of a Prisoner's Dilemma game with costly punishment reduces the rate of moral punishment compared to a control condition. However, this reduction is small, and moral punishment remains common. Privately expressing emotions (without communication) does not reduce moral punishment. Both emotion communication and private expression can reduce cooperation and increase other forms of punishment (hypocritical punishment, punishment bias). The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as a function of punishment, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on how emotion communication and private expression affect punishment behavior in a repeated one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma with punishment. However, it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings as a function of punishment, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment rates (especially moral punishment) can be reduced by allowing emotion communication, but the effect is small. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a two-player Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment stage. The results are most useful for understanding moderators of punishment behavior, not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2021). Moral labels increase cooperation and costly punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma game with punishment option. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that moral framing (using labels like 'I cooperate' and 'I cheat') increases the likelihood of both moral and hypocritical punishment compared to neutral framing ('A' and 'B'). In Experiment 1, where only participants could punish, moral framing increased punishment but did not affect cooperation. In Experiment 2, where both participants and partners could punish, moral framing increased both punishment and cooperation. Antisocial punishment and general punishment bias were not affected by framing. The results suggest that the effect of moral framing on cooperation is contingent on the presence of punishment. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes as a function of punishment, so its direct relevance to efficiency prediction is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and framing of punishment options can increase both punishment behavior and, under certain conditions, cooperation in a Prisoner's Dilemma game. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a two-player Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment stage, but the findings about behavioral responses to punishment and framing may be informative for predicting when punishment will increase cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) in similar environments. The results suggest that punishment is more likely to increase cooperation when it is symmetric (i.e., both players can punish), and that moral framing amplifies this effect.

- source: Jordan, J. J., & Kteily, N. S. (2023). How reputation does (and does not) drive people to punish without looking. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that reputational incentives can increase the likelihood that individuals engage in punishment (signing punitive petitions), and as a byproduct, some of this punishment occurs without considering opposing perspectives. However, when the process of 'looking' is made observable, individuals are more likely to consider opposing perspectives, and punishment without looking does not increase. Evaluators (the audience) reward punishment in general, but do not reward punishment without looking; in fact, they see it as less fair and competent. The effect is stronger with more ideological audiences. The study does not use a public goods game or measure group efficiency, but rather focuses on reputational and behavioral outcomes in a Dictator Game context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or social dilemmas. Its design is adjacent: it uses a reputational Dictator Game with real-world punitive actions (signing petitions) and manipulates observability of punishment and information-gathering. The main outcomes are behavioral (punishment, looking) and reputational (evaluations, money shared as a reward), not group efficiency or welfare. The findings suggest that making punishment observable increases punishment (and punishment without looking), but making deliberation observable increases deliberation. For prediction tasks about efficiency in PGGs, this paper provides only indirect, contextual evidence about how reputational incentives can increase punishment rates, but not about their effect on group efficiency or welfare.

- source: Cabrales, A., Kendall, R., & Sánchez, A. (2022). The effectiveness of prosocial policies: Gender differences arising from social norms. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that policy interventions (framing, exogenous punishment, endogenous punishment) do not reduce the overall average speed (a proxy for collective safety) compared to control. However, there are strong gender differences: policies reduce risky (Fast) choices and average speed among female participants, but increase them among males. This results in no net improvement in group-level outcomes and increases gender inequality in payoffs. The effect is mediated by differences in social norms and normative expectations. The most effective policy for changing female behavior is endogenous punishment, but male participants respond by increasing risky behavior, offsetting the gains. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff in the public goods sense, and the main outcomes are behavioral and norm-related.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the effect of punishment mechanisms (both exogenous and endogenous) on group-level efficiency or payoff in public-goods-like environments may be highly heterogeneous and context-dependent, especially along gender lines. In this specific driving game, punishment does not improve group-level efficiency (as proxied by average speed or safety) because positive effects for one subgroup (females) are offset by negative effects for another (males). The design is not a standard PGG, and efficiency is not directly measured, so the findings should be transferred with caution. The main implication for prediction is that punishment may not always increase efficiency, especially if there are strong heterogeneities in norm compliance or behavioral response. The study highlights the importance of considering subgroup responses and social norms when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Krasnow, M. M., Cosmides, L., Pedersen, E. J., & Tooby, J. (2012). What Are Punishment and Reputation for?. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiments show that (1) subjects use third-party reputation to regulate trust only when first-party (personal) information is unavailable; (2) punishment is not more likely to be directed at partners with a reputation for norm violation; (3) subjects are more likely to cooperate with defectors whom they have punished, consistent with punishment as a bargaining tool for future cooperation; (4) when deciding to cooperate, subjects rely on the partner's most recent behavior toward themselves, not on past reputation. The results support social exchange theory over group norm maintenance theory in the context of dyadic trust games with punishment. No direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological function of punishment in dyadic trust games, showing that punishment is used to bargain for better treatment rather than to enforce group norms. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game or a multi-player setting. The findings suggest that in small-scale, dyadic games, the presence of punishment may not increase group efficiency, but rather serves to regulate individual relationships. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on whether the game context is dyadic or group-based, and on the psychological mechanisms engaged. The design dimensions most relevant here are the dyadic structure, the use of reputation, and the role of punishment as a bargaining tool.

- source: Henrich, J., McElreath, R., Barr, A., Ensminger, J., Barrett, C., Bolyanatz, A., Cardenas, J. C., Gurven, M., Gwako, E., Henrich, N., Lesorogol, C., Marlowe, F. W., Tracer, D., & Ziker, J. (2006). Costly punishment across human societies. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that costly punishment (both second-party and third-party) is a widespread human behavior, but its prevalence and intensity vary substantially across societies. In all populations, more unequal offers are punished more frequently, but the threshold for punishment and the willingness to punish differ. There is a strong positive correlation between a population's willingness to punish and its average altruistic behavior (as measured by dictator game offers). The findings support theories of gene-culture coevolution in human cooperation. However, the paper does not use public goods games and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so its direct relevance to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that costly punishment is a robust and variable feature of human behavior across societies, and that it is positively associated with altruism. However, because the experiments are ultimatum and third-party punishment games (not public goods games), and because efficiency or group payoff is not reported, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The main implication is that the effect of punishment on cooperation-related behaviors is likely to be highly variable across populations, and that willingness to punish is linked to local norms of altruism. The paper does not provide direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or payoff in repeated or group-based public goods settings.

- source: Weiss, M., Saulin, A., Iotzov, V., Hewig, J., & Hein, G. (2023). Can monetary incentives overturn fairness-based decisions?. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that offering monetary incentives that conflict with fairness-based punishment or compensation reduces the frequency of fairness-congruent decisions and slows the decision process (lower drift rate in DDM). This effect is similar for both punishment and compensation domains. The effect of conflicting incentives is stronger at higher incentive levels. The study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, but rather focuses on behavioral and cognitive process outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that monetary incentives can undermine fairness-based punishment and compensation behavior, reducing the likelihood and efficiency of such decisions. However, because the study does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design is adjacent to PGGs, using a modified dictator game with punishment/compensation framing, but lacks repeated interaction, group structure, or explicit efficiency measures. The main predictive value is in showing that the cost and alignment of incentives can strongly moderate the behavioral impact of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Morris, A., MacGlashan, J., Littman, M. L., & Cushman, F. (2017). Evolution of flexibility and rigidity in retaliatory punishment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that when punishment is costly, flexible (learning-based) victims are less likely to learn to punish, making them more vulnerable in repeated interactions. This leads to the evolution of rigid punishment strategies (i.e., always punish theft), even when such punishment is costly and not always effective. The learning dynamics of reinforcement learning agents show that thieves are more likely to adapt flexibly, while victims tend to persist in punishment. The evolutionary simulations confirm that the cost of punishment biases selection toward rigid punishment. The findings are primarily about the evolution and persistence of punishment strategies, not about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence about the evolution of punishment strategies in repeated dyadic games, focusing on the interplay between learning dynamics and evolutionary selection. While it does not directly address efficiency or group payoff in public goods games, it is informative about when rigid punishment is likely to evolve and persist, especially when punishment is costly. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper suggests that the cost of punishment and the learning dynamics of participants may influence the prevalence of rigid punishment, but it does not provide direct evidence about efficiency outcomes. Its relevance is mainly in understanding the conditions under which punishment is likely to be rigid or flexible, which may indirectly affect efficiency in related environments.

- source: Xiao, E., & Houser, D. (2005). Emotion expression in human punishment behavior. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that when responders in the ultimatum game are allowed to send a message to proposers expressing their feelings about the offer, the rejection rate of unfair offers (≤20% to responder) drops from 60% (no emotion expression allowed) to 32% (emotion expression allowed). This effect is statistically significant. The ability to express negative emotions through messages appears to reduce the use of costly punishment. The distribution of offers by proposers does not differ between treatments, indicating that the effect is due to responder behavior. The study does not report on efficiency or total group payoff, but the behavioral change would mechanically increase total earnings by reducing the frequency of zero-payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that, in environments where costly punishment is possible, introducing a non-costly channel for emotion expression can reduce the use of costly punishment. While the study is not a public goods game, it is highly relevant for understanding the psychological drivers of punishment and the potential for communication or emotion expression to moderate punishment behavior. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling communication or emotion expression may reduce the negative efficiency impact of punishment by reducing its frequency. However, the findings are specific to the ultimatum game and may not transfer directly to public goods games without further evidence.

- source: Wang, R. Y., & Ng, C. N. (2015). Can Centralized Sanctioning Promote Trust in Social Dilemmas? A Two-Level Trust Game with Incomplete Information. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a centralized sanctioning authority into a repeated trust game with incomplete information can increase trust and reciprocity, especially when actors believe the authority is likely to punish defectors. The model shows that the threat of centralized punishment relaxes the conditions under which trust is placed and honored, compared to the baseline trust game without an authority. The effect is strongest when there is optimism about the authority's willingness to punish. The model predicts that centralized sanctioning can mitigate the end-game effect (decline in cooperation in final rounds). However, the analysis is theoretical and does not provide explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that adding a centralized punishment mechanism to a trust-based social dilemma can increase trust and cooperation, especially under incomplete information. While the model is not a public goods game, it is adjacent and relevant for predicting the effect of centralized punishment in repeated social dilemmas. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling centralized punishment is likely to increase cooperation and, by implication, group payoffs or efficiency, particularly when the punishment is sufficiently strong and actors believe the authority is likely to punish. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency or group payoff, so its value for direct efficiency prediction is limited to qualitative inference.

- source: Behnk, S., Barreda-Tarrazona, I., & García-Gallego, A. (2018). Punishing liars-How monitoring affects honesty and trust. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that a severe, cost-free punishment mechanism for dishonesty in a sender-receiver game leads to a large increase in honest messages and trust by receivers, compared to a baseline with no punishment. This effect persists even when the probability of detecting dishonesty is reduced to 10%, except at a moderate detection probability (25%), where honesty is lower. The deterrence effect of punishment is less pronounced when the sender's potential gain from deception is high. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, but the behavioral results indicate that punishment can sustain high levels of honesty and trust with minimal monitoring.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a principal-agent (sender-receiver) environment with asymmetric information, the availability of severe, cost-free punishment for dishonesty can dramatically increase honest behavior and trust, even with low monitoring (detection) probabilities. However, the game is not a public goods game or a direct variant, and efficiency or group payoff is not measured. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in PGG-like environments, the results suggest that punishment mechanisms with high severity and low cost may have strong positive effects on pro-social behavior, but the transfer to efficiency outcomes in PGGs is indirect. The findings are most relevant for settings where the main concern is honesty/truth-telling under asymmetric information, rather than contribution to a public good.

- source: Berenji, B., Chou, T., & D'Orsogna, M. R. (2014). Recidivism and Rehabilitation of Criminal Offenders: A Carrot and Stick Evolutionary Game. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The model demonstrates that neither punishment alone nor rehabilitation alone is optimal for reducing crime and recidivism. Instead, the best outcomes (highest ratio of reformed to unreformable individuals) are achieved by balancing punishment severity and rehabilitation resources, subject to a fixed total resource constraint. There is an optimal allocation of resources between punishment and rehabilitation, and both too little and too much of either can worsen outcomes. The model also shows that the duration and intensity of rehabilitation matter, with intermediate durations being most effective. These findings are robust across different initial conditions and parameter values. However, the model does not report efficiency or group payoff in the sense of public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, adjacent evidence for the prediction task. It supports the idea that the effect of punishment on group-level outcomes (here, the ratio of reformed to unreformable individuals) depends on the balance between punishment and rehabilitation, and that both are needed for optimal outcomes. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct predictive value for efficiency in PGG-like environments is limited. The main implication is that punishment's effect is non-monotonic and context-dependent, especially when resources are constrained and when rehabilitation is possible. The findings suggest that in analogous PGG settings, enabling punishment without considering complementary positive interventions or resource tradeoffs may not maximize efficiency.

- source: de Quervain, D. J. F., Fischbacher, U., Treyer, V., Schelthammer, M., Schnyder, U., Buck, A., & Fehr, E. (2004). The neural basis of altruistic punishment. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that when participants are given the opportunity to punish defectors in a trust game, they frequently do so, even at a personal cost. Effective (costly) punishment, as opposed to symbolic (non-impactful) punishment, is associated with increased activation in the dorsal striatum (caudate nucleus), a brain region implicated in reward processing. The amount of money spent on punishment correlates with the level of caudate activation, suggesting that anticipated satisfaction motivates punishment. The costliness of punishment reduces the amount of punishment compared to costless punishment, but does not eliminate it. The study does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, but does show that punishment is used and is psychologically rewarding.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in trust-game-like environments, individuals are willing to pay to punish defectors, and that this behavior is motivated by anticipated satisfaction. However, the study does not report on group efficiency or total welfare, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a trust game with a punishment stage. The findings support the idea that costly punishment will be used when available, but do not provide direct evidence about whether this increases or decreases group efficiency. The costliness of punishment reduces its frequency/intensity, which may be relevant for predicting efficiency effects in similar designs.

- source: Vollan, B., Blanco, E., Steimanis, I., Petutschnig, F., & Prediger, S. (2020). Procedural fairness and nepotism among local traditional and democratic leaders in rural Namibia. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that both traditional chiefs and democratically elected leaders in rural Namibia are similarly likely to implement democratic decision-making procedures and are similarly unlikely to engage in nepotistic punishment. Chiefs may be slightly more likely to choose democratic procedures and less likely to be lenient toward relatives in punishment, but these differences are not statistically robust. The main outcomes are behavioral (procedural choice, nepotism in punishment), not efficiency or group payoff. The punishment task is a trust game with third-party punishment, not a public goods game. The study suggests that, in this context of legal pluralism, traditional leaders are not more autocratic or nepotistic than elected leaders.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on punishment behavior (altruistic punishment) and procedural fairness in a trust game with third-party punishment, not in a public goods game. It does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it does show that, in a real-world setting, leaders' willingness to punish norm violations is not strongly affected by whether they are democratically elected or traditional chiefs, and that nepotism in punishment is low for both. The design and context differ from standard PGGs, so the transfer value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs is limited.

- source: Rostovtseva, V. V., Puurtinen, M., Salinas, E. M., Cox, R. F. A., Groothuis, A. G. G., Butovskaya, M. L., & Weissing, F. J. (2023). Unravelling the many facets of human cooperation in an experimental study. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that individuals are consistent in their prosocial and punishment-related decisions across a variety of economic games, but that punishment is not correlated with other prosocial tendencies. The appearance of interaction partners (as judged by facial attributes) systematically affects prosocial behavior in most games, but not in the punishment game. There is no evidence that facial appearance predicts actual prosocial behavior. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and thus does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a suite of economic games including a punishment game, the tendency to punish is not associated with other prosocial behaviors and is not influenced by partner appearance. However, it does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, so it cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its main value is in showing that punishment behavior is psychologically and behaviorally distinct from other forms of cooperation, and that its drivers may differ from those of prosociality. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment may not always align with higher prosociality, but provides no direct evidence about efficiency effects.

- source: Tang, Z. X., Qu, C., Hu, Y., Benistant, J., Moisan, F., Derrington, E., & Dreher, J. C. (2023). Strengths of social ties modulate brain computations for third-party punishment. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment increases as the social distance between the punisher and the norm violator increases. The costliness of punishment reduces the amount of punishment assigned, but the effect of social distance remains robust. Computational modeling shows that participants integrate social distance, inequity aversion, and cost to punish when making punishment decisions. fMRI results identify brain regions involved in these computations. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and thus does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the willingness to punish norm violators depends on the social distance between the punisher and the violator, and that costliness of punishment reduces punishment levels. However, because the experiment is a third-party punishment variant of the Dictator Game (not a public goods game), and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings may be useful for understanding moderators of punishment behavior, especially the role of social distance and cost, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes.

- source: Shen, C., He, Z. X., Shi, L., Wang, Z., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Prosocial punishment bots breed social punishment in human players. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a fraction of bots that always cooperate and punish defectors (CP) into a population of normal players in a two-stage Prisoner's Dilemma can significantly increase the prevalence of prosocial punishment among normal players. This effect is stronger when bots are placed on high-degree nodes in scale-free networks or when players have a conformist bias (copying the majority). The effect is robust to different network structures, learning rules, and parameter values. However, the main reported outcomes are behavioral (strategy frequencies), not efficiency or group payoff. The study does not report the effect of punishment on efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the conditions under which punishment (specifically, prosocial punishment implemented by bots) can increase the prevalence of prosocial punishment and cooperation in a two-stage Prisoner's Dilemma. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings may be useful for understanding how network structure, imitation rules, and the presence of committed punishers affect the spread of prosocial punishment, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Alam, R., & Rai, T. S. (2025). Profitable third- party punishment destabilizes cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that profitable third-party punishment (i.e., paying punishers to punish) destabilizes cooperation: dictators are less likely to share when punishers are paid, both before and after any punishment is experienced. This effect is robust across multiple experimental designs, including repeated games and conditions optimized for reinforcement learning. Even when receivers (who benefit from cooperation) can choose the punishment regime, they systematically select profitable punishment, reducing their own expected earnings by about 20%. The negative effect of profitable punishment on cooperation is only eliminated when the game structure makes antisocial punishment impossible and this is clearly signaled. The main outcome is cooperation rate, but expected receiver earnings are also reported, showing a negative welfare effect. The study highlights the importance of the communicative function of punishment and the dangers of introducing profit motives into punishment systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing profit motives for punishment (i.e., making punishment profitable for the punisher) can reduce cooperation and lower welfare, even when punishment is otherwise available. While the experiments use dictator game variants rather than standard public goods games, the mechanisms are closely related: the presence and structure of punishment, and especially the motives signaled by punishment, are critical moderators of its effect on cooperation and efficiency. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling punishment is not always beneficial for efficiency—if punishment is profitable for the punisher, it may reduce efficiency compared to both unpaid punishment and no punishment. The effect is robust to repeated play and persists even when learning is optimized. The results also show that the structure of punishment (e.g., whether antisocial punishment is possible) is a key moderator. However, because the main outcome is cooperation rate rather than group efficiency, and the game is a dictator game variant, direct quantitative predictions for public goods games should be made with caution, but the qualitative direction is clear: profitable punishment can backfire.

- source: Brañas-Garza, P., Espín, A. M., Exadaktylos, F., & Herrmann, B. (2014). Fair and unfair punishers coexist in the Ultimatum Game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both prosocial and antisocial individuals engage in costly punishment in the Ultimatum Game, as measured by rejection of unequal offers. Prosocial punishers are those who also behave fairly in the Dictator Game, while antisocial punishers are unfair as dictators but still reject unequal offers in the Ultimatum Game. The coexistence of these types is robust across two large-scale experiments (one with a representative city sample, one with university students). The paper highlights that antisocial punishment may undermine the efficiency and stability of social systems, but does not provide direct experimental evidence on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it focuses on the motivations behind punishment in the Ultimatum Game. However, it offers important contextual evidence that not all punishment is prosocial or norm-enforcing; antisocial punishment can exist and may have negative implications for efficiency in broader social dilemmas. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee increased efficiency, especially if antisocial motives are prevalent. The findings are most relevant for understanding heterogeneity in punishment motives, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Gao, D. D., & Guo, W. (2025). Evolutionary game and simulation analysis of tripartite subjects in public health emergencies under government reward and punishment mechanisms. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The study finds that government reward and punishment mechanisms (subsidies and fines) for pharmaceutical companies and the public promote compliant production and cooperation in prevention and control, respectively. Increasing the magnitude of rewards or punishments accelerates the convergence to full compliance and cooperation. The initial willingness of parties to participate also affects the speed of convergence.
