However, the model does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and all results are in terms of the probability of adopting certain strategies and the speed of convergence to stable equilibria.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that increasing the strength of punishment and reward mechanisms can promote compliance and cooperation in a multi-party public-goods-like setting. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report empirical results. The findings may inform the qualitative direction of punishment effects in similar environments, but cannot be used for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes as required by the downstream task.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Diekmann, A. (2018). Heterogeneous groups overcome the diffusion of responsibility problem in social norm enforcement. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that diffusion of responsibility (lower individual sanctioning rates as group size increases) is present in symmetric groups but is largely overcome in heterogeneous groups where one member has a lower cost of sanctioning. Over repeated rounds, groups tacitly coordinate on the strong member to sanction, leading to higher and more efficient norm enforcement. However, the main outcomes are behavioral (sanctioning rates), not payoff-based efficiency or group welfare. The findings suggest that group heterogeneity can mitigate the bystander effect in norm enforcement, but do not provide direct evidence on how this affects group efficiency or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative behavioral evidence about how group size and heterogeneity in punishment cost affect the likelihood and efficiency of norm enforcement in a volunteer's dilemma context. However, it does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings suggest that introducing heterogeneity in punishment cost can increase the likelihood and efficiency of norm enforcement, especially in larger groups, but the translation to efficiency or welfare outcomes in standard PGGs is indirect.

- source: Wang, Z., Xia, C. Y., Meloni, S., Zhou, C. S., & Moreno, Y. (2013). Impact of Social Punishment on Cooperative Behavior in Complex Networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a punisher strategy (who pays a cost to reduce defectors' payoffs) in networked 2-person games (PD and SD) increases the fraction of cooperative strategies (cooperators and punishers) at equilibrium, across a range of network topologies (lattice, ER, scale-free) and punishment severities. The effect is robust: even small punishment fines can substantially increase cooperation. The paper analyzes the spatial and network organization of strategies, showing that punishers can form protective clusters that shield cooperators from defectors. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that social punishment increases the prevalence of cooperative strategies in 2-person networked games, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games, the results are only indirectly informative: they suggest that punishment can increase cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency, but the actual payoff consequences (including the cost of punishment) are not analyzed. The game structure is not a public goods game but a 2-person PD/SD, so transfer to PGG-like settings should be cautious.

- source: Perc, M., Donnay, K., & Helbing, D. (2013). Understanding Recurrent Crime as System-Immanent Collective Behavior. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that increasing punishment or inspection does not always reduce crime; instead, the system exhibits complex, sometimes counterintuitive dynamics, including cyclic dominance and abrupt phase transitions between different equilibria (e.g., dominance of criminals, coexistence of criminals and inspectors, or cyclic coexistence of all three strategies). The effectiveness of punishment depends critically on the temptation to commit crime, the cost and incentive for inspection, and the structure of social interactions. The model demonstrates that crime can be recurrent and robust to increased punishment, especially when spatial structure and local interactions are considered. However, the paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are not directly translatable to predictions about group welfare or efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on crime (and by analogy, on cooperation in social dilemmas) is highly sensitive to the interplay of temptation, punishment cost, and network structure, and that simple linear predictions (e.g., more punishment always increases efficiency) are likely to fail. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value is in highlighting the potential for non-monotonic, phase-transition-like effects and the importance of considering spatial and network structure. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests caution in assuming monotonic or simple effects of punishment, especially in spatially structured or networked environments.

- source: Wang, G. C., Su, Q., & Wang, L. (2022). Evolution of cooperation with joint liability. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that joint liability, when combined with costly punishment, strongly promotes the evolution of cooperation in a lending game. Punishment alone is insufficient to sustain cooperation, but joint liability provides incentives for individuals to punish defectors, leading to high levels of cooperation and low levels of defection. The condition for cooperation to evolve is relaxed from β−γ>4c (without joint liability) to β>γ (with joint liability and low tolerability for defection). The effect is robust to model extensions, including intragroup donation games (Prisoner's Dilemma). The abundance of cooperators and punishers is maximized at intermediate values of selection intensity, population size, and mutation rate. The model is adjacent to public goods games but differs in structure and outcome measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the combination of joint liability and costly punishment can greatly increase cooperation in group settings similar to, but not identical with, public goods games. The findings suggest that punishment is much more effective at promoting cooperation when joint liability is present, and that the threshold for punishment to be effective is lower. However, because the model is not a standard PGG and does not report efficiency as a ratio of realized to maximum group payoff, the results should be transferred to PGG efficiency predictions with caution. The paper is most informative for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment and group-level liability interact, and for predicting that punishment will be more effective in environments with joint liability or similar group-level consequences.

- source: Jordan, J. J., Hoffman, M., Bloom, P., & Rand, D. G. (2016). Third-party punishment as a costly signal of trustworthiness. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that TPP is used as a signal of trustworthiness when it is the only available signal, leading to greater trust and reciprocation in a subsequent trust game. When helping is also available as a signal, the use and signaling value of TPP are reduced, while helping remains a strong signal. The results support a reputational account of TPP, showing that people may punish to signal their character rather than solely to deter selfishness. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the signaling value and frequency of punishment depend on the availability and informativeness of alternative prosocial signals (like helping). However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoffs in public-goods-game-like environments. Its main relevance is in highlighting that the context and available signals can moderate the behavioral use of punishment, which may indirectly affect efficiency in other designs. For direct prediction of efficiency outcomes, this paper is only indirectly informative.

- source: Hu, K. P., Tao, Y. W., Ma, Y. J., & Shi, L. (2021). Peer pressure induced punishment resolves social dilemma on interdependent networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a peer-pressure-like punishment mechanism—where players with inconsistent strategies across interdependent network layers have their payoffs reduced—can promote cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The effect of punishment is non-monotonic: moderate punishment maximizes cooperation, while too little or too much punishment is less effective. The effect is robust to changes in network structure (lattice, small-world, random) and the degree of payoff interdependence between layers. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare; the main focus is on cooperation rates and the spatial distribution of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma settings, especially when the punishment is moderate. However, because the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game or direct variant), and because the main outcome is cooperation rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be transferred to efficiency prediction with caution. The results suggest that moderate punishment is likely to be most effective for promoting cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence about the effect on group efficiency or total payoff. The design dimensions most relevant here are the punishment cost (modeled as a reduction in payoff for inconsistent pairs) and the degree of interdependence between players' payoffs. The absence of direct efficiency or payoff outcomes limits the paper's direct utility for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Zhang, Y. L., & Fu, F. (2018). Strategy intervention for the evolution of fairness. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of fairness in the Ultimatum Game is promoted by the presence of spiteful strategies (which reject low or unequal offers, i.e., costly punishment) and inhibited by altruistic strategies (which accept any offer). The introduction of non-monotonic rejection (rejecting both low and high offers) further helps fairness overcome selfishness. In group-structured populations, larger population size increases the frequency of altruism and decreases the frequency of selfishness and spitefulness, but does not change the dominance of fairness. Migration inhibits the evolution of selfishness and fairness, but promotes altruism and, with sufficient migration, spitefulness. Mutation has non-monotonic effects, with intermediate mutation maximizing fairness and selfishness, and minimizing spitefulness. The main dependent variable is the frequency of each strategy, not group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the role of costly punishment (rejection) in promoting fairness in strategic environments similar to the Ultimatum Game, which is adjacent to public goods games. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it supports the idea that the availability of costly punishment can increase the prevalence of fairness-oriented behavior, especially when non-monotonic punishment is possible. The findings are most relevant for understanding how punishment mechanisms affect the evolutionary stability of fairness, not for predicting group efficiency outcomes.

- source: Ruff, C. C., Ugazio, G., & Fehr, E. (2013). Changing Social Norm Compliance with Noninvasive Brain Stimulation. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that the threat of punishment increases norm compliance (higher transfers) in a modified dictator game, and that noninvasive brain stimulation of the rLPFC can causally modulate this effect. Anodal tDCS (increasing excitability) increases sanction-induced compliance (larger transfer difference between punishment and baseline rounds), while cathodal tDCS (decreasing excitability) reduces it. The effect is stronger in social (human) punishment contexts than in non-social (computer) contexts. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral compliance rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of punishment opportunities increases norm compliance in a dictator-like game, and that this effect is modulated by neural mechanisms. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The behavioral results (increased compliance under punishment) are consistent with the broader literature, but the lack of payoff data limits its direct utility for efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Mathew, S., & Boyd, R. (2011). Punishment sustains large-scale cooperation in prestate warfare. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, show_punishment_id
  findings: The Turkana sustain costly, large-scale cooperation in warfare through informal punishment of free riders. Raiding parties are large and composed of unrelated individuals from multiple social units. Free riding (desertion, cowardice, unfair loot appropriation) is common, but is often sanctioned through verbal reprimands, corporal punishment, and fines. The system relies on third-party consensus and community enforcement, not formal institutions. Norms governing warfare are group-beneficial at the ethnolinguistic scale, not just for smaller social units. While the paper provides rich evidence on the mechanisms and scale of cooperation and punishment, it does not provide direct measures of efficiency or group payoff as defined in public goods games, nor does it experimentally manipulate punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative about the real-world operation of punishment and its role in sustaining cooperation at large scales, especially in the absence of formal institutions. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff, nor does it use a controlled experimental design. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in public goods games, the paper offers strong qualitative support for the idea that punishment can sustain cooperation and reduce free riding, even in large, anonymous groups. The evidence is most relevant for understanding the mechanisms and social context of punishment, rather than for parameterizing efficiency outcomes in laboratory PGGs. The findings suggest that punishment can be effective at large group sizes and in the absence of repeated interaction or kinship, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency changes due to punishment in a controlled game setting.

- source: Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2015). A double-edged sword: Benefits and pitfalls of heterogeneous punishment in evolutionary inspection games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneous punishment (punishers who invest different amounts in sanctioning) can reduce the prevalence of second-order free-riders (ordinary people) compared to uniform punishment, but this does not always translate into lower crime rates. In some parameter regions, uniform punishment is as effective or more effective at reducing crime. The system exhibits complex phase transitions and cyclic dominance, which maintain the coexistence of criminals, punishers, and ordinary people. The main obstacle to eliminating crime is the ability of ordinary people to free-ride on the efforts of punishers. The results are presented as fractions of strategies in the population, not as efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction of punishment effects in public-goods-like environments, especially regarding the role of punishment heterogeneity and second-order free-riding. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict changes in efficiency when punishment is enabled. The findings suggest that the presence of second-order free-riders and the structure of punishment (uniform vs. heterogeneous) can have complex, non-monotonic effects on cooperation and norm enforcement, which may inform qualitative expectations about efficiency in related games. The results highlight the importance of considering strategy diversity and cyclic dominance in predicting the effects of punishment, but quantitative predictions about efficiency require additional evidence.

- source: Chowdhury, S. N., Kundu, S., Perc, M., & Ghosh, D. (2021). Complex evolutionary dynamics due to punishment and free space in ecological multi-games. *Proceedings of the Royal Society A-mathematical Physical and Engineering Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the inclusion of punishment as a strategy can promote the coexistence of cooperation and punishment, and can reduce the prevalence of defectors under certain parameter regimes. The cost of punishment, the temptation to defect, and the probability of playing the Prisoner's Dilemma versus the Snowdrift game all modulate the long-term fractions of cooperators, punishers, and defectors. The model exhibits multi-stability, oscillatory, and chaotic dynamics, with the possibility of cyclic dominance among strategies. However, the paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the dynamical and stationary properties of the system.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how punishment can affect the prevalence of cooperation and defection in evolutionary games, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff. Its results may inform qualitative expectations about the conditions under which punishment can sustain cooperation or lead to complex dynamics, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games. The model is adjacent to PGGs, as it is based on two-person games (PD and SD) rather than multi-person public goods games, and the outcomes are reported as fractions of strategies rather than payoffs or efficiency.

- source: Roy, S., Chowdhury, S. N., Kundu, S., Sar, G. K., Banerjee, J., Rakshit, B., Mali, P. C., Perc, M., & Ghosh, D. (2023). Time delays shape the eco-evolutionary dynamics of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the inclusion of punishment as a strategy can, depending on parameter values (notably the cost of punishment and the temptation to defect), lead to the coexistence of punishers and defectors, dominance of punishers, or extinction of all strategies. Time delay can induce oscillatory or chaotic dynamics, and the ecological 'free space' variable can support the survival of cooperation-related strategies. The fate of cooperation, punishment, and defection is highly sensitive to parameter values and initial conditions. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on the population dynamics of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how punishment, its cost, and related parameters affect the prevalence of cooperation and punishment in a prisoner's dilemma context with eco-evolutionary feedbacks and time delay. However, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The results may inform qualitative expectations about the conditions under which punishment can suppress defection or support cooperation, but cannot be used for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Li, K., Szolnoki, A., Cong, R., & Wang, L. (2016). The coevolution of overconfidence and bluffing in the resource competition game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that in the absence of punishment, both overconfidence and bluffing evolve to high levels, with bluffing generally higher than overconfidence. Introducing punishment (with probability p) for revealed deception reduces both overconfidence and bluffing, with overconfidence being more sensitive to punishment. The structure of the interaction network (degree, heterogeneity) modulates these effects: higher degree or more heterogeneous networks promote bluffing and can make punishment less effective. System bias (delta) that increases conflict frequency also enhances the effectiveness of punishment. Players with higher real ability tend to evolve higher overconfidence and bluffing. The paper provides some analysis of average payoff for punished vs. non-punished players, showing that those with lower ability and lower payoff are punished more often, but does not report group-level efficiency or total welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and probability of punishment can reduce the prevalence of overconfidence and bluffing in resource competition games, especially overconfidence. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (deception levels) and not group efficiency or total payoff, the direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes in public goods or similar games is limited. The model is adjacent to PGGs: it is a resource competition game with pairwise contests, not a standard PGG. The punishment mechanism is modeled as a reduction in deception traits rather than direct payoff reduction. The paper does provide some subgroup payoff analysis, suggesting that punishment targets and reduces the payoff of lower-ability, higher-deception individuals, but does not quantify overall group efficiency. Thus, the main decision support is qualitative: punishment reduces harmful behavioral biases, and its effectiveness depends on network structure and conflict frequency, but the effect on group efficiency is not directly established.

- source: Ibrahim, A. M. (2022). The conditional defector strategies can violate the most crucial supporting mechanisms of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that conditional defector strategies—cheaters who sometimes cooperate (e.g., to share dispersal costs or pay to escape punishment)—can successfully invade and dominate populations even when mechanisms like punishment, kin selection, and spatial structure are present. In the models, when cheaters can share dispersal costs, they can migrate and outcompete cooperators, leading to cooperator extinction. Similarly, when cheaters reduce their own payoff to avoid detection and punishment, they can dominate despite the presence of punishment mechanisms. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of agent types and extinction events, not direct efficiency or group payoff measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a cautionary theoretical perspective: the presence of punishment mechanisms does not guarantee the maintenance of cooperation or high efficiency if conditional defector strategies are possible. The models are adjacent to public goods games and do not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, so direct quantitative prediction is not possible. However, the findings suggest that in environments where agents can adaptively evade punishment (e.g., by reducing their own payoff to avoid detection), the expected efficiency gain from enabling punishment may be much lower or even negative, as cheaters can still dominate. For prediction tasks, this paper highlights the importance of considering the sophistication of defector strategies and the limitations of punishment as a stabilizing mechanism.

- source: Yamagishi, T., Li, Y., Fermin, A. S. R., Kanai, R., Takagishi, H., Matsumoto, Y., Kiyonari, T., & Sakagami, M. (2017). Behavioural Differences and Neural Substrates of Altruistic and Spiteful Punishment. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that individuals who engage in costly punishment in the TPPG (altruistic punishers) also behave more pro-socially in a variety of non-strategic games, while those who only reject unfair offers in the UG (spiteful punishers) behave more selfishly. The distinction is supported by neural data: spiteful punishers have larger left caudate nucleus and nucleus accumbens volumes. The paper does not analyze the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff, nor does it manipulate the presence or absence of punishment in a public goods or social dilemma context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or social dilemma games. It does, however, suggest that the motivations behind punishment (altruistic vs. spiteful) are heterogeneous and that not all punishment is associated with pro-social or efficiency-enhancing behavior. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment may not always increase efficiency, depending on the underlying motives of punishers, but it does not provide quantitative or design-specific evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Balafoutas, L., Nikiforakis, N., & Rockenbach, B. (2014). Direct and indirect punishment among strangers in the field. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that (1) direct punishment of norm violators is not rewarded by third parties in one-shot field interactions; (2) indirect punishment, operationalized as withholding help from a norm violator, is common and significantly reduces the likelihood that violators receive help; (3) the availability of indirect punishment opportunities crowds out the use of direct punishment; and (4) there are notable sex differences in the application of direct and indirect punishment. The experiment does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, but provides evidence on the behavioral mechanisms of norm enforcement in natural settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their close variants. Its main contribution is to show, in a natural field setting, that indirect punishment (withholding help) is more common and preferred over direct punishment when both are available, and that direct punishment is not socially rewarded. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that in environments where indirect punishment is possible and not costly, direct costly punishment may be less prevalent, but the paper does not inform how this affects group efficiency or payoff. The design is adjacent to PGGs, as the norm violation (littering) is a negative externality, but there is no explicit group payoff or contribution structure. Thus, the paper is more informative about behavioral mechanisms than about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Brethel-Haurwitz, K. M., Stoycos, S. A., Cardinale, E. M., Huebner, B., & Marsh, A. A. (2016). Is costly punishment altruistic? Exploring rejection of unfair offers in the Ultimatum Game in real-world altruists. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that extraordinary real-world altruists (altruistic kidney donors) do not engage in more costly punishment (rejection of unfair offers) in the Ultimatum Game than controls. However, individuals with higher self-reported normative altruism (SRA) are more likely to reject unfair offers, indicating that costly punishment is associated with norm sensitivity rather than altruism per se. The study provides evidence that costly punishment in the UG is better understood as norm-enforcing behavior rather than altruistic behavior. No direct analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare is conducted.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding the motivations behind costly punishment in the UG, suggesting that such punishment is driven by norm enforcement rather than altruism. For the downstream prediction task, this paper offers little direct guidance on how punishment affects efficiency, as it does not report or analyze payoff-based outcomes. However, it does reinforce that the presence of punishment in UG-like games is not necessarily linked to increased altruism or prosociality, and that behavioral outcomes (like rejection rates) may not translate to efficiency gains.

- source: Brüne, M., Scheele, D., Heinisch, C., Tas, C., Wischniewski, J., & Güntürkün, O. (2012). Empathy Moderates the Effect of Repetitive Transcranial Magnetic Stimulation of the Right Dorsolateral Prefrontal Cortex on Costly Punishment. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that inhibiting the right DLPFC via rTMS increases the amount of costly punishment assigned by third-party observers in a Dictator Game with punishment option. This effect is significantly moderated by individual differences in empathy: the increase in punishment is stronger for individuals with lower empathy scores. No significant effect is found for left DLPFC stimulation. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not use a public goods game or a direct variant, but rather a third-party punishment Dictator Game. The findings are relevant for understanding the neural and psychological mechanisms underlying costly punishment, but do not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does, however, provide informative evidence about the psychological and neural mechanisms that may underlie costly punishment, showing that punishment can be increased by disrupting cognitive control (right DLPFC) and that this effect is moderated by empathy. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or group payoff, this paper is only weakly relevant, as it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it use a PGG or close variant. It may be useful for understanding individual differences in punishment behavior, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Kodaka, F., Takahashi, H., Yamada, M., Takano, H., Nakayama, K., Ito, H., & Suhara, T. (2012). Effect of Cooperation Level of Group on Punishment for Non-Cooperators: A Functional Magnetic Resonance Imaging Study. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the degree of punishment assigned to a non-cooperator is significantly higher when the rest of the group is highly cooperative compared to when the group is less cooperative. This is reflected both in behavioral choices and in increased activation in the right dorsolateral prefrontal cortex and left anterior cingulate cortex. The results suggest that the social context of group cooperation modulates punitive behavior, but the study does not address efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the social context—specifically, the overall cooperation level of the group—modulates the severity of punishment assigned to non-cooperators. However, it does not provide any data on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main relevance is in highlighting that punishment behavior is context-dependent, which may be important for interpreting or modeling punishment effects in environments where group cooperation levels vary.

- source: Konishi, N., Oe, T., Shimizu, H., Tanaka, K., & Ohtsubo, Y. (2017). Perceived Shared Condemnation Intensifies Punitive Moral Emotions. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that perceived shared condemnation increases moral outrage and, to a lesser extent, moral disgust, while reducing empathy for norm violators. These emotional shifts are associated with greater willingness to punish, both in hypothetical scenarios and in self-reported real-life interventions. The experimental manipulation in Study 2 shows that providing information about others' condemnation causally increases moral outrage and willingness to impose a fine. However, the studies do not use economic games, do not measure actual punishment behavior with real stakes, and do not report any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar economic games. It does, however, suggest that social cues about shared condemnation can increase willingness to punish, which may be relevant for understanding psychological drivers of punishment in group settings. For the prediction task, this paper is only indirectly informative: it supports the idea that punishment is more likely when people believe others will also punish, but it does not address how this affects group efficiency, payoff, or cooperation rates in economic games.

- source: Molho, C., Tybur, J. M., Van Lange, P. A. M., & Balliet, D. (2020). Direct and indirect punishment of norm violations in daily life. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that people frequently use both direct and indirect punishment strategies in response to norm violations in daily life. Direct confrontation is more likely when the punisher has been personally victimized, has more power, or values the offender more. Indirect punishment (gossip, social exclusion) is more likely when the violation is severe, the punisher has less power, or values the offender less. Emotional responses (anger, disgust) also differentially predict punishment type. The findings support the idea that punishment is context-sensitive and motivated by both the benefits of changing others' behavior and the risks of retaliation. However, the study does not measure or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any economic outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar economic games. Its findings are relevant for understanding the psychological and contextual determinants of punishment strategy in naturalistic settings, but not for quantifying the impact of punishment on efficiency. The study may be useful for mechanism discussions or for understanding when punishment is likely to be deployed, but it does not inform the downstream prediction task of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Ferguson, E., Quigley, E., Powell, G., Stewart, L., Harrison, F., & Tallentire, H. (2019). To help or punish in the face of unfairness: men and women prefer mutually-beneficial strategies over punishment in a sexual selection context. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across three studies using a one-shot 3PPC game, men primed with a romantic context are more likely to choose compensation or both compensation and punishment, rather than punishment alone or doing nothing. Women rate men who compensate (or both compensate and punish) as more attractive, compassionate, fair, and strong, regardless of whether they are considering short-term or long-term relationships. Trait empathy and left-wing political ideology are associated with a preference for compensation. The studies do not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct monetary outcomes for the group; all outcomes are behavioral preferences or social evaluations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-like games. The design is a one-shot third-party punishment and compensation game, and the outcomes are social preferences and evaluations, not efficiency or payoff. Therefore, it does not directly inform prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. It may be relevant for understanding social or reputational motivations for punishment and compensation, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Dimant, E., & Gesche, T. (2023). Nudging enforcers: how norm perceptions and motives for lying shape sanctions. *Pnas Nexus*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment increases with the size of the lie and is harsher when the lie increases inequity or is for personal gain, compared to when it restores equity. Norm-nudges (empirical or normative information about others' behavior or approval) increase punishment, especially when preexisting norms are ambiguous. The effect of norm-nudges is strongest in scenarios where the social norm is less clear. These findings are robust across a behavioral experiment, a vignette experiment, and a norm elicitation study. However, the study does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on how punishment behavior is shaped by norm-nudges and the context of norm violations, but it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public-goods-like games, this paper is only indirectly informative: it shows that punishment is sensitive to context and can be increased by norm-nudges, especially when norms are ambiguous. However, it does not provide evidence on whether or how punishment affects group efficiency or welfare, nor does it use a public goods game structure. Thus, it should be used as indirect evidence about the determinants of punishment behavior, not about efficiency effects.

- source: Winter, F., & Zhang, N. (2018). Social norm enforcement in ethnically diverse communities. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds strong ethnic asymmetries in social norm enforcement: native Germans are more likely to sanction norm violations, and ethnic minorities are more likely to be the targets of sanctions. These patterns are interpreted as resulting from status inequalities between groups. The aggregate probability of being sanctioned for a norm violation (social control) increases with the share of minorities in the population, but this is driven by natives sanctioning minorities at higher rates. The study does not address efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and is not a public goods game or direct variant, but is adjacent in that it studies real-world norm enforcement and punishment behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about real-world punishment (sanctioning) behavior in response to norm violations, with a focus on ethnic asymmetries. It does not use a public goods game or measure efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. However, it may be useful for understanding how social context (e.g., group identity, status, diversity) can moderate the frequency and direction of punishment, which could be relevant for interpreting or designing PGG experiments with diverse populations. The lack of payoff or efficiency outcomes limits its direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Gordon, D. S., Madden, J. R., & Lea, S. E. G. (2014). Both Loved and Feared: Third Party Punishers Are Viewed as Formidable and Likeable, but These Reputational Benefits May Only Be Open to Dominant Individuals. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that third party punishment is perceived as both a dominant and likable act, distinguishing it from other aggressive behaviors. However, only dominant individuals are seen as able to punish effectively and at low cost, with reduced risk of retaliation and greater ability to use non-violent (social) punishment. Subordinate individuals are perceived as less likely to succeed and at higher risk if they attempt punishment. The reputational benefits of punishment (increased likability and dominance) are thus mainly accessible to dominant individuals. These findings are based on observer judgments in hypothetical scenarios, not on actual behavior in economic games or real payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, suggest that heterogeneity in the cost and effectiveness of punishment (due to dominance/status) may be important moderators of the impact of punishment on cooperation and norm enforcement. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that the effectiveness and social acceptability of punishment may depend on the perceived status of the punisher, but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental data on efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are relevant for understanding mechanisms and moderators of punishment, but not for direct prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: Zinchenko, O. (2019). Brain responses to social punishment: a meta-analysis. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that social punishment tasks consistently activate the bilateral claustrum, right inferior frontal gyrus, and left superior frontal gyrus. These regions are associated with salience detection, executive function, and emotional empathy. The meta-analysis includes a range of paradigms (Ultimatum Game, Dictator Game, third-party punishment, social rejection), but does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or related economic outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding the neural basis of social punishment but do not provide evidence on the effects of punishment on cooperation, efficiency, or payoff in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their variants. While it confirms that social punishment is a robust neural phenomenon across several economic and social paradigms, it does not report or synthesize any data on group earnings, efficiency, or welfare. Therefore, it should not be used to inform quantitative predictions about the impact of punishment on efficiency in public goods game-like environments.

- source: Lie-Panis, J., Fitouchi, L., Baumard, N., & André, J. B. (2024). The social leverage effect: Institutions transform weak reputation effects into strong incentives for cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that institutions, by leveraging contributions from individuals (motivated by reputation), can create strong enough incentives (via punishment, monitoring, or rewards) to sustain cooperation in settings where reputation alone would fail. The efficiency of the institution (how much incentive is generated per unit contribution) is a key moderator: high efficiency greatly expands the parameter space where cooperation and high payoffs are possible. The model also shows that when the population is patient (values future reputation), more individuals contribute, further increasing institutional effectiveness. However, when cooperation is easy, the institution can be wasteful, as its costs are not offset by additional gains in cooperation or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling efficient institutional punishment or monitoring in a public-goods-like environment will increase group efficiency and payoffs, especially when baseline (control) efficiency is low due to hard cooperation problems. The model clarifies that the effect of punishment depends critically on the efficiency of the institution (punishment technology), the cost of contributing to the institution, and the patience of the population. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that when institutional punishment is efficient and the population is sufficiently future-oriented, enabling punishment will lead to large increases in efficiency relative to control. However, if cooperation is already high in control, the marginal benefit of punishment may be negligible or even negative due to unnecessary costs.

- source: Hooper, P. L., Kaplan, H. S., & Jaeggi, A. V. (2021). Gains to cooperation drive the evolution of egalitarianism. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The model demonstrates that punishment of dominance (hawkishness) by withholding cooperation (defection in the PD game) can be evolutionarily stable and lead to egalitarian outcomes when the future benefits of cooperation outweigh the short-term gains from dominance. The efficiency of the group increases as leveller strategies proliferate, reducing the frequency of costly contests and increasing resource sharing. Simulations show that an ecological shift increasing the gains to cooperation leads to a transition from dominance/defection equilibria to egalitarian/cooperative equilibria with higher payoffs. Empirical analysis of forager societies finds that greater reliance on cooperative hunting predicts a higher probability of egalitarianism, consistent with the model's predictions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment (here, the ability to withhold cooperation from dominant individuals) increases group efficiency and resource equality, especially when the marginal benefit of cooperation is high and the probability of future interaction is high. The model is adjacent to public goods games but not an exact match; however, the logic and payoff structure are highly relevant. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that in repeated, high-mpcr, high-future-interaction environments, enabling punishment (even in the form of exclusion or shunning) will increase efficiency relative to control. The effect is stronger when the benefits of cooperation are high relative to the benefits of dominance or defection.

- source: Qian, X. H., Huang, M., Zhang, Q. Y., Yin, M. Q., & Wang, X. W. (2018). Mechanism design of incentive-based reverse auctions with loss-averse 3PLs under incomplete attributes. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that combining incentive-based contracts (cost-sharing) with contingent penalties (punishment for delivery failure) in reverse auctions increases the ex ante expected profit (efficiency) of the system and the principal, compared to standard reverse auctions without these mechanisms. The efficiency gains are moderated by the number of agents (more agents increase efficiency), the fraction of low-risk agents, and the degree of loss aversion (higher loss aversion reduces efficiency). The presence and magnitude of the penalty (punishment) is a key design variable, and its optimal value depends on delivery risk parameters. The model provides explicit formulas for optimal efficiency under different mechanism designs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that introducing punishment (contingent penalty) in a competitive procurement game with cost uncertainty and risk can increase system efficiency, but the effect is moderated by agent risk aversion and the number of agents. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that, all else equal, adding a punishment mechanism to a reverse auction or similar environment will increase efficiency, especially when there are more agents and when agents are less loss-averse. However, the magnitude of the effect depends on the specific design of the punishment and incentive parameters. The findings are most directly applicable to procurement/auction settings but are informative for adjacent public-goods-like environments.

- source: McCabe, K. A., Rassenti, S. J., & Smith, V. L. (1996). Game theory and reciprocity in some extensive form experimental games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments show that allowing for direct punishment of defection (game 1) leads to higher cooperation and efficiency compared to when punishment is not available (game 2), though the difference is modest in single-play but more pronounced in repeated interactions. Efficiency (as a percentage of the cooperative joint payoff) is highest in repeated same-pair treatments with punishment (90.6%), and lower in random or single-play treatments (82.6%-86.9%). The presence of punishment increases the expected payoff for cooperative choices and reduces the profitability of defection. The probability of cooperation increases with the likelihood of repeated interaction with the same partner, and punishment is used at nontrivial rates even when costly. Overall, punishment and repeated interaction both have positive effects on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in extensive form bargaining games with a punishment option, enabling punishment increases efficiency, especially when combined with repeated interaction with the same partner. The efficiency boost from punishment is present even in single-play, but is much larger in repeated games. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the presence of punishment, the matching protocol (same partner vs random), and the number of rounds. The efficiency values reported (Table 4) can be used as quantitative anchors for similar game designs. The findings support the prediction that, controlling for baseline efficiency, enabling punishment will increase efficiency, with the effect size moderated by the probability of repeated interaction and the cost/effectiveness of punishment.

- source: Brandt, H., & Sigmund, K. (2005). Indirect reciprocity, image scoring, and moral hazard. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in models of indirect reciprocity with binary reputation, cooperation can be stably maintained if individuals' knowledge of others' reputations increases over time (e.g., with age or experience). Costly punishment—refusing to help those with a Bad score, even at the risk of one's own reputation—can support a stable equilibrium of cooperation, provided the information environment is sufficiently rich. If information is too scarce, defectors dominate and cooperation collapses. The model demonstrates that the efficiency (total payoff relative to the cooperative optimum) is higher when these conditions for stable cooperation are met, and that the presence of costly punishment is essential in the minimal model for resisting defectors. The findings are directly relevant to efficiency and payoff outcomes in social dilemmas with reputation and punishment-like mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment (in the form of costly refusal to help those with a Bad reputation) can increase efficiency in social dilemma environments, but only when the information environment allows for effective reputation tracking. The model is not a literal public goods game, but the mechanisms and payoff logic are closely related. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to control only if players can reliably observe or infer others' reputations, and if the cost-to-benefit ratio of helping is not too high. The findings also highlight the importance of the number of rounds (or opportunities for information to accumulate) and the structure of information flow. However, the model does not address group size, communication, or some other public goods game design features directly.

- source: Shinya, O., Yusuke, I., & Hiroki, T. (2016). The Condition for Generous Trust. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated trust games with uncertainty about partner intention and competence, neither pure punishment nor pure generosity alone can sustain high efficiency. Generous trust (forgiveness) becomes viable and can support efficient outcomes when uncertainty is moderate and is combined with moderate punishment. If uncertainty (incompetence) is too high, efficiency collapses. The agent-based simulation shows that a mix of matching generosity (forgiving in partner selection) and exit generosity (forgiving in relationship maintenance) strategies coevolve and support higher efficiency, but only above a threshold of partner competence. The results highlight the importance of balancing punishment and forgiveness to achieve efficient cooperation under uncertainty.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in trust-game-like environments depends critically on the level of uncertainty (competence) and the balance between punishment and forgiveness. In environments with moderate uncertainty, introducing moderate punishment alongside generosity increases efficiency compared to either strategy alone. However, if uncertainty is too high, even punishment cannot sustain efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the efficiency gain from punishment will be highest when the environment allows for some, but not excessive, uncertainty, and when punishment is not too strict or too lenient. The findings are most directly relevant to trust games and adjacent repeated dyadic games, rather than standard public goods games, but the mechanisms may transfer to PGG variants with similar uncertainty and partner selection features.

- source: Verma, P., Nandi, A. K., & Sengupta, S. (2017). Bribery games on inter-dependent regular networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations, the spread of honest officers (and thus reduction in bribery) is favored over a larger region of parameter space compared to mixed populations. However, the effect of network connectivity is non-monotonic: increasing the degree of the citizen network initially helps honest strategies, but beyond an optimal point, further increases hinder the spread of honesty. Similarly, the degree of the interaction network (citizen-officer links) has an optimal value for maximizing the prevalence of honest officers. Asymmetry in population sizes (more citizens per officer) makes it harder to eradicate bribery. The cost of complaining and the bribe amount are key parameters: lower cost of complaining and lower bribe amounts favor honest strategies. The main dependent variables are the fixation probability of honest officers and the phase space area where honest strategies dominate, not direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it analyzes how network structure, punishment asymmetry, and behavioral parameters affect the prevalence of honest (non-corrupt) strategies. In public-goods-like environments with asymmetric punishment, structured populations can reduce corruption, but only up to an optimal level of network connectivity. The findings suggest that, for similar games, enabling punishment (asymmetric, targeting only one side) in a structured population can increase the prevalence of socially beneficial behavior, but the effect depends on network connectivity and behavioral costs. For prediction, the paper supports the idea that punishment can improve outcomes (in terms of honest behavior) in structured settings, but warns that too much connectivity can undermine this effect. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured, the findings should be used as indirect evidence.

- source: Truskanov, N., Emery, Y., & Bshary, R. (2020). Juvenile cleaner fish can socially learn the consequences of cheating. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Juvenile cleaner fish, after observing adults interacting with model clients (plates) that respond negatively to cheating (either by fleeing or punishing), subsequently behave more cooperatively (feed more against their preference) and prefer partners that are tolerant to cheating. Social learning about the consequences of cheating shapes cooperative strategies, but juveniles do not simply copy arbitrary preferences of adults. The study provides evidence that social learning about sanctions can promote cooperation in a non-human animal, but does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff as in economic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that observation of punishment or negative partner responses can increase cooperative behavior in a dyadic animal model. While not a public goods game, the findings suggest that the presence of observable sanctions can bias individuals toward more cooperative choices, which in analogous human or multi-player games could translate to higher efficiency if similar mechanisms operate. However, the lack of direct payoff or efficiency measurement, and the dyadic (not group) structure, limit direct transfer to the prediction of efficiency in public goods games with punishment. The main value is mechanistic: social learning about sanctions can promote cooperation.

- source: Dong, D. K., & Li, S. (2023). How a Smart regulatory platform can improve the property fee adjustment dilemma. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that under traditional regulation, the game between homeowners and property management tends to a 'prisoner's dilemma' with mutual defection (betrayal). Introducing a smart regulatory platform with government oversight, information transparency, and punishment for non-cooperation shifts the evolutionary stable strategy toward mutual cooperation. Stronger regulation and higher punishment increase the speed and likelihood of cooperation, while higher property fee increases make cooperation less likely. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulation of the evolutionary game, not on empirical data. The outcomes are expressed in terms of cooperation rates, not explicit efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theory-based evidence that introducing punishment and regulatory oversight in a repeated social dilemma can increase cooperation rates, especially when punishment is strong and information is transparent. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it use a standard public goods game. The results suggest that, in similar environments, enabling punishment and increasing regulatory strength would likely increase cooperation and, by implication, could improve efficiency, but this is not directly measured. The model's structure and findings may inform predictions about the effect of punishment in PGG-like environments, especially regarding the importance of punishment strength and transparency, but the lack of direct efficiency outcomes limits its predictive value for the target task.

- source: Julle-Danière, E., Whitehouse, J., Vrij, A., Gustafsson, E., & Waller, B. M. (2020). The social function of the feeling and expression of guilt. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that individuals who feel more guilt after being blamed for a cooperative failure allocate more of a shared reward to their partner, regardless of friendship strength. When the victim of a transgression (Player 2) perceives their partner as guilty, they are more likely to 'punish' (i.e., take more for themselves) if the partner is a close friend, though this effect is sensitive to outliers. The expression of guilt thus has a complex social function, sometimes leading to harsher punishment from close friends. The main behavioral outcome is the reward split in a dictator game, not efficiency or group payoff. The study does not directly address the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is adjacent to PGGs, using a one-shot dictator game after a cooperative task with induced guilt, but does not involve repeated contribution decisions, group efficiency, or explicit punishment mechanisms as in standard PGGs. The main outcome is the allocation of a fixed reward, interpreted as relationship repair or punishment, but not as efficiency. The findings suggest that social context (friendship, perceived guilt) can modulate punitive or reparative behavior, but do not provide quantitative evidence on how punishment affects efficiency in PGG-like games. Thus, the paper is more informative about the social and emotional moderators of punishment-like behavior than about efficiency outcomes relevant to the prediction task.

- source: Goodman, J. R., Caines, A., & Foley, R. A. (2023). Shibboleth: An agent-based model of signalling mimicry. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost
  findings: The models show that increasing the sensitivity to mimicry (the ability to detect outsiders) is a much more effective way to maintain group-defining traits than increasing the cost imposed on detected mimics. Punishment mechanisms (altruistic punishment of in-group members who are insufficiently sensitive) can help maintain group traits, but their effect is less dramatic than that of detection sensitivity. The cost of punishment, even when high, does not strongly predict which trait will go to fixation. The models do not report group efficiency or payoff, but focus on trait fixation as a proxy for group integrity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or surplus. However, it provides indirect theoretical support for the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in maintaining cooperation (or group integrity) depends more on the likelihood of detection than on the severity of punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that punishment mechanisms that increase detection or monitoring may be more effective than those that simply increase punishment cost. The findings are most relevant to environments where group identity or norm compliance is the main outcome, rather than efficiency or payoff.

- source: Helbing, D., & Johansson, A. (2010). Cooperation, Norms, and Revolutions: A Unified Game-Theoretical Approach. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the dynamics and outcomes of cooperation and norm establishment in multi-population games depend critically on the payoff structure (game type), the relative power of populations, and initial conditions. In multi-population prisoner's dilemma games, defection dominates regardless of power. In stag hunt games, shared norms can emerge, often set by the more powerful or initially larger group, but sometimes by the weaker group depending on initial conditions. In snowdrift games, polarization and abrupt 'revolutionary' transitions can occur. Costly punishment can transform a prisoner's dilemma into a stag hunt, supporting norm establishment. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral equilibria and norm dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how punishment, payoff structure, and group power shape the emergence of cooperation and norms in environments related to, but not identical with, public goods games. It suggests that punishment can promote norm establishment by changing the effective game structure, but does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it supports the idea that punishment can shift equilibria toward more cooperative outcomes in some settings, but does not quantify efficiency gains or losses. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of game structure and group asymmetries as moderators of punishment effects.

- source: Li, Q., Iqbal, A., Perc, M., Chen, M. Y., & Abbott, D. (2013). Coevolution of Quantum and Classical Strategies on Evolving Random Networks. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when agents can punish by reducing link weights, cooperation can dominate for a wider range of temptation values, especially when the network structure is updated frequently. Stronger punishment (lower minimum link weight) increases the range where cooperation dominates. As the temptation to defect increases, quantum strategies eventually dominate. The number of neighbors (network degree) has little effect if all agents have equal degree. The network evolves to have higher clustering and small-world properties. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the findings are about strategy prevalence, not direct payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (reducing link weights) on the prevalence of cooperation in an evolving network Prisoner's Dilemma. It suggests that stronger punishment and more frequent network updating support cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only adjacent: it supports the idea that punishment can sustain cooperation under some conditions, but does not quantify efficiency gains or losses. The game is not a public goods game, but a networked PD with quantum strategies, so transfer to PGG-like settings should be cautious.

- source: Wang, S. Y., Yao, X., Yang, Y. M., Chen, D. N., Wang, R. W., & Xie, F. J. (2023). Super-rational aspiration promotes cooperation in the asymmetric game with peer exit punishment and reward. *Heliyon*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that in structured populations, higher super-rational aspiration and greater asymmetry both promote the prevalence of cooperation (i.e., higher proportion of cooperators) and participation (i.e., fewer loners). Exit punishment (negative exit payoff) and exit reward (positive exit payoff) have distinct effects: exit punishment, combined with high super-rationality, can lead to coexistence of cooperators, defectors, and loners, while exit reward tends to result in coexistence of defectors and loners at low super-rationality, but can also promote cooperation at higher super-rationality. The effect of selection intensity is quantitative, not qualitative. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that peer-based exit punishment and reward, as well as super-rational aspiration and asymmetry, can influence the prevalence of cooperation and participation in public-goods-like social dilemmas. However, because the outcomes are reported as strategy frequencies rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency changes due to punishment. The results suggest that punishment can promote cooperation under certain conditions, but do not quantify the impact on group efficiency or welfare. Thus, the paper is useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms but provides only indirect, non-quantitative support for efficiency prediction.

- source: Nowak, M. A., & Sigmund, K. (2005). Evolution of indirect reciprocity. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that indirect reciprocity, supported by reputation mechanisms and sometimes by costly punishment (altruistic punishment), can stabilize cooperation in social dilemmas. Theoretical models show that discriminating strategies (which punish defectors by withholding help) can be evolutionarily stable under certain conditions, especially when information about others' behavior is available.
