  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds a positive correlation between social comparison sensitivity and justice sensitivity, both in behavioral tasks (UG and SCG) and in self-report inventories. In the UG, participants are more likely to reject disadvantageous offers, and this tendency is associated with their behavior in the SCG. The results suggest that social comparison motives underlie fairness-related decisions, particularly the rejection of unfair offers. However, the study does not include a public goods game, does not manipulate or analyze punishment as a separate stage, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses a modified ultimatum game (dyadic, proposer-responder) and a social comparison game, focusing on individual differences in fairness and comparison sensitivity. There is no group interaction, no punishment stage as in PGGs, and no efficiency or group payoff data. The findings may be relevant for understanding motives behind rejection (which can be interpreted as a form of costly punishment), but the lack of group-level payoff outcomes and absence of a PGG or punishment-enabled/control design means this paper offers only indirect, limited support for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Mancini, A., Betti, V., Panasiti, M. S., Pavone, E. F., & Aglioti, S. M. (2011). Suffering Makes You Egoist: Acute Pain Increases Acceptance Rates and Reduces Fairness during a Bilateral Ultimatum Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that acute pain leads to higher acceptance rates of unfair offers and lower fairness in offers made, suggesting that pain induces more self-interested behavior in both roles of the Ultimatum Game. The effect is specific to pain (not just heat) and is most pronounced for moderately unfair offers. The intensity of pain predicts acceptance rates for these offers. However, the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses the Ultimatum Game, which is adjacent but not equivalent to PGGs, and focuses on the effect of pain (a state manipulation) rather than institutional punishment. The outcomes are behavioral (acceptance, offer size) rather than payoff-based. Thus, its relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games is limited.

- source: Han, X., Cao, S. N., Shen, Z. S., Zhang, B. Y., Wang, W. X., Cressman, R., & Stanley, H. E. (2017). Emergence of communities and diversity in social networks. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that in static network structures (both regular and random bipartite networks), proposers form stable local communities with diverse offers, while in randomly rewired networks, proposers become homogeneous. The diversity in offers (SD of p) is much higher in structured networks. Responders' behavior is heterogeneous but not affected by network structure. The mechanism is that rational proposers best-respond to their local responder 'leaders,' whose heterogeneous minimum acceptance levels drive local agreement. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or surplus, and does not include a public goods game or explicit costly punishment mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experiment uses the ultimatum game, where 'punishment' is the rejection of offers, not a costly sanctioning mechanism. The main outcomes are behavioral (offer diversity, community formation), not payoff-based. Therefore, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games. It may provide some indirect context about how local interaction structure and heterogeneity can affect behavioral diversity, but not efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Bengart, P., Gruendler, T., & Vogt, B. (2021). Acute tryptophan depletion in healthy subjects increases preferences for negative reciprocity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that lowering serotonin via ATD increases negative reciprocity in the Hawk-Dove game: participants in the ATD group are more likely to choose Hawk in response to an expected Hawk, compared to placebo. This effect is specific to negative reciprocity and does not extend to positive reciprocity or beliefs about the opponent's action. The incentive for mutual cooperation (payoff for mutual Dove) reduces Hawk choices. Risk attitude and mood are unaffected by ATD and do not predict strategy choice. The study does not analyze or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that biological factors (serotonin availability) can modulate negative reciprocity in a competitive, dyadic game, but it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment mechanisms or sanctions in public goods or similar games. The Hawk-Dove game is structurally adjacent to public goods and punishment games, but lacks explicit punishment or sanctioning stages, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Therefore, the findings are only weakly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The main relevance is in showing that negative reciprocity (costly, non-payoff-maximizing responses to perceived unkindness) can be increased by serotonin depletion, but the translation to efficiency or group payoff in multi-player, repeated, or sanction-enabled games is limited.

- source: Ma, L. K., Tunney, R. J., & Ferguson, E. (2014). Gratefully Received, Gratefully Repaid: The Role of Perceived Fairness in Cooperative Interactions. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that recipients of unconditional (free) help perceive the donor as more genuinely helpful, feel more gratitude, and report a higher tendency to reciprocate in the future compared to recipients of conditional (repayment-required) help. Among those who received conditional offers and accepted, 38% defaulted (either partially or fully) on repayment, suggesting a behavioral response to perceived unfairness. The effect of offer conditionality on willingness to reciprocate is serially mediated by perceived helpfulness and gratitude. The study does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and the game is a one-shot, dyadic trust game rather than a repeated public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how conditionality (akin to punishment or sanctions) affects willingness to reciprocate and actual repayment behavior in a one-shot, dyadic trust game. It does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings suggest that perceived fairness and the framing of help (conditional vs. unconditional) can influence cooperation and defaulting, which may be relevant for understanding psychological mechanisms in cooperation, but the results cannot be directly used to predict efficiency changes in public goods games with punishment enabled or disabled.

- source: Frey, V., & de Rijt, A. V. (2016). Arbitrary Inequality in Reputation Systems. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that when trustors can observe the outcomes of others' exchanges (information sharing), they are more likely to repeatedly select the same trustee, leading to 'reputation cascades' and increasing inequality in how often trustees are chosen. This effect is strongest when there is a risk of trust abuse (the 'trust problem' condition). The paper shows that these cascades are arbitrary, as trustees with similar trustworthiness can end up with very different exchange volumes. While the paper discusses payoffs in the game structure, it does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. The main focus is on behavioral dynamics of trust and reputation, not on efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of information sharing and reputation systems on behavioral outcomes in trust-based exchange games. It does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods or punishment games, as it does not report these outcomes. However, it suggests that increased information sharing can increase trust and exchange, but also leads to arbitrary inequality in participation. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, as the game structure and outcomes differ from standard PGGs and do not include punishment or efficiency as primary outcomes.

- source: Madeo, D., & Mocenni, C. (2020). Self-regulation versus social influence for promoting cooperation on networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that strong self-regulation (relative to social influence from neighbors) can lead to full cooperation in networked prisoner's dilemma games, while weak self-regulation leads to defection. For intermediate self-regulation, complex behaviors such as partial cooperation, reciprocity, and anti-reciprocity emerge. Network topology matters: scale-free networks foster higher average cooperation than Erdös-Rényi or regular networks, especially when self-regulation is moderate. The model suggests that social diversity and heterogeneity in network structure can promote cooperation, even when the 'cost' of self-regulation is not high. However, the study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff directly, nor does it include explicit punishment or reward treatments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It models a continuous-strategy prisoner's dilemma with self-regulation (an internal, not externally imposed, mechanism) and analyzes cooperation levels, not efficiency or group payoff. There is no manipulation of punishment, punishment cost, or related design dimensions. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding how intrinsic motivation or network structure can affect cooperation, but do not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled efficiency.

- source: Huang, K. K., Zheng, X. P., Li, Z. J., & Yang, Y. Q. (2015). Understanding Cooperative Behavior Based on the Coevolution of Game Strategy and Link Weight. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that allowing link weights between players to coevolve with strategies in a spatial prisoner's dilemma can significantly enhance the fraction of cooperators, especially at intermediate values of link weight amplitude. The mechanism works by reinforcing links to successful neighbors and weakening links to less successful ones, which leads to the formation and expansion of cooperator clusters. The effect is non-monotonic: too little or too much link weight adaptation is less effective than an intermediate level. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods or punishment games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It uses a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a PGG) and studies the coevolution of strategies and link weights (not explicit punishment or sanctions). The main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency or payoff. However, the findings may be contextually informative for understanding how dynamic network structures or adaptive relationships can promote cooperation, which could be relevant for models where network adaptation or endogenous partner selection is a feature. For the core prediction task, this paper offers only adjacent, indirect support.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The study finds that introducing environment-based preference selection (parameter \alpha) and environmental heterogeneity (parameter u) into a spatial prisoner's dilemma game increases the fraction of cooperators. The combination of these mechanisms is more effective than either alone. The effect is robust across a range of parameters and network sizes. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The findings are thus relevant for understanding cooperation dynamics but not directly for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.

It does not include punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes. Its findings on cooperation rates in spatial prisoner's dilemma games with preference selection and heterogeneity may be contextually informative for understanding mechanisms that promote cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Podder, S., Righi, S., & Takács, K. (2021). Local reputation, local selection, and the leading eight norms. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that the leading eight social norms for indirect reciprocity can maintain high levels of cooperation in networked populations, even when reputation and strategy updating are local rather than global. However, the ability to sustain cooperation depends on the accuracy of reputation transmission, the speed of evolutionary updating, and the specific norm used. Norms that reward justified defection are less robust to noise and invasion by defectors. The main outcome is the proportion of cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. Its findings are adjacent: they show that certain reputation-based norms can sustain high cooperation in repeated pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma games on networks, and that the robustness of cooperation depends on information and evolutionary parameters. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper suggests that the structure of information and the harshness of social norms can affect the sustainability of cooperation, but it does not quantify efficiency or payoff outcomes. Use this paper for qualitative context about the importance of information structure and norm design, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Gou, Z. Q., & Li, Y. (2023). Prisoner's dilemma game model Based on historical strategy information. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that increasing the length of historical memory (M) and the weight of memory information (β) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma model leads to higher cooperation rates and more stable cooperative clusters. The effect saturates at high memory lengths, and excessive memory does not further promote cooperation. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and all outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rate and strategy stability, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It models a spatial prisoner's dilemma with memory-based strategy updating, focusing on how memory and its weight affect cooperation rates. There is no manipulation or analysis of punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes. Thus, its relevance to the downstream prediction task is limited to providing indirect, adjacent evidence that memory and historical information can promote cooperation, but it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Danku, Z., Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2019). Knowing the past improves cooperation in the future. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that when agents in spatial social dilemma games (prisoner's dilemma, snowdrift, stag-hunt) update their strategies based on a combination of current and past payoffs, cooperation is strongly promoted compared to the standard model where only current payoffs matter. This effect is robust to the details of how the past is incorporated (weighted average or single past payoff with decaying probability). The mechanism works by slowing down the invasion of defectors into cooperative clusters, allowing cooperation to persist and spread. The effect is most pronounced when the memory window is sufficiently long or the selected past payoff is not too far in the past. The results are shown for the stationary fraction of cooperators, not for efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or closely related environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are about how memory of past payoffs (a strategy-neutral mechanism) can promote cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. While the results may be conceptually relevant for understanding how information and history affect cooperation, they do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task, except perhaps as very weak, indirect context for the role of history or memory in cooperation.

- source: Santos, F. P., Pacheco, J. M., Paiva, A., & Santos, F. C. (2017). Structural power and the evolution of collective fairness in social networks. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the structural power of a network—a measure of how often individuals appear in each other's groups—strongly predicts the evolution of fairer strategies in Multiplayer Ultimatum Games. Networks with high SP (such as regular rings or modular community structures) promote higher average offers and acceptance thresholds, leading to more equitable outcomes. The mechanism is that repeated interactions within overlapping groups allow individuals to exert more influence and enforce fairness, analogous to effective punishment. However, the paper does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on behavioral measures of fairness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction of efficiency in public-goods-like environments with punishment, by showing that network structure (specifically, high structural power) can promote fairer behavioral outcomes in group bargaining games. However, because the model is based on the Multiplayer Ultimatum Game (not a public goods game), does not include explicit costly punishment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that structural features enabling repeated peer influence may enhance the effectiveness of fairness enforcement (analogous to punishment), but do not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment or other design dimensions.

- source: Hock, K., & Fefferman, N. H. (2011). Violating Social Norms when Choosing Friends: How Rule-Breakers Affect Social Networks. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when individuals in a network break social conventions for choosing partners, the effects depend on the convention in use. In 'intermediary-based' (betweenness) networks, rule-breakers primarily affect their own social position, and only at higher frequencies do they impact group organization. In 'popularity-based' (in-degree) networks, rule-breakers do not affect their own or others' individual positions, but as their frequency increases, they significantly alter the overall network structure (decentralization). The paper discusses the potential for rule-breaking to transform social systems, but does not measure or report efficiency, payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or measure punishment, efficiency, or payoff, nor does it use a PGG or close variant. Its findings on how rule-breaking alters network structure may be contextually relevant for understanding the broader consequences of norm violation, but do not inform quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The paper may be useful for mechanism discussion or for understanding the importance of social structure, but not for direct prediction in the target task.

- source: Bshary, R., & Grutter, A. S. (2006). Image scoring and cooperation in a cleaner fish mutualism. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The study shows that client fish preferentially associate with cleaner fish that are observed to behave cooperatively, providing evidence for image scoring in a non-human animal. Cleaner fish, in turn, behave more cooperatively (feed against their preference) when their actions are being observed by a potential client (image-scoring scenario) compared to when they are not observed. This supports the hypothesis that reputation-based partner choice can promote cooperation. However, the study does not measure group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and the experimental setup is not a public goods game but an asymmetric mutualism with partner choice.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation mechanisms (image scoring) can increase cooperative behavior in asymmetric mutualisms, which may be relevant for understanding the effects of reputation or partner choice in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their close variants. The findings may inform predictions about the role of observation and reputation in promoting cooperation, but not about the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Yamamoto, H., Okada, I., & Suzuki, T. (2025). Gradual reputation dynamics evolve and sustain cooperation in indirect reciprocity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The experimental results show that people use a three-level (good, neutral, bad) reputation system and update reputations gradually: cooperation increases reputation by one level, defection decreases it by one, except that defection against a bad recipient (justified defection) leaves reputation unchanged. Theoretical evolutionary game modeling demonstrates that, under certain conditions, tolerant norms that cooperate with good and neutral and defect only against bad can dominate and sustain high cooperation rates. However, the study does not use a public goods game or measure group efficiency or payoff; it focuses on reputation dynamics and the evolution of cooperation in indirect reciprocity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its findings are adjacent: it informs how nuanced reputation and justified defection (a form of social sanction) are perceived and how these perceptions can support cooperation in indirect reciprocity. However, it does not manipulate or measure punishment costs, punishment effectiveness, or group payoffs, nor does it use a PGG or similar economic game. Thus, it should be used only as indirect, contextual evidence about the psychological and evolutionary underpinnings of social sanctions and cooperation.

- source: Bshary, R., Grutter, A. S., Willener, A. S. T., & Leimar, O. (2008). Pairs of cooperating cleaner fish provide better service quality than singletons. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: Pairs of cleaner fish provide higher service quality to clients than singletons, mainly because females behave more cooperatively in pairs. Theoretical modeling shows that cooperation in pairs can yield higher service quality if there is added search efficiency, and empirical data (field and lab) support this. Aggression by males toward cheating females may function as a form of punishment, but this is not systematically manipulated or measured as a treatment. The outcomes are behavioral (cheating, cooperation, aggression), not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that, in a biological system with prisoner's dilemma-like structure, the presence of a partner (pair vs singleton) can increase cooperative behavior and service quality, especially when there is some mechanism for partner control (e.g., aggression/punishment). However, the study does not use a public goods game, does not manipulate punishment as a treatment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff. Thus, it is only adjacent to the prediction task and should be used as weak, indirect support for the idea that social context and partner control can moderate cooperation and service quality.

- source: Wiessner, P. (2020). The role of third parties in norm enforcement in customary courts among the Enga of Papua New Guinea. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The study documents that in Enga customary courts, norm violations are addressed primarily through restorative justice: compensation to victims, reintegration of wrongdoers, and broad third-party participation in settlements. Punitive sanctions (punishment) are rare and not institutionally emphasized. Third parties contribute to compensation both during and outside court hearings, motivated by kinship, reputation, and community harmony. Repeated offenders and free riders receive less support over time. The system is designed to maintain cooperation and social cohesion, not to maximize punishment or deterrence. The paper argues that such restorative systems are more effective for sustaining cooperation in small-scale societies than third-party punishment, but does not provide quantitative efficiency or payoff data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides contextual and ethnographic evidence that, in small-scale societies like the Enga, restorative justice and third-party compensation are preferred over punitive sanctions for norm enforcement. While it does not provide quantitative data on efficiency or group payoff, it suggests that punishment is not the primary or most effective means of sustaining cooperation in such environments. For prediction tasks involving public goods games or efficiency outcomes, this paper is mainly informative for understanding the limits of punishment's effectiveness and the importance of alternative, restorative mechanisms. It does not provide direct evidence for how punishment affects efficiency in public goods games or similar experimental settings.

  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A

  findings: The study finds that cooperation (helping behavior) among union members is sustained not by explicit punishment, perfect monitoring, or reputation, but by a focal-point equilibrium where members help others in expectation of reciprocal help when they need it. Most members leave the union after their own disputes are resolved, indicating that helping is conditional and self-interested rather than purely altruistic. The equilibrium is supported by direct observation and mutual monitoring during joint activities, but not by formal sanctions or payoff-based incentives. The paper provides qualitative evidence and a theoretical model, but does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar laboratory environments. It provides qualitative, contextual evidence about how cooperation can be sustained in a real-world OLG setting with limited observability, but does not report or manipulate efficiency, payoff, or formal punishment mechanisms. The findings may be useful for understanding the range of mechanisms that can support cooperation in loosely knit groups, but do not provide quantitative or experimental evidence relevant to the prediction task.

- source: Pisor, A. C., Gervais, M. M., Purzycki, B. G., & Ross, C. T. (2020). Preferences and constraints: the value of economic games for studying human behaviour. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that economic games can be strategically designed to reveal either private-world or real-world preferences, depending on which constraints are relaxed (e.g., resource constraints, anonymity, framing). In the Colombian case study, allocation and punishment behaviors in the games map onto real-world social variables (e.g., kinship, ethnicity, need), but also reveal preferences that are masked in observational data due to real-world constraints. Costly punishment (costly reduction) is more likely to be directed at wealthy or high-status individuals, and less likely at marginalized individuals, but the paper does not analyze the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff. The main contribution is methodological: showing how game design choices affect what is measured and how results relate to real-world behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it discusses costly punishment (costly reduction) games, the outcomes are about who is punished and why, not about group payoff or efficiency. The findings are useful for understanding how punishment behavior is shaped by social context and game design, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency. The paper is most useful for methodological considerations and for understanding the importance of matching game design to research questions and local context.

- source: Yan, M. H., Mathew, S., & Boyd, R. (2023). Doing what others do does not stabilize continuous norms. *Pnas Nexus*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in models where norm-relevant behaviors are continuous (e.g., contribution levels in a public goods context), similarity-based payoffs (including social sanctions or punishment for deviating from the mean) do not stabilize arbitrary or costly norms. Instead, the equilibrium norm is determined by direct effects (such as individual or group payoffs, moral preferences, or cognitive/cultural attractors). Only in discrete norm spaces, or when categorization or multiple cognitive attractors are present, can similarity-based payoffs maintain multiple stable equilibria or persistent between-group differences. For public goods-like environments, this suggests that punishment or social sanctions alone are insufficient to maintain high-cooperation norms unless additional forces (e.g., moral preferences, discrete categories, or multiple optima) are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in continuous public goods games or similar environments, enabling punishment or social sanctions (modeled as similarity-based payoffs) will not, by itself, maintain high-efficiency (cooperative) equilibria. The equilibrium will be determined by direct payoff incentives, not by the presence of punishment, unless the game is discretized or there are additional forces (e.g., moral preferences, cognitive attractors) that create multiple optima. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that, in continuous-choice PGGs, the presence of punishment may not increase efficiency unless the design includes discrete choices, strong moral framing, or other mechanisms that create stable high-cooperation equilibria. The paper does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency, but it strongly suggests that punishment alone is not sufficient for efficiency gains in continuous settings.

- source: Song, L., Yu, Z. L., & He, Q. (2023). Evolutionary game theory and simulations based on doctor and patient medical malpractice. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The study finds that increasing the benefits of standardized (legal) practice for doctors, increasing penalties for illegal practice, and raising the cost to patients of pursuing malpractice claims all increase the probability that doctors will choose standardized, cooperative strategies and that patients will not pursue malpractice. The model suggests that effective reward and punishment mechanisms for doctors, as well as third-party oversight, can reduce medical malpractice. However, all findings are in terms of behavioral strategy probabilities, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides only indirect, adjacent evidence for the prediction task. While it models a social dilemma with punishment-like mechanisms, it is not a public goods game or close variant, and it does not report efficiency or group payoff. The results may suggest that increasing punishment for non-cooperation (illegal practice) can increase cooperative behavior, but the lack of payoff or efficiency outcomes limits its direct utility for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments. The model's focus on behavioral strategy frequencies rather than payoffs means it should be used only as weak, contextual support.

- source: Capraro, V., & Perc, M. (2021). Mathematical foundations of moral preferences. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that a wide range of unselfish behaviors in one-shot, anonymous economic games are better explained by a 'moral preference hypothesis'—that is, people have preferences for following their own personal norms about what is right or wrong, beyond the monetary consequences of their actions. This framework organizes and explains empirical deviations from standard social preference models in games such as the dictator game, ultimatum game (altruistic punishment), Prisoner's Dilemma (cooperation), trust game (trustworthiness), sender-receiver game (truth-telling), and trade-off games (equality vs. efficiency). The review also discusses the practical implications of targeting personal norms (rather than social or injunctive norms) in interventions to promote pro-sociality, and proposes a formal utility function incorporating personal norms. However, the paper does not provide new data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes, nor does it analyze the effect of punishment or sanctions in multi-player, repeated, or public goods games. Its main contribution is theoretical: it suggests that personal moral norms are a key driver of unselfish behavior (including punishment) in one-shot, anonymous games, and that interventions targeting personal norms may be more effective than those targeting social norms. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides only indirect, mechanistic context about why people might punish or cooperate, but not quantitative or design-specific evidence about efficiency effects.

- source: Yan, J. (2023). Personal sustained cooperation based on networked evolutionary game theory. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that cooperative strategies in networked evolutionary games lead to more cohesive and robust interpersonal networks, while betrayal strategies result in fragile, loosely connected networks that require continual addition of new nodes to persist. Trust and reputation are central to sustaining cooperation. The model simulates the evolution of trust and network structure but does not report on group payoffs, efficiency, or explicit punishment costs. Punishment is modeled as the social consequence of betrayal (loss of trust and relationship), not as a costly action. The findings are relevant for understanding the emergence and maintenance of cooperation in dynamic networks but do not directly inform the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. Its theoretical and simulation results are adjacent, focusing on network cohesion and the role of trust/reputation rather than on payoff-based outcomes or explicit punishment mechanisms. It may inform high-level reasoning about the importance of social consequences and network structure for cooperation, but it does not supply quantitative or experimental evidence for the prediction task. Use with caution and only as indirect, contextual background.

- source: Yukalov, V. I., Yukalova, E. P., & Sornette, D. (2013). Utility Rate Equations of Group Population Dynamics in Biological and Social Systems. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The authors develop a system-theoretic, utility-based model for the evolution of group fractions (cooperators, defectors, regulators) in self-organized societies. They show analytically that, under resource constraints, neither defectors nor regulators can exceed about 10% of the population in stable equilibrium. The model demonstrates that cooperation can persist without explicit punishment mechanisms, provided resources are finite. When regulators (punishers) are present, their fraction is also limited by resource costs. The model is applied to real-world data from countries, but only to estimate group fractions and stability, not efficiency or payoff. No direct predictions or measurements of efficiency, group payoff, or surplus are made.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar experimental designs. It offers a theoretical perspective that, in resource-constrained societies, both defectors and regulators (punishers) are limited in equilibrium, and cooperation can be stable even without explicit punishment. However, the model does not map onto the game design dimensions or efficiency outcomes used in experimental PGG research. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments, but may provide indirect, high-level context about the limits of punishment and defection in self-organized systems.

- source: HEINSOHN, R., & PACKER, C. (1995). COMPLEX COOPERATIVE STRATEGIES IN GROUP-TERRITORIAL AFRICAN LIONS. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The study finds that female lions show consistent individual differences in their willingness to participate in group territorial defense, with some acting as leaders and others as laggards. Laggards are recognized by leaders, who become more cautious in their presence but do not punish them or withdraw cooperation. Some laggards adjust their participation based on the odds of group success, while others do not. The diversity of strategies suggests that cooperation in large groups is more complex than predicted by simple reciprocity or punishment models. No evidence of punishment or sanctioning of laggards is found.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the maintenance of cooperation in group settings without formal punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It suggests that in naturalistic, high-stakes group conflicts, cooperation can persist with a mix of unconditional and conditional cooperators and laggards, and that punishment is not always used to enforce cooperation. However, because the study does not use a public goods game or measure efficiency/payoff outcomes, it offers only adjacent, qualitative insight for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. It highlights the possibility that cooperation can be stable without punishment, but does not provide quantitative or design-specific evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Kümmerli, R. (2011). A Test of Evolutionary Policing Theory with Data from Human Societies. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that (1) higher similarity among citizens is associated with lower crime rates (interpreted as higher cooperation), (2) lower similarity is associated with higher policing effort, and (3) increases in policing effort over time are associated with reductions in crime rates. These findings support evolutionary policing theory in a real-world context. However, the outcomes are not directly efficiency or payoff-based, but rather use crime rate as a behavioral proxy for defection/cooperation. The study does not use a public goods game or experimental design, and does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence that increased punishment (policing) can reduce defection (crime) in real-world settings, especially in less homogeneous populations. However, because it does not use a public goods game, does not manipulate punishment experimentally, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. Its main value is in supporting the general plausibility of punishment reducing defection, but it does not inform the quantitative relationship between game design dimensions and efficiency.

- source: Tucker, W. T., & Ferson, S. (2008). Evolved altruism, strong reciprocity, and perception of risk. *Strategies for Risk Communication: Evolution, Evidence, Experience*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that human cognitive architecture is shaped by evolutionary pressures to maintain cooperation and punish free riders in social contract networks, such as those modeled by public goods games. It reviews evidence that people are willing to incur personal costs to punish defectors, supporting the concept of 'strong reciprocity.' The authors suggest that these evolved mechanisms are central to risk perception and cooperation, and that they help explain why people often resist narrowly self-interested, rational-maximizing behavior in favor of fairness and contract maintenance. However, the paper does not provide new empirical data or direct analysis of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and contextual support for the idea that punishment mechanisms are evolutionarily grounded and play a key role in sustaining cooperation in public-goods-like environments. It supports the expectation that punishment can increase cooperation and deter free riding, but does not provide quantitative or direct empirical evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment is likely to increase cooperation and potentially efficiency, but does not provide parameter-specific or design-dimension-specific guidance. Its value is mainly in supporting the plausibility and generality of punishment effects, not in calibrating their magnitude or moderators.

- source: Tobeña, A. (2009). Lethal Altruists Itineraries along the Dark Outskirts of Moralistic Prosociality. *Values, Empathy, and Fairness Across Social Barriers*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that strong altruism (including costly punishment) and parochialism are important temperamental traits that can drive both prosocial and antisocial behaviors, such as martyrdom and suicide terrorism. It reviews evidence that some individuals are predisposed to punish norm violators at personal cost, and that these tendencies are influenced by genetic, neurobiological, and temperamental factors. The paper references behavioral economic games (ultimatum, dictator, third-party punishment) as tools for measuring these traits, but does not report new experimental results or analyze efficiency or group payoff. The main contribution is a conceptual framework for understanding the interplay of individual traits and group dynamics in extreme forms of moralistic prosociality.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it discusses the existence of costly punishment and its association with certain temperamental and genetic traits, it does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The references to behavioral games are used to illustrate individual differences in punishment and altruism, not to quantify their effects on group efficiency. Therefore, this paper offers only indirect, contextual support for the idea that costly punishment is a stable human trait, but does not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in experimental game settings.

- source: Andrighetto, G., & Vriens, E. (2022). A research agenda for the study of social norm change. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society A-mathematical Physical and Engineering Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that social norms, especially when enforced by mechanisms like punishment, can facilitate cooperation in collective action problems. It reviews advances in measuring the causal effect of norms, the use of agent-based models to study norm change and tipping points, and the importance of contextual and individual heterogeneity (e.g., tightness-looseness theory). The authors emphasize that while social norms can promote large-scale behavioral change, they can also reinforce undesirable or inefficient behaviors, and that their strength can be both a solution and a barrier to change. The paper calls for future research integrating empirical and computational methods to better predict and intervene in norm change processes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative findings that can be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. However, it offers theoretical and conceptual insights: (1) punishment is discussed as a key enforcement mechanism for social norms that can support cooperation; (2) the effectiveness of punishment and norm enforcement is context-dependent, influenced by factors like group heterogeneity and cultural tightness; (3) strong norms (and by extension, strong punishment) can sometimes lead to inefficiency or undesirable outcomes. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the impact of punishment on efficiency is likely to be moderated by norm strength, group context, and the presence of tipping points, but does not provide direct parameter-level evidence.

- source: Ge, E. H., Chen, Y., Wu, J. J., & Mace, R. (2019). Large-scale cooperation driven by reputation, not fear of divine punishment. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that cooperation (measured as donations) is higher in small, stable communities and among those with more public religious practice, but not among those with stronger belief in divine punishment. When reputational concerns are removed (private dice game), neither community size nor religiosity predicts honesty. The results suggest that reputation, not fear of supernatural punishment, drives large-scale cooperation in these settings. No evidence is provided on the effects of punishment or sanctions, nor on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experimental design does not include punishment, sanctions, or group payoff/efficiency measures. Its findings are only indirectly relevant: they suggest that reputational mechanisms (public observability, community stability) can increase prosocial behavior, but do not address the impact of punishment or efficiency. Thus, it should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task, except as very weak contextual support for the importance of reputation versus punishment.

- source: Clutton-Brock, T. (2009). Cooperation between non-kin in animal societies. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation between non-kin in animal societies is most often maintained by immediate mutualistic benefits or manipulative tactics (including punishment and coercion), rather than by direct reciprocity as modeled in iterated Prisoner's Dilemma or public goods games. It reviews evidence that punishment and coercion can enforce cooperation, but emphasizes that most animal cooperation occurs in kin groups and that non-kin cooperation with delayed payoffs (as in human economic games) is rare. The paper highlights the limitations of applying standard economic game models to animal societies, due to differences in partner choice, communication, and the immediacy of benefits.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and comparative context for interpreting the mechanisms that maintain cooperation and the role of punishment in animal societies. It suggests that, in natural animal groups, punishment and coercion can enforce cooperation, but that the structure and payoffs of animal cooperation often differ from those in laboratory public goods games. The paper does not provide quantitative evidence or direct predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games, but it does suggest that punishment is more likely to be effective in small, stable groups where benefits are immediate and cheating is costly. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency may depend on group structure, immediacy of benefits, and the ability to enforce sanctions, but it does not provide direct empirical estimates.

- source: Dawes, C. T., Fowler, J. H., Johnson, T., McElreath, R., & Smirnov, O. (2007). Egalitarian motives in humans. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that people are willing to pay to reduce or augment others' incomes even when there is no cooperative norm to enforce and no material gain from doing so. Both costly taking (punishment-like) and costly giving (reward-like) are common, and are strongly targeted by relative income: high earners are penalized, low earners are rewarded. Negative emotions towards high earners increase with inequality and predict more costly taking and giving. The design randomizes group composition and income, ensuring that actions cannot be motivated by reciprocity or reputation. The study provides evidence for egalitarian motives in income-altering behavior, but does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes in a public goods or cooperation context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as it does not include a contribution/cooperation stage or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs with punishment/reward, but isolates egalitarian motives by removing cooperation. The findings suggest that punishment-like and reward-like behaviors can be motivated by inequality aversion and negative emotions, not just norm enforcement. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in PGGs, this paper provides only indirect, contextual evidence about the psychological motives behind punishment/reward, not about their effect on efficiency.

- source: Ma, N., Li, N., He, X. S., Sun, D. L., Zhang, X. C., & Zhang, D. R. (2012). Rejection of Unfair Offers Can Be Driven by Negative Emotions, Evidence from Modified Ultimatum Games with Anonymity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that rejection rates of unfair offers are significantly above zero even when anonymity is strictly enforced, suggesting that negative emotions alone can drive costly punishment-like behavior. Rejection rates are higher in public (experimenter-observed) or 'informed' conditions, indicating that reputational concerns and cognitive processing of consequences further increase rejection. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and the game is a modified ultimatum game rather than a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its main contribution is to clarify the psychological motivations for costly punishment (rejection) in ultimatum-like games, distinguishing between emotional and reputational drivers. It does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so it cannot be used to calibrate the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like settings. It may be useful for understanding the psychological underpinnings of punishment behavior, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Yamagishi, T., Horita, Y., Mifune, N., Hashimoto, H., Li, Y., Shinada, M., Miura, A., Inukai, K., Takagishi, H., & Simunovic, D. (2012). Rejection of unfair offers in the ultimatum game is no evidence of strong reciprocity. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that individuals who reject unfair offers in the ultimatum game do not show greater prosociality or positive reciprocity in other economic games (dictator, trust, or prisoner's dilemma). The rejection of unfair offers is not correlated with prosocial value orientation or reciprocity scales, but is positively correlated with assertiveness. The results challenge the interpretation of UG rejection as evidence of strong reciprocity and suggest it may instead reflect a desire to avoid inferior status. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not use a public goods game or a punishment mechanism analogous to those in PGG experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The study is focused on the ultimatum game, which is structurally different from PGGs, and examines rejection (a form of costly punishment) as a behavioral outcome, not its effect on group efficiency or payoff. The findings suggest that rejection in the UG may not be motivated by the same prosocial or norm-enforcing preferences that drive punishment in PGGs. Therefore, the results caution against interpreting UG rejection as evidence for the effectiveness or motivation of punishment in PGG-like settings, but do not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency outcomes under punishment.

- source: de Melo, C. M., Terada, K., & Santos, F. C. (2021). Emotion expressions shape human social norms and reputations. *Iscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that participants cooperate more with counterparts who have positive or unknown reputations, use generous strategies, and display cooperative emotion expressions. Reputation, strategy, and emotion all independently and interactively affect cooperation rates and how reputations are updated. Emotion expressions can help forgive defectors with good reputations or rehabilitate those with bad reputations if they behave generously and express cooperative emotions. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses an iterated prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and focuses on cooperation rates and reputation rather than efficiency or group payoff. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the sense of costly punishment; instead, the study manipulates emotion expressions and reputation. Thus, its relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games is limited. It may be useful for understanding how nonverbal cues and reputation affect cooperation, but not for payoff-based prediction tasks.

- source: Singh, M., & Garfield, Z. H. (2022). Evidence for third-party mediation but not punishment in Mentawai justice. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that, in the Mentawai context, third-party punishment is absent: no cases were found where third parties (outside the victim, their close kin, or spouse) directly punished or demanded penalties from transgressors. Indirect or second-order sanctions by third parties are also not observed. Instead, penalties (tulou) are negotiated and paid primarily to restore dyadic cooperation, with kinship playing a major role in the likelihood and size of payments. Mediation by third parties (especially shamans and elders) is common, particularly in more severe or between-clan disputes, but the mediator's role is to facilitate negotiation, not to enforce punishment. The study suggests that, in this small-scale society, institutionalized penalties serve to repair relationships rather than to enforce group-level norms through punishment. The findings are based on retrospective interviews and ethnographic observation of 302 reliable cases of transgression and response.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The studied context is not a PGG or close variant, and the outcomes are not efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that, in small-scale, kin-based societies, third-party punishment is rare or absent, and penalties serve to restore dyadic cooperation rather than to enforce group norms or increase group efficiency. If extrapolating to PGG-like settings, the results would suggest that the presence or absence of third-party punishment may depend on the social structure and the nature of cooperation (private vs. public goods), but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency outcomes. Thus, it is of limited use for the downstream prediction task, except as a caution that punishment mechanisms may not always function as assumed in small-scale, kin-based societies.

- source: Yamamoto, H., & Suzuki, T. (2025). Exploring condition in which people accept AI over human judgements on justified defection. *Scientific Reports*.
