  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The experiments show that participants are generally neutral in judging justified defection (not helping a person with a bad reputation). When asked to accept the judgement of a manager (AI or human) who rates the defector as good or bad, participants' acceptance depends on the combination: they only prefer the AI's positive judgement over a human's negative judgement. In all other cases, acceptance is neutral. The study does not measure or report any efficiency, payoff, or group welfare outcomes, nor does it manipulate or analyze game-theoretic parameters such as MPCR, punishment cost, or group size.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods or indirect reciprocity games. It does not report or analyze any payoff-based outcomes, nor does it manipulate or report on game design dimensions such as MPCR, punishment cost, or group size. Its findings are limited to attitudinal acceptance of AI versus human judgements in a scenario of justified defection. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Wallace, B., Cesarini, D., Lichtenstein, P., & Johannesson, M. (2007). Heritability of ultimatum game responder behavior. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that individual differences in the willingness to reject unfair offers in the ultimatum game are substantially heritable, with additive genetic effects explaining about 42% of the variance in acceptance thresholds. The study uses a large sample of monozygotic and dizygotic twins from the Swedish Twin Registry and applies standard structural equation modeling. There is no evidence that common environment plays a significant role. The results are robust across sexes. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment institutions, and does not use a public goods game or a repeated game structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its relevance is limited to showing that individual differences in costly punishment behavior (as measured by ultimatum game rejections) have a substantial genetic component. It does not manipulate punishment institutions, does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, and does not use a public goods or repeated game design. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments with or without punishment.

- source: Jensen, K., Call, J., & Tomasello, M. (2007). Chimpanzees are vengeful but not spiteful. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: Chimpanzees do not engage in 'spiteful' punishment (i.e., incurring a cost to reduce another's payoff in response to disadvantageous outcomes alone), but do engage in 'vengeful' punishment (i.e., retaliating when a conspecific actively steals food from them). The tendency to retaliate declines over repeated sessions, and is not affected by kinship or prior reciprocity. There is no evidence that such punishment-like behavior enforces cooperation or increases group welfare, as stealing increases over time and retaliation declines. The study does not use a public goods game or measure efficiency, but provides evidence about the motivations underlying punishment-like behaviors in chimpanzees.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as it does not use a PGG or measure efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. However, it is informative for understanding the motivational basis of punishment: chimpanzees retaliate against harmful actions but do not punish mere outcome inequality. This suggests that, in species or contexts where punishment is only vengeful and not spiteful, punishment may not stabilize cooperation or improve efficiency as it does in human PGGs. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment mechanisms alone is not sufficient for efficiency gains; the underlying motivation and context (e.g., response to intentional harm vs. outcome disparity) matter. However, the lack of payoff outcomes and the non-PGG design limit its direct predictive value.

- source: Lin, C. A., & Bates, T. C. (2022). Free to choose: Mutualist motives for partner choice, proportional division, punishment, and help. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that mutualism is a reliable, stable, and discriminant psychological construct, with four components: partner choice, proportional sharing, proportional punishment, and proportional helping. Higher mutualism scores predict attitudinal opposition to equal sharing and redistribution, and preference for proportional division in hypothetical scenarios. The mutualism scale is largely independent of compassion, envy, self-interest, and utilitarianism. The findings are robust across two large samples. However, all outcomes are attitudinal or psychometric; there is no evidence on actual behavior in public goods games, punishment games, or on efficiency/welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It may be useful for understanding individual differences in motives that could influence behavior in such games, but it does not report any behavioral or payoff-based outcomes, nor does it manipulate or observe game design dimensions. Thus, it should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment interventions. Its main value is in providing a validated measure of mutualist motives, which could be used in future studies that do measure relevant outcomes.

- source: Basu, S., Dickhaut, J., Hecht, G., Towry, K., & Waymire, G. (2009). Recordkeeping alters economic history by promoting reciprocity. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Allowing subjects to keep records in a repeated multi-dyad trust game leads to higher mean investor returns (18.7% vs. 7.3% in periods 6-10) and lower risk (coefficient of variation 0.221 vs. 0.358) compared to no-recordkeeping conditions. Recordkeeping promotes reputation formation, more coordinated reciprocal behavior, and greater discrimination between trustworthy and untrustworthy partners. The effect is especially pronounced in later rounds. The study provides direct evidence that external memory (recordkeeping) can beneficially alter economic history by increasing efficiency and reducing risk in complex exchange environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that informational institutions like recordkeeping can substantially increase efficiency and reduce risk in repeated trust-based exchange, especially in complex (multi-partner) settings. While it does not study punishment, it demonstrates that mechanisms which improve memory and reputation tracking can have large positive effects on efficiency. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling information storage or reputation tracking (even without explicit punishment) can boost efficiency, particularly as the number of partners increases and over repeated rounds. The results are most directly relevant to settings where the main challenge is memory and reputation, not norm enforcement via punishment.

- source: Das Bairagya, J., Mondal, S. S., Chowdhury, D., & Chakraborty, S. (2023). Eco-evolutionary games for harvesting self-renewing common resource: effect of growing harvester population. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the realization or prevention of the tragedy of the commons (TOC) in a common-pool resource system depends critically on the relationship between the resource's intrinsic growth rate and the harvesting rates of the population, as well as on the incentive structure encoded in the payoff matrix. When the resource's growth rate is between the low and high harvesting rates, both collapse and partial/complete prevention of TOC are possible, depending on initial conditions and parameter values. Finite population size (carrying capacity) can introduce bistability and limit cycles, making the system's fate sensitive to initial conditions. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, but suggests that such extensions could be studied in future work. The main outcome relevant to efficiency is the long-run resource state, which serves as a proxy for group welfare or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how resource dynamics, harvesting strategies, and population size interact to determine group efficiency in common-pool resource settings. While it does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, it offers a detailed analysis of how efficiency (resource state) depends on game parameters such as harvesting rates, resource growth, and population carrying capacity. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for understanding baseline efficiency outcomes in the absence of punishment or reward, and for highlighting the importance of ecological feedbacks and population size. It does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does suggest that efficiency is highly sensitive to the incentive structure and ecological parameters. The discussion of future extensions to include policing or punishment indicates that the model could be adapted for such analyses.

- source: Ibbotson, P., Hauert, C., & Walker, R. (2019). Effort Perception is Made More Accurate with More Effort and When Cooperating with Slackers. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that participants are generally accurate in perceiving their partner's effort in a cooperative task, with higher accuracy when the partner is a slacker and when the participant invests more effort. The experimental design involves a two-player online game where both invest effort to move balls into a bucket, with rewards split 50/50. The main outcome is the accuracy of effort perception, not efficiency or payoff. Theoretical modeling suggests that strategies based on effort perception can help avoid inefficient cooperation, but this is not empirically tested in terms of group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. The findings are relevant to understanding how effort perception may support cooperation, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment or game design dimensions. The theoretical discussion of effort-prober strategies is adjacent to efficiency but not empirically linked to payoff outcomes.

- source: Knoch, D., Schneider, F., Schunk, D., Hohmann, M., & Fehr, E. (2009). Disrupting the prefrontal cortex diminishes the human ability to build a good reputation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when trustees know their past actions will be visible to future partners (reputation condition), they return significantly more to investors than in the anonymous condition. Disrupting the right DLPFC with rTMS eliminates this effect, causing trustees to behave as if reputation incentives are absent. The effect is strongest when the temptation to defect is highest (i.e., when the investor sends the maximum amount). The study provides causal evidence that the right DLPFC is necessary for overcoming short-term temptations to build a good reputation. However, the paper does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and efficiency or group payoff is not the primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is only weakly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design is a repeated trust game with reputation incentives, not a public goods game, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The main behavioral outcome is back-transfer rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings may be indirectly informative about the role of reputation and self-control in sustaining cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence about punishment effects or efficiency outcomes. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Francey, D., & Bergmüller, R. (2012). Images of Eyes Enhance Investments in a Real-Life Public Good. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Images of eyes, as subtle cues of being watched, increased the amount of time and effort people invested in removing garbage from public bus stop benches, but did not affect the likelihood that people would engage in garbage removal at all. The effect was not due to increased attention to a sign requesting garbage removal. The study provides field evidence that reputational cues can enhance non-monetary investments in public goods, but does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it involve punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or measure punishment, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its main relevance is as indirect evidence that reputational cues (such as being watched) can increase cooperative investment in real-world public good contexts, but this is not mediated by punishment or efficiency. It should not be used as primary evidence for the prediction task, but may provide weak contextual support for the importance of reputational mechanisms.

- source: Nockur, L., & Pfattheicher, S. (2020). Intuitive decision-making promotes rewarding prosocial others independent of the personality trait Honesty-Humility. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that instructing participants to make intuitive decisions (as opposed to reflective or control) increases the amount of reward given to prosocial allocators in a third-party reward task. Honesty-Humility, a personality trait, is not significantly associated with reward behavior. The effect of intuition is robust to controls for other personality dimensions and social value orientation. The study does not examine group efficiency, total payoff, or public goods game settings, and does not include punishment or sanctioning options.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is a one-shot third-party reward task based on a dictator game, not a repeated or group-based public goods game. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported. The findings may be weakly relevant for understanding the psychological drivers of rewarding prosocial behavior, but do not inform the effect of punishment or the efficiency consequences of institutional design in public goods games.

- source: Blair, G., Littman, R., & Paluck, E. L. (2019). Motivating the adoption of new community-minded behaviors: An empirical test in Nigeria. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study found that both a social norms intervention (film with role models) and a nudge intervention (mass text message) increased the number of people who reported corruption by text message in Nigerian communities. The mass text message had a much larger and more immediate effect than the film. The film shifted some perceptions of social norms (e.g., increased perceived prevalence of corruption and anger about corruption), but did not increase the perceived prevalence of reporting. There was no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, and no punishment or sanction mechanism was present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The experimental context is adjacent to public goods (encouraging costly community-minded behavior), but there is no group payoff, no efficiency measure, and no punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The findings may be of limited conceptual interest for understanding the adoption of rare cooperative behaviors, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: Liu, G. F., Lin, C. D., & Xin, Z. Q. (2014). The Effects of Within- and Between-Group Competition on Trust and Trustworthiness among Acquaintances. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both within-group and between-group competition increase trust (investment) among acquaintances in an investment game. However, within-group competition reduces trustworthiness (return ratio) and leads to lower payoffs for trustors but higher payoffs for trustees. Between-group competition increases trustee payoffs but does not significantly affect trustworthiness or trustor payoffs compared to the non-competition condition. The results suggest that competition type differentially affects efficiency for trustors and trustees, and that within-group competition can undermine overall group efficiency by harming trustor payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, introducing within-group competition can increase trust but reduce trustworthiness, leading to lower payoffs for trustors and higher payoffs for trustees. Between-group competition increases trust and trustee payoffs but does not harm trustor payoffs. Since the study does not include punishment, it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does show that competitive structures can have asymmetric effects on efficiency depending on the role. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the presence or absence of competition (and its type) can moderate efficiency outcomes, but the absence of punishment means the results are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled PGGs.

- source: Johnson, T., & Obradovich, N. (2025). Testing for completions that simulate altruism in early language models. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study demonstrates that certain LLMs (notably text-davinci-003 and GPT-4) generate completions that simulate both payoff-maximizing behavior in non-social tasks and altruistic sharing in dictator game scenarios. The simulated sharing rates for text-davinci-003 are close to those observed in human dictator game experiments, while less advanced models do not consistently maximize payoffs or share resources. The findings are robust to prompt variations, model parameters, and resource framing. No punishment, sanctioning, or public goods game (PGG) structure is present; the focus is on one-shot dictator games and non-social maximization tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does, however, offer high-quality evidence on LLMs' simulated behavior in dictator games and non-social maximization tasks, including payoff-based outcomes. Since there is no punishment or sanctioning, and the game is not a PGG or close variant, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. It may be useful for understanding LLMs' simulated social preferences or for comparison with human dictator game data, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Johnson, T., & Smirnov, O. (2018). Inequality as information: Wealth homophily facilitates the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a well-mixed population playing repeated one-shot prisoners' dilemma games, a strategy that uses wealth homophily (COEQUALS) can successfully invade and dominate a population of defectors, leading to high levels of cooperation and higher average payoffs (efficiency). The success of COEQUALS is robust to noise, population size, number of rounds, and the presence of certain mimics. The key moderator is the ability to observe the wealth of a sufficient portion of the population: when COEQUALS agents can view at least 10-20% of the population's wealth, the strategy spreads widely and average payoffs increase. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The findings suggest that visible inequality can serve as an informational mechanism to support cooperation and efficiency, even in the absence of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly informative for predicting efficiency in repeated prisoners' dilemma environments where agents can condition their cooperation on visible wealth (past payoffs), but it does not address punishment or sanctions. The findings suggest that mechanisms enabling positive assortment (e.g., wealth homophily) can substitute for punishment in supporting high efficiency. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is less directly relevant, but it does highlight the importance of informational cues and partner selection. The results also indicate that efficiency is robust to variations in player count, number of rounds, and noise, but is sensitive to the ability to observe others' payoffs. If a public goods game allows for similar informational mechanisms, efficiency may be high even without punishment.

- source: Efferson, C., Lalive, R., & Fehr, E. (2008). The coevolution of cultural groups and ingroup favoritism. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated two-player coordination game with two equilibria, allowing players to freely choose and maintain symbolic markers (shapes) leads to the endogenous formation of 'cultural groups' where markers become predictive of behavior. This results in strong ingroup favoritism (preference for same-marker partners), increased coordination on the optimal behavior, and significantly higher mean payoffs compared to a control where markers are randomized and cannot acquire meaning. When the payoff structure removes unobservable heterogeneity or markers are fixed, the effect disappears. The findings show that endogenous group formation via symbolic markers can substantially increase efficiency in coordination games, but only under specific conditions (marker mutability and persistent heterogeneity). No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in coordination games with multiple equilibria and persistent unobservable heterogeneity, allowing players to endogenously form groups via flexible symbolic markers can increase efficiency (mean payoff) by enabling better coordination. However, the design does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The results are most relevant for understanding how group markers and endogenous assortment affect efficiency, not punishment. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it does highlight the importance of group structure and endogenous assortment for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Lewis, H. M., Vinicius, L., Strods, J., Mace, R., & Migliano, A. B. (2014). High mobility explains demand sharing and enforced cooperation in egalitarian hunter-gatherers. *Nature Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The simulation demonstrates that enforced demand sharing (forced equal sharing of food among all camp members) combined with high mobility enables populations to survive in unpredictable environments, maintain high group efficiency (measured by survival, energy surplus, and life expectancy), and buffer against the presence of free riders. In contrast, loner (non-sharing) populations and sedentary (non-mobile) demand-sharing populations are much more likely to collapse, especially under harsh environmental conditions. The model explicitly excludes punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, showing that high efficiency and cooperation can be maintained without punishment, as long as mobility is high. The findings are directly relevant to understanding the efficiency of enforced cooperation in the absence of punishment, but do not inform the effect of adding punishment to a public-goods-like environment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as punishment is not present in the model. Instead, it shows that enforced cooperation (demand sharing) combined with high mobility can maintain high efficiency and group survival without the need for punishment. For the downstream prediction task—predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency—this paper suggests that, in some environments, enforced cooperation and mobility may substitute for punishment, but it does not provide direct evidence on the marginal effect of punishment. The model is adjacent to PGGs, but the absence of a punishment condition means it cannot be used to predict the efficiency impact of enabling punishment.

- source: Chiang, Y. S. (2015). Good Samaritans in Networks: An Experiment on How Networks Influence Egalitarian Sharing and the Evolution of Inequality. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that voluntary sharing in networks can reduce inequality, but the extent depends on network topology. When networks connect the very rich and very poor (Lattice_Hetero, SF_Negative), local inequality is salient and motivates more redistribution, leading to significant reductions in the Gini coefficient. In other network structures (Full, Lattice_Homo, SF_Positive), inequality is not significantly reduced. The study does not involve public goods provision, punishment, or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency as the ratio of group payoff to the fully cooperative benchmark. The findings are most relevant to how network structure moderates voluntary redistribution and the evolution of inequality.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency in the sense required for the downstream prediction task. The findings are about voluntary redistribution and inequality in networked settings, which is adjacent but not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The paper may provide some contextual insight into how network structure affects redistribution, but it does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Drouvelis, M., Gerson, J., Powdthavee, N., & Riyanto, Y. E. (2023). Large losses from little lies: Strategic gender misrepresentation and cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when participants are randomly allowed to misrepresent their gender to their partner in a one-shot Golden Balls game, the average defection (steal) rate increases by about 10-12 percentage points compared to control conditions (blind or true gender revealed). Those who actually choose to misrepresent are about 32 percentage points more likely to defect. The possibility of misrepresentation also increases defection among those who do not misrepresent, due to increased distrust. The result is a substantial reduction in overall cooperation and group welfare, as measured by the proportion of participants receiving the maximum possible payoff (£10 each for mutual cooperation) and the average payment per participant. The effect is robust across lab and online samples, and is not explained by differences in personality traits or risk/trust attitudes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing opportunities for strategic misrepresentation of identity (here, gender) in a one-shot social dilemma with communication reduces cooperation and group welfare, even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. While not a public goods game, the Golden Balls game is a close variant of the prisoner's dilemma, and the findings are likely to generalize to other one-shot or anonymous social dilemmas with communication. The results suggest that any design feature that increases uncertainty about partner identity or intentions (such as allowing misrepresentation) can reduce efficiency. For the prediction task, this paper supports the expectation that enabling deception or increasing uncertainty will lower efficiency, even if punishment is not present. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is included in the design.

- source: Grujic, J., Gracia-Lázaro, C., Milinski, M., Semmann, D., Traulsen, A., Cuesta, J. A., Moreno, Y., & Sánchez, A. (2014). A comparative analysis of spatial Prisoner's Dilemma experiments: Conditional cooperation and payoff irrelevance. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The main findings are: (1) In spatial Prisoner's Dilemma experiments on lattices, cooperation rates start high but quickly decline to low, nonzero levels (15-35%). (2) Defectors consistently earn more than cooperators, with a significant negative correlation between cooperation and earnings. (3) The behavioral rule most consistent with the data is moody conditional cooperation, where players' probability to cooperate depends on their own previous action and the number of cooperating neighbors. (4) There is no evidence that spatial structure (network topology, number of neighbors, or system size) promotes higher cooperation or changes the behavioral rule. (5) Payoff-based imitation rules (imitate-the-best, Fermi rule) do not explain the observed behavior. (6) The paper does not study punishment, rewards, or sanctions, and does not report group efficiency relative to the fully cooperative outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It does, however, provide indirect evidence that in the absence of punishment, cooperation rates and group payoffs remain low in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma games, and that spatial structure alone does not increase efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper may help establish a baseline for control (no-punishment) efficiency in spatial PD-like environments, but offers no information about the effect of enabling punishment. The behavioral findings (moody conditional cooperation) may inform models of baseline behavior in the absence of incentives.

- source: Molleman, L., Pen, I., & Weissing, F. J. (2013). Effects of Conformism on the Cultural Evolution of Social Behaviour. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of conformism on the evolution of cooperation depends on the game structure. In social dilemmas, conformism can make cooperation a stable equilibrium if it is already common, but in group-structured populations, conformism hinders the spread of cooperation because it prevents rare cooperative strategies from invading. In evasion games, conformism erodes polymorphic equilibria and leads to fixation of a single strategy. The main outcomes are fixation probabilities and average payoffs at equilibrium, which are related to efficiency. The paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or reward mechanisms, and does not directly model public goods games, but the results are relevant for understanding how social learning biases affect group-level cooperation and welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or reward mechanisms. However, it does provide theoretical insight into how social learning rules (payoff-based vs. conformist) affect the spread and stability of cooperation and group payoffs in two-strategy games, including social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant: it suggests that mechanisms which stabilize cooperation (such as conformism) may not always increase group efficiency, especially in group-structured populations. However, since punishment is not modeled, and the game structure is not a PGG or its direct variant, this paper should be considered only for background or mechanistic context, not for quantitative prediction.

- source: Yang, C. L., & Yue, C. S. J. (2019). Cooperation in an Assortative Matching Prisoners Dilemma Experiment with Pro-Social Dummies. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that assortative matching based on recent cooperation history can increase cooperation rates compared to random matching, but the effect is inconsistent across groups. Introducing pro-social dummies (always-cooperating computer players) into the assortative matching environment (WHc) leads to a substantial and robust increase in cooperation rates for all types of players, including those initially inclined to defect. The presence of dummies shifts the distribution of cooperation rates to higher values and reduces the prevalence of all-defection. Payoff is positively correlated with cooperation in the assortative matching treatments, but efficiency or group welfare is not directly measured or reported. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods or similar games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in any treatment. The findings are relevant for understanding the effects of assortative matching and exogenous pro-social agents on cooperation in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The study does show that structural features (like matching rules and initial population composition) can have large effects on cooperation rates, which may be conceptually relevant for understanding moderators of cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

The study does not involve a public goods game or any punishment/sanction mechanism, nor does it analyze group-level efficiency or total payoff. - source: Ma, S. M., Zhang, B. Y., Cao, S. N., Liu, J. S., & Wang, W. X. (2021). Limited memory optimizes cooperation in social dilemma experiments. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that providing players with memory of their own and their partner's actions from previous rounds (memory length L) in a repeated, randomized-pair Prisoner's Dilemma leads to a non-monotonic effect on cooperation: cooperation rates are lowest with no memory (L=0), increase sharply with minimal memory (L=1), and peak at intermediate memory (L=2), then decline with longer memory (L=3,4). The behavioral mechanism is memory-based conditional cooperation, where most subjects cooperate if their partner's recent cooperation rate is high. Theoretical models (replicator dynamics and Markov processes) fit the observed cooperation rates well. No punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes are studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It is relevant for understanding how memory and information about past actions affect cooperation rates in repeated dyadic social dilemmas, but it does not address group payoff, efficiency, or the impact of punishment or reward mechanisms. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that memory length (information about past actions) can have a non-monotonic effect on cooperation rates, but provides no evidence about efficiency or about punishment effects. It is not directly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public-goods-like environments.

- source: Romano, A., Balliet, D., Yamagishi, T., & Liu, J. H. (2017). Parochial trust and cooperation across 17 societies. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that people cooperate more with ingroup than outgroup members in a trust game, but reputational concern (common knowledge of group membership) increases cooperation with both ingroup and outgroup members. Men show slightly more ingroup favoritism than women, but social value orientation predicts general cooperation rather than parochialism. There is little cross-societal variation in ingroup favoritism, and this variation is not explained by material security, religiosity, or pathogen stress. The study does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. It is relevant for understanding how reputational concerns (a form of indirect social incentive) can increase cooperation, but not for payoff-based or punishment-based interventions. Its design and findings are adjacent to the prediction task, offering only indirect context for how social information and group identity affect cooperation.

- source: Brodbeck, F. C., Kugler, K. G., Reif, J. A. M., & Maier, M. A. (2013). Morals Matter in Economic Games. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  findings: Across four lab experiments, participants induced to adopt a Unity moral motive (via framing or subliminal priming) allocated more money to another participant in a one-shot risk-sharing game than those induced to adopt a Proportionality motive. This effect was only present in interpersonal (DSG) and not solitary (SIG) settings. The study does not use a public goods game or any punishment/sanction mechanism, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The findings are about the psychological determinants of solidarity behavior (unconditional giving) in risk-sharing contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not manipulate or measure punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. However, it provides adjacent evidence that psychological framing (moral motives) can strongly affect other-regarding behavior in one-shot risk-sharing games. The design is not a PGG, and the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, so its direct value for the prediction task is limited.

  findings: The study finds that in a spatial continuous-strategy prisoner's dilemma with limited resources, lower total resource and intermediate temptation to defect can promote higher average cooperation (measured by the average characteristic parameter, CP). However, the model does not include punishment or sanctions, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The results are primarily about the evolution of cooperation and survival under resource constraints.

  findings: The main finding is that success-driven migration, when combined with imitation, enables the formation and persistence of cooperative clusters in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, even when noise (random strategy mutation and random relocation) is present. The results are robust across a range of payoff parameters and noise types. - source: Grujic, J., Eke, B., Cabrales, A., Cuesta, J. A., & Sánchez, A. (2012). Three is a crowd in iterated prisoner's dilemmas: experimental evidence on reciprocal behavior. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in iterated multiplayer Prisoner's Dilemma games, cooperation is sustained and even increases in two-player groups, but decays rapidly and remains low in groups of three or more. The main behavioral mechanism is 'moody conditional cooperation,' where players' likelihood to cooperate depends on both their own previous action and the number of cooperators in the previous round. There is substantial heterogeneity in initial generosity but less in reciprocity. The presence of more than two players leads to a sharp drop in sustained cooperation, suggesting that reciprocity alone is insufficient to maintain cooperation in larger groups without additional mechanisms such as punishment. No direct measures of efficiency or group payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms, increasing group size from 2 to 3 or more sharply reduces sustained cooperation in iterated social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, it suggests that, without punishment, efficiency (if measured) would be high in two-player games and low in groups of three or more. However, because the study does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes, it cannot directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It does, however, highlight the importance of group size as a moderator of cooperation and suggests that additional mechanisms (like punishment) may be necessary to sustain cooperation and efficiency in larger groups.

The study uses custom BT games and standard two-player Prisoner's Dilemma games, but does not include public goods games, punishment, or analyze efficiency or group payoff.

It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings about benevolence and cooperation may be contextually relevant for understanding individual factors that promote cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games.
